This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public 
website in that context, which does not imply a presumed outcome. Decisions taken in relation to this document and updates to it, if any,

Meeting of the Board 
30 June – 3 July 2025 
Port Moresby, Papua New Guinea 
Provisional agenda item 11 
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
9 June 2025 
Consideration of funding proposals – 
Addendum XVII 
Funding proposal package for FP275 
Summary  
This addendum contains the following six parts: 
a) A funding proposal summary titled “Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated 
Utility Services (IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean 
(Phase 1) Programme” by Caribbean Development Bank; 
b) No-objection letter(s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal 
point(s);  
c) Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure; 
d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment;  
e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's 
assessment; and 
f) Gender documentation of the funding proposal.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template 
• 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to 
annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in 
the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all 
relevant documents.  
• 
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. 
Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard 
time. 
• 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• 
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be 
disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the 
redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are 
asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

A.20. Executing Entity 
information 
• 
Caribbean Development Bank (via the CDB TA Execution Team) 
• 
Barbados Light and Power Limited is a regulated electric utility in Barbados. It is a 
limited liability company incorporated under the Laws of Barbados, and is a wholly 
(100%) owned subsidiary of Emera Inc., a Canadian utilities conglomerate. 
• 
Belize Electricity Limited is a regulated electricity utility in Belize. It is a limited 
liability company incorporated under the laws of Belize, and is majority owned by 
the Belize public sector. The Government of Belize owns 32.58%, while the Social 
Security Board owns 31.27%. The remaining shares are owned by Fortis Cayman 
Inc. (33.3%) and 1,500 small shareholders (2.85%). 
• 
Jamaica Public Services Company Limited is a regulated electricity utility in 
Jamaica. It is a limited liability company incorporated under the laws of Jamaica, 
and is majority owned by a consortium comprising the Marubeni Corporation from 
Japana and the East-West Power Corporation from South Korea (combined 80%). 
The Government of Jamaica owns 19% of the shares while a small group of 
minority shareholders control the remaining 1%.

=== PAGE 9 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 3 
 
 
The Caribbean region is currently facing the adaptation & mitigation challenges of climate change, evidenced by 
intensified storms and rising temperatures, and an energy sector heavily reliant on imported fossil fuels, contributing 
significantly to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. 
 
Climate: The Borrowing Member Countries (collectively “Barbados, Belize and Jamaica” or the “BMCs”) are facing 
significant climate change impacts, including intensified storms, floods, droughts, sea-level rise, and rising 
temperatures. These changes are affecting not only the availability and supply of energy resources but also the 
transmission, distribution, operations, and energy demand. The historical and projected impacts of climate change 
indicate a trend of rising temperatures, escalating the risk of heat stress and severe weather events, which are expected 
to continue and intensify. For instance, the International Energy Agency has noted that extreme heat boosts the demand 
for cooling, with sustained average daily temperatures of 30°C typically resulting in a 16% spike in weekly sales of 
electricity.3 Moreover, the BMCs are committed to ambitious goals to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by increasing 
the share of renewables in their energy mix and improving energy efficiency across all sectors. 
 
Energy: The BMCs heavily rely on imported fossil fuels for electricity, which constitutes a significant portion of their 
annual greenhouse gas emissions. The energy sectors in these countries are critical to their GHG profiles, with other 
sectors such as road transport, industry, agriculture, and land-use change also being notable contributors. The 
Integrated Utility Services (IUS) Programme intends to drive investment in renewable energy and energy efficiency 
measures to reduce this dependency, which is increasingly important as the demand for electricity is expected to rise 
with economic growth. The integration of Distributed Energy Resources (DERs) is also a focus for enhancing the 
resilience and diversification of the electricity grid.  
 
The introduction of an IUS model in BMCs is seen as a strategic step toward addressing various challenges in their 
energy sectors. These include reducing GHG emissions, decentralizing energy systems, enhancing resilience to 
climate change impacts, and decreasing dependence on imported fossil fuels. Additionally, the model supports 
adaptation to heat through providing cooling solutions to ensure comfortable indoor temperatures, using energy-efficient 
air conditioners and decentralized renewable energy supply to reduce peak electricity demand during times of high 
temperatures, thereby supporting the reliability of the grid. The Programme aims to democratize energy policy, increase 
access to clean energy, reform utility business models, and enhance capabilities for financing DERs. Furthermore, it 
seeks to foster regional learning and knowledge sharing while developing a sustainable market ecosystem for behind-
the-meter solutions in the Caribbean.   
Programme Approach 
The Governments of Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica aim to accelerate decarbonization efforts in their electricity sector 
and increase the resilience of the communities to climate change impacts. They aim to meet international climate 
change commitments by focusing on renewable energy (RE) and energy efficiency (EE) technologies. The proposed 
IUS Programme will be administered by electric utilities to support the deployment of these technologies. The 
Programme will involve education for customers, financing for installations, and repayment through monthly bills, 
overcoming the barrier of high upfront costs. This Programme constitutes the first of two phases for the overall IUS 
Programme in the region. While Phase I would provide support to the three countries to implement the Programme, 
Phase II will result in a broader implementation and scale-up to other member-countries of the Caribbean Development 
Bank (CDB). 
This IUS model is a mechanism to directly implement demand-side RE and EE projects through utilities in an open 
market system.  
The IUS Programme will include a “national project” in each country to be executed by the national electric utility and 
overseen by the Ministry and regulator responsible for oversight of the electricity sector. Each “national project” will 
include a series of sub-projects at the level of the utility’s customers, notably residential customers (i.e. households) 
and commercial customers (i.e. local businesses). Through each of these small-scale sub-projects, eligible RE, EE 
and/or other DER technologies will be installed at the premises of the interested utility customers.  Utility customers will 
first receive education and awareness about the options available to them and then they will choose to implement the 
technologies through registered qualified service providers that work closely with the utility. The utility will provide the 
upfront financing for the installation of the sub-projects and support the service providers, while the customers will then 
repay the amount owed to the utility for the installation through their monthly bill. A major barrier for the uptake of these 
technologies has historically been the high upfront cost to install them, so this Programme is expected to alleviate that 
pressure.

=== PAGE 10 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4 
 
 
Programme Components: 
The IUS Programme is structured in a way that it not only addresses all the key risks and barriers assessed during the 
programme preparation phase but also supports the much-needed ecosystem development for a sustainable, scalable, 
and replicable growth of behind the meter energy solutions in the BMCs. The below figure outlines the key components 
of the IUS Programme. 
 
 
Source: CDB/CPCS 
The Programme, through Component 1, establishes and promotes IUS as a core element of each Executing Entity’s 
business model. It underpins timely and effective delivery of country-level support and enables the BMCs to guide and 
support one another during and after the Programme. Component 2 aims to build on this foundation to scale up 
investments in distributed RE, EE and other DERs through widespread use of the IUS model by the Executing Entities 
in the BMCs. 
Each programme component is broken down into sub-components, which themselves comprise several activities that 
support a robust programme design and delivery. 
Programme Benefits: 
The IUS Programme anticipates significant climate-related benefits: 
 
• 
Reduction in GHG Emissions: The Programme is projected to make a significant reduction in the carbon 
footprint of the participating countries by enabling the avoidance of an estimated 601,550 tons of CO2 
equivalent (tCO2e) over the lifetime of the Programme. This results in an estimated cost of USD 70 per tCO2e 
avoided,4 and for the GCF's contribution, the cost is approximately USD 43 per tCO2e avoided.5  
 
• 
Increasing Resilience Against Heat Stress: The Programme is projected to provide energy efficient air 
conditioners (AC) to about 9,444 residential and 1,211 commercial beneficiaries. 
 
• 
Decreased Burden on Utilities and Increased Renewable Energy Generation: Energy efficiency 
interventions are expected to reduce energy consumption across various sectors, including residential, 
commercial, and public. The Programme anticipates considerable energy savings which translate into lower 
utility bills for consumers and decreased overall demand on energy infrastructure. A shift towards renewables 
is anticipated, with a targeted increase in the contribution of clean energy to the national grids. This includes 
 
3 IEA (2023). Keeping cool in a hotter world is using more energy, making efficiency more important than ever. 
4 Estimated cost per tCO2e avoided refers to the total cost of the GCF loan, GCF grant and CDB loan (USD41.95 mil), 
excluding parallel investments by the utilities, divided by the total GHG emissions avoided. 
5 Estimated GCF cost per tCO2e avoided refers to the total GCF cost (USD25.76 mil) divided by the total GHG emissions 
avoided.

A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5 
 
 
the deployment of technologies like solar photovoltaic systems, solar water heating, and possibly wind and 
geothermal energy, depending on the country-specific strategies and natural resource availability. 
 
• 
Economic Development: The transition to a greener energy sector is intended to stimulate local economies 
through job creation in the renewables sector, foster innovation, and provide opportunities for local businesses. 
Energy cost savings and the potential for new energy-related businesses are additional economic benefits. 
 
• 
Capacity Building and Knowledge Sharing: A core outcome of the Programme is the enhancement of 
institutional capacities and regulatory frameworks in each participating country. This involves training programs, 
development of standards, and the establishment of best practices for EE and RE deployment. 
 
• 
Scaling Up of Investments: By de-risking investments and establishing favourable conditions for private and 
international financing, the Programme aims to catalyse additional resources for the energy sector 
transformation. This will enable a scaling up of clean energy projects and attract further investments into the 
region. 
 
• 
Gender and Social Inclusion: The Programme places an emphasis on ensuring that the benefits of the energy 
transition are equitably distributed, with specific measures to include women and vulnerable communities in 
the opportunities created by the shift to sustainable energy sources. The Programme aims to provide a more 
inclusive way to support sustainable energy investments, particularly for women who often face more economic 
barriers than men due to traditional divisions of labour and social norms. Through the Gender Action Plan 
(GAP), the Programme will address the following gender-specific needs: governance and participation (through 
targeted actions focused on policies, utilities, and other key actors), and access, education, and assets (through 
targeted actions focused on the marginalised beneficiaries).

B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
 
i. 
Climate change problem 
Mitigation needs 
B.1.1. Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica 
heavily rely on imported fossil fuels for 
electricity, contributing significantly to 
their GHG emissions: The energy 
sectors in the BMCs that are participating 
in Phase I are characterized by a 
continued 
dependence 
(to 
varying 
degrees) on imported fossil fuels. Despite 
recent efforts to reduce this dependency, 
fossil fuels remain an important element 
of each country’s electricity generation 
mix.  As a result, electricity generation is 
responsible for a significant proportion of 
total annual GHG emissions in Barbados, 
Belize, and Jamaica. Other sectors that contribute significantly to total annual GHG emissions in these 
BMCs include road transport, industry, agriculture and land-use change & forestry. 
B.1.2. The Governments of Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica have each outlined clear strategies for addressing 
climate change and reducing emissions, initiatives that will be supported by the IUS programme. While 
Belize acts as a net carbon sink and the emissions from Barbados and Jamaica are comparatively lower 
than those of many high-emitting countries worldwide, all three nations have committed to ambitious goals 
to contribute to global carbon emission reduction targets. The following section provides a summary of the 
mitigation needs of these BMCs as derived from the Baseline Assessment. For detailed information, please 
refer to Annex 2b. 
Mitigation rationale

B.1.5. Associated current and projected emissions from the sector without the program: The Second 
National Communication from Barbados submitted to the UNFCCC in 2018 reported total emissions of 1.4 
Mt CO2e in 2010 for the energy sector. While the NDC included an economy-wide Business as Usual 
(BAU) emission projection, it did not disaggregate by sector. The economy-wide BAU emission projections 
for 2030 is 1.9 Mt CO2e, while the emissions for the NDC base year (2008) is 2.1Mt CO2e. Hence, the 
projected economy-wide emission reductions by 2030 is 165 kt CO2e 6, which is an average annual 
decrease in emissions by 7.5 kt CO2e since 2009.7 This is equivalent to a 0.35% annual decrease in 
emissions from the 2008 base year emission values, based on the assumption that the emissions reduction 
is uniform across the years. In order to project energy emissions, the 0.35% annual emission reduction 
was then applied to the 2010 emissions for the energy sector (1.4 Mt CO2e). Based on this, the projected 
emissions from Barbados’s energy sector in 2030 is estimated at 1.3 Mt CO2e.

B.1.8. Associated current and projected emissions from the sector without the program 
The emissions attributable to Belize’s electricity sector in 2020 were estimated at 170 kt CO2e, excluding 
emissions associated with imported electricity whose value for the same year was estimated at 106 kt CO2e9. 
Hence, the 2020 total emissions from the electricity sector including imported and domestic production were 
276 kt CO2e. The Low Emission Development Strategy (LEDS) of Belize assumes an annual BAU energy 
consumption growth rate of 3%. Based on this, the BAU emissions for the electricity sector (domestic plus 
imports) in 2030 is projected to be 371 kt CO2e.

B.1.11. Associated current and projected emissions from the sector without the program 
The emissions from the energy sector of Jamaica in 2012 was reported to be 7.4 Mt CO2e, out of which 2.8 
Mt CO2e was attributed to heat and electricity production10. As per Jamaica’s revised NDCs, BAU emissions 
in the energy sector are projected to reach 8.2 Mt CO2e by 2030. 
B.1.12. The IUS Programme will support the BMCs to achieve this vision through the provision of concessional 
financing which will drive investment in RE and EE measures. The attributable emissions from the energy 
sectors11 for each of the three countries are shown in Figure 2. 
B.1.13. To deliver on their emission reduction targets, it is of paramount importance that BMCs scale 
up the use of RE and EE measures. The need for such investments is reinforced by the fact that electricity 
demand in these countries is expected to increase over the coming decades as their economies grow. 
Consequently, the countries are working to reduce the dependence of their energy systems and energy-
intensive sectors (e.g. tourism, agriculture, industry) on fossil fuels. Opportunities for doing so are 
increasing and seen as critical to the attainment of climate change and sustainable development goals but 
may simultaneously further increase overall demand for electricity. 
Adaptation needs 
B.1.14. The Caribbean region, including Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica, is already facing, and will 
continue to face, more intense storms, floods, droughts, rising sea levels, and higher 
temperatures.12 The rise in temperature in the Caribbean is expected to gradually increase across all 
seasons consistent with global warming, through to the end of the century.13.  All BMCs broke temperature 
records in 2023, experiencing the hottest days in the last 20 years. More recently, in April 2024, Belize 
recorded the highest temperature observed in any month for the second consecutive year. These extreme 
temperatures can lead to heat stress, negatively impacting human health and well-being. Active cooling 
solutions, such as energy-efficient air conditioning, can help alleviate these issues. Additionally, pairing PV 
generation with AC demand can flatten the consumption curve during peak hours. The IUS Programme 
will support beneficiaries in accessing these adaptation measures, increasing resilience against heat 
stress. 
 
Table 4: Historical and projected climate change impacts for the BMCs 
Relevant changes in climatic conditions 
Barbados 
Historical:  Since the 1960s, Barbados has experienced a notable increase in air temperature by 
approximately 0.14°C per decade, largely influenced by the El Niño Southern Oscillation, which significantly 
impacts the island's climate by dictating weather patterns and intensity. Additionally, located within the 
hurricane belt, Barbados is prone to natural hazards like hurricanes, floods, droughts, and landslides, 
especially during the hurricane season from June to November, with a peak in activity from September to 
November, as evidenced by severe damage from hurricanes such as Elsa in 2021 and Tomas in 2010. 
Projected:    Modelling conducted under the CMIP6 framework, featured in the World Bank Climate Change 
Knowledge Portal (CCKP), projects a significant rise in the mean annual temperatures of Barbados under a 
high emissions scenario (SSP3-7.0). By the end of the century, temperatures are expected to reach 
29.07°C, up from the 1995 baseline of 26.11°C (see Figure 7). Additionally, maximum temperatures are 
projected to increase by 3.5% to 13% by the 2100s across scenarios, relative to the same baseline. This 
data suggests a general rise in maximum temperatures throughout the year, peaking slightly from August 
to October. Minimum temperatures are expected to see a greater increase than maximum temperatures,

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 11 
 
 
 
 
14 Caribbean Development Bank (2020). The state of the Caribbean Climate. 
15 GFDRR (2020). ThinkHazard. Barbados. 
with projections showing a 4% to 18% rise across different scenarios compared to the baseline temperature 
of 25.43°C in 1995. These projections are consistent with data from more detailed, downscaled modelling 
efforts. 14 Despite these projections, the current risk associated with extreme heat is considered low, 
indicating a 5% to 25% chance of experiencing at least one instance of prolonged extreme heat exposure, 
potentially leading to heat stress, in the next five years.15 
Barbados is projected to start to face heat stress from the 2040s onwards, as well as an increase in humidity 
during the summer season is anticipated. Although there is a lack of detailed data on the adoption of cooling 
technologies in Barbados, the tourism sector, a significant part of the country's economy, has highlighted in 
its National Cooling Strategy the rising demand for air conditioning. 
 
 
Source: World Bank. Climate Change Knowledge Portal. Information retrieved on February 2024. 
Belize 
Historical:  According to the World Bank CCKP, Belize has experienced a significant warming trend, with 
mean annual temperatures rising by 0.45°C since 1960, at an average rate of 0.10°C per decade. From 
1960 to 2022, surface temperatures increased by 0.81°C. This period also saw a marked change in 
temperature patterns, with hot days and nights increasing by 67 days per year from 1960 to 2003, while cold 
days and nights decreased by 21 days per year.  
Projected:  Modelling conducted under the CMIP6 framework, featured in the World Bank CCKP, projects 
a significant rise in the mean annual temperatures of Belize under a high emissions scenario (SSP3-7.0).  
Temperatures are anticipated to increase by 1.05°C (0.88°C-1.33°C) by 2030 and are projected to reach 
29.45°C (28.07°C-30.68°C) by the end of the century, from a baseline of 25.4°C (24.95°C-25.73°C) in 1995 
(see Figure 9).  Days with temperatures above 30°C are projected to increase by 74-175% across scenarios 
by the 2100s from 1995 baseline of 122 days (90 days-149 days). Days with temperatures above 35°C are 
projected to increase an average of 6 days per decade by 2100s (SSP3-7.0), with higher-than-average 
increase in days from April to September. Nights with temperatures above 26°C are projected to increase 
by 307-430% across scenarios from 1995 baseline of 7 days (3 days-13 days) by the end of the century.  
Beginning in the 2030s, Belize is anticipated to face a significant escalation in heat stress risks, even under 
the more optimistic SSP2-4.5 scenario (see Figure 10).

16 GFDRR (2020). ThinkHazard. Jamaica. 
Heat stress is likely to constitute a major challenge for Belize and other Caribbean countries as the climate 
changes over the coming decades. These impacts are expected to be particularly pronounced in the summer 
months. Indeed, although humidity levels in Belize tend to be consistently high throughout the year (often 
between 75-85%), they are typically highest during the rainy/wet season that occurs during the summer. 
These are the same months during which temperatures and temperature extremes (hot days, hot nights) 
are projected to increase the most over the coming decades, this highlights the need for Belize to increase 
uptake of/access to cooling solutions. In 2022, penetration of cooling technologies in Belize was 67%. 
 
 
Source: World Bank. Climate Change Knowledge Portal. Information retrieved on February 2024. 
Jamaica 
Historical:  As outlined in the World Bank CCKP maximum, average, and minimum temperatures are all 
exhibiting an increasing linear trend. Since 1960, average surface temperatures have increase by an 
average of 0.11°C per decade, maximum surface temperatures have increased by 0.08°C and minimum 
temperatures have increased by 1.04°C.   
 
Projected:  Modelling undertaken in the context of CMIP6, as featured in the World Bank CCKP, projects 
that the mean annual temperatures in Jamaica will increase moderately from 25.58°C (25.35°C - 25.8°C) in 
1995 under the most optimistic scenario (SSP1-2.6) reaching 26.52°C (26.12°C - 26.89°C) by the end of 
the century. However, this scenario is highly unlikely given the current global GHG emissions trajectories.  
Comparatively, under an intermediate scenario (SSP3-7.0), temperature is anticipated to increase by 0.88°C 
(0.6°C-1.11°C) by 2030 and is projected to reach 28.77°C (28.01°C-29.64°C) by the end of the century. 
Additionally, the risk of extreme heat is classified as medium, indicating that there is more than a 25% 
chance of experiencing at least one period of prolonged exposure to extreme heat, leading to heat stress, 
within the next five years.16  
The effects of heat stress have become increasingly evident in Jamaica. Schools, for instance, have 
reported that extreme heat has negatively impacted students' learning and well-being due to inadequate 
ventilation and cooling systems.  In response, the Jamaican government has initiated pilot programs to 
install air-conditioning units in 30 schools.  Additionally, the Jamaica Public Service Company has noted a 
significant increase in electricity demand, attributed to the rising use of cooling technologies.  Furthermore, 
the Government of Jamaica has issued warnings to the population about the dangers of heat exposure, 
including heat-related illnesses such as heat exhaustion, heat rash, heat cramps, and most severely, heat 
stroke, which can be fatal.

The programme is expected to increase the adaptation potential of all three countries by building resilience for 
customers and the grid. The region is expected to experience more frequent and severe storms, 
including flooding, storm surges, and other severe weather events that have the high potential to 
damage power sector infrastructure. Customers who are part of the program to install distributed 
energy resources such as solar rooftops with energy storage will be far more resilient to power 
disruptions from severe weather events. In addition, DER will support grid resilience by alleviating 
pressure on the grid during increased demand for active cooling solutions like air conditioning due to 
high temperatures through localized energy generation and storage.

B.1.15. The IUS Programme aims to facilitate the adoption of renewable energy and energy-efficient 
technologies, supporting countries to address the above-described needs/challenges by: (i) 
reducing greenhouse gas emissions and accelerating the transition away from reliance on fossil 
fuels; and (ii) increasing resilience against the mounting risk of heat stress in the Caribbean. 
Additionally, the integration of Distributed Energy Resources (DERs) into the grid will enhance its resilience 
by diversifying the sources of electricity generation and peak shaving the demand. This diversification 
means that the grid will be powered by electricity generated from various energy resources and generation 
facilities. 
 
ii. 
Baseline and context 
B.1.16. Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica, as Small Island Developing States (SIDS) in the Caribbean, face 
common challenges inherent to their geographical and socio-economic contexts. These include 
vulnerability to natural disasters, reliance on tourism and agriculture, and the impacts of climate change, 
such as increasing temperatures, sea-level rise and extreme weather events. Despite varying geographic 
characteristics—with Belize on the mainland and Barbados and Jamaica as islands—all three countries 
strive for sustainable development, environmental conservation, and climate resilience. They also share 
developmental challenges such as resource management, food security, and economic diversification, 
underscored by a collective commitment to overcoming these obstacles through regional cooperation and 
policy initiatives. 
 
B.1.17. A summary of relevant baseline conditions and characteristics in the three participating BMCs 
is included below. More information is available in the Baseline Assessment Report – see Annex 2b. 
Table 6: Context/baseline conditions in the three participating BMCs 
Context of targeted BMCs 
Barbados 
Barbados, located in the eastern Caribbean, is a 34 km long and 23 km wide 21 island nation 
characterized by its limestone composition, which contributes to its renowned white sand beaches 
and clear waters. With a diverse population of approximately 290,000,22 mainly of African descent,23 
the nation boasts a high-income economy, heavily reliant on tourism, finance, and international 
business. 24  Despite its economic success, Barbados encounters environmental challenges like 
climate change, yet maintains a commitment to sustainable practices. The geography, featuring a relatively flat terrain 
rising gently to the central highland region, allows for a concentrated population along the coasts.25

Electricity sector:  The Barbados Light & Power Company (BLPC) holds a monopoly on electricity 
generation, transmission, and distribution. Since 2018, Independent Power Producers (IPPs) have been 
allowed, enhancing the solar energy contribution.26 As of January 2023, distributed generation systems 
had a capacity of approximately 80 MW, with about 500 MW of renewable projects under 
development.27  As of 2020, BLPC served 131,522 customers, with a peak demand of 141 MW and an installed capacity 
of about 276 MW, predominantly oil-fired.28 Renewable energy contributed 7.20% of total generation in 2021. Efforts to 
expand renewable generation include the addition of solar PV farms and energy storage devices to improve grid stability. 
The energy sector aims for 100% renewable sources by 2030, with distributed generation expected to play a key role. 
The Renewable Energy Rider (RER) and Feed-in Tariff (FIT) programs have been instrumental in increasing solar PV 
and wind contributions.29 Energy efficiency measures and the diversification of the energy mix are ongoing to reduce 
emissions and enhance sustainability. 
Belize 
Belize, located on the Caribbean coast of Central America, is distinguished by its diverse ecosystems, 
including the Belize Barrier Reef, the second largest in the world. With a land area of 22,960 km² and 
a population of approximately 421,754,30 it boasts a multicultural society with a mix of Indigenous, 
Mestizo, Creole, and Afro-Amerindian heritage. 31 Belize's economy is classified as upper-middle-
income by the World Bank, 32 despite facing challenges such as poverty and unemployment. Its 
economy is supported by agriculture, tourism, and services, with significant contributions from the 
barrier reef through tourism and fishing.33 The country is characterized by its tropical climate, experiencing significant 
variability in rainfall and temperatures, which, along with its location, makes it susceptible to climate change and natural 
disasters like hurricanes. Belize's commitment to protecting its natural heritage is evident in its extensive network of 
protected areas, encompassing marine reserves, national parks, and nature reserves. Despite its beauty and 
biodiversity, Belize confronts issues related to food security and safety, with high rates of violent crime in certain areas.34 
Electricity sector:  Belize Electricity Limited (BEL), regulated by the Public Utilities Commission, is 
Belize's sole electricity provider, selling approximately 561 GWh in 2021 with a peak demand of 104 
MW. While Belize has made progress relating to use of alternative energy sources such as biomass 
and hydropower, petroleum-based fuels still represent the main sources of energy. Electricity 
generation witnessed slight increases in the use of petroleum fuels combined with the continuous use 
of biomass, hydropower and electricity importation from Mexico. Despite low solar PV contribution, efforts are being 
made to increase distributed generation, with regulatory frameworks for Distributed Generation (DG) under 
development. BEL, connecting over 170,000 customers across all major municipalities, is expanding its service range 
to include DG, allowing customers to sell excess energy back to the grid. Electricity consumption patterns show 
commercial and residential sectors as primary consumers, with street lighting demand expected to decrease due to an 
ongoing LED replacement program. Belize aims for a 75% renewable energy target by 2030, encouraging solar PV 
adoption despite the lack of a formal interconnection policy.35 Projections suggest an increase in electricity demand, 
with BEL planning to enhance grid electrification, particularly in rural areas, and reduce emissions through increased 
renewable energy use and energy efficiency measures. Electricity rates in Belize are relatively low, with tiered pricing 
to support various customer classes.36 
Jamaica 
Jamaica, located in the West Indies, is the third largest island in the Caribbean, positioned south of 
Cuba and west of Haiti. With a multi-racial population of over 2.9 million,37 Jamaica's demographic 
fabric includes African, European, East Indian, and Chinese ancestries. 38 The island's population 
density stands at 272 persons per square kilometre, reflecting a diverse yet densely settled nation. The 
economy of Jamaica, as of the third quarter of 2022, showcased a 5.6 percent year-on-year

=== PAGE 22 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 16 
 
 
 
 
39 Trading Economics (2023). Jamaica GDP Annual Growth Rate.  
40 Britannica (2023). Economy of Jamaica.  
41 Government of Jamaica (2018).  
42 Ministry of Science Energy and Technology (2016). 
expansion,39 evidencing recovery from the COVID-19 pandemic's impacts with contributions from agriculture, forestry, 
fishing, and manufacturing. 40 Despite the economy's gradual recovery, Jamaica grapples with challenges such as 
income inequality and a high vulnerability to climate change, with significant risks including sea level rise and changing 
rainfall patterns impacting its agriculture-dependent economy. Jamaica's natural beauty and cultural heritage, alongside 
its challenges, portray a complex tapestry of development, resilience, and ongoing efforts to mitigate environmental and 
economic vulnerabilities. 
 
Electricity sector:  The Jamaican electricity sector includes the Jamaica Public Services Company 
Limited (JPS) as the primary utility and various privately owned Independent Power Producers (IPPs), 
servicing 98% of the population. JPS, owned by Marubeni and East West Power, alongside the 
Government of Jamaica, holds a monopoly on transmission and distribution with a license until 2027. 
The sector has seen a renewable energy increase to almost 18% by 2022, with the national target set 
at 30% by 2030, potentially increasing to 50% as suggested by the Prime Minister. 41 Despite the shift towards 
renewables, fossil fuels dominate the energy mix, contributing to high CO2 emissions. Electricity demand is expected to 
grow, driven by industrial expansion and increased residential and commercial usage. Distributed generation initiatives 
like the Net Billing Programme aim to enhance renewable energy contributions.42 However, high installation costs and 
financing challenges limit broader access to renewable technologies. The government's efforts to integrate more 
renewables and improve energy efficiency are anticipated to shape future energy demand and supply, aiming for 
significant energy savings and reduced emissions by 2035. Electricity costs, among the highest globally due to reliance 
on expensive fuel sources, are subject to government interventions to alleviate impacts on residential customers. 
 
iii. 
BMCs readiness for programme deployment 
B.1.18. All three countries have expressed a strong interest in implementing IUS to enhance their 
investment in distributed energy, EE, and related sectors. This commitment is evidenced by their 
proactive efforts towards institutionalising and launching IUS frameworks. By adopting IUS, these nations 
aim to scale up their energy initiatives, fostering sustainable development and reducing their carbon 
footprints. In addition, the BMCs are making positive institutional and regulatory improvements, motivated 
to support a strong and effective IUS rollout.  
B.1.19. Many participating BMCs have sought regulatory approval from their respective electricity regulators 
for the approval of an IUS Pilot Programme, supported by GIZ and Caricom, to advance the BMCs’ 
readiness for full-scale programme deployment. The programme design of the IUS Pilot Programme is 
similar to the programme design as elaborated under Section B3 of this funding proposal i.e., the utilities 
funding energy efficiency retrofits and renewable energy installations through an on-bill repayment 
mechanism. However, the regulatory approval in the BMCs is delayed primarily due to capacity constraints 
and the regulatory clarity required for a disruptive and innovative business model for the promotion of 
DERs. The following presents the current readiness status of each BMC studied in terms of their readiness 
to support and implement the IUS programme, specifically in terms of the legal, regulatory and institutional 
frameworks.  Detailed information about the readiness of BMCs can be found in Annex 2, Feasibility Study. 
The TA planned under Component 1 of the IUS Programme is designed to accelerate country readiness 
by providing adequate capacity support to the key stakeholders in the first year of Programme 
Implementation (“Programme Year 1” or “PY1”). 
B.1.20. Barbados:  Our assessment of the level of IUS readiness in Barbados in terms of the legal, regulatory 
and institutional frameworks is outlined below.

B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus 
diagram) 
Climate problem  
B.2a.1 The Caribbean region is currently grappling with two significant challenges: climate change, 
characterized rising temperatures, and an energy sector heavily dependent on imported fossil fuels, which 
substantially contribute to greenhouse gas emissions. Reflecting regional and global trends, the BMCs are 
experiencing severe climate change impacts, including intensified storms, floods, droughts, sea-level rise, 
and increasing temperatures. These changes are affecting not only the availability and supply of energy 
resources but also the transmission, distribution, operations, and energy demand. 
 
B.2a.2 Historical and projected climate change impacts show a trend of rising temperatures, increasing the 
risk of heat stress and severe weather events, which are expected to continue and intensify. For example, 
the International Energy Agency has noted that extreme heat increases the demand for cooling, with 
sustained average daily temperatures of 30°C typically leading to a 16% spike in weekly sales. Additionally, 
the BMCs are committed to ambitious goals to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by increasing the share of 
renewables in their energy mix and improving energy efficiency across all sectors. 
Goal  
B.2b.3 The IUS Programme aims to address the climate problem through a paradigm shift impact in the BMCs 
energy sector. This paradigm shift can be achieved IF the project establishes and operationalizes an innovative 
Integrated Utility Services (IUS) model by facilitating access to concessional financing, capacity building and 
regulatory reforms, THEN a paradigm shift will occur, leading to an accelerated transition to decentralized clean 
energy, increased energy security, and climate resilience across Barbados, Belize and Jamaica, BECAUSE a 
conducive and inclusive ecosystem for DER utilization will have been established that will lower GHG 
emissions, improve energy security and enhance climate resilience. 
 
B.2a.4 The proposed IUS Programme aims to reduce GHG emissions from the energy sector and 
enhance resilience to heat stress (cooling systems coupled with PV generation) in the three 
participating countries by scaling up investment in (and adoption/use of) distributed RE, EE and other 
DER technologies/measures. The Programme will accomplish this by addressing key barriers currently 
inhibiting businesses, homeowners, and other stakeholders from making such investments, including limited

=== PAGE 28 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22 
 
 
 
 
44 Detailed programme design is available B.3. 
awareness, knowledge, and capacity; limited access to financing; limited risk appetite; limited availability of 
qualified ESCOs; and policy and regulatory frameworks that do not sufficiently incentivize investment in DERs.  
B.2a.5. To overcome these barriers to investment, the Programme will support the electric utilities in Barbados, 
Belize, and Jamaica to establish and operate the IUS Model44 as part of their regular business operations.  To 
accomplish this, the Programme will also address key barriers currently preventing utilities from implementing 
this model at scale, including internal capacity constraints; limited access to the concessional financing needed 
for the ‘launch’ phase of the IUS scheme; the need for complementary technical assistance (TA) to successfully 
begin operating such a scheme at scale; and other policy and regulatory barriers. In so doing, the programme 
will directly assist the three national electric utilities to support behind-the-meter investment in distributed RE, 
EE and other DERs among a critical mass of customers (mostly households and businesses, as well as some 
public sector entities), while also equipping the utilities and other stakeholders to continue scaling up 
investment using the IUS Model after Programme closure.  
Paradigm shift 
B.2a.6. The Programme aims to be a long-term sustainable, scalable, and replicable solution to address the 
BMCs, and, by extension the region, climate challenges. The Programme is envisaged to achieve the shift 
impact, as elaborated in the programme’s Goal Statement, by: 
(a) ensuring a robust market ecosystem to accelerate deployment of renewable energy technologies as well 
as energy efficiency solutions; 
(b) strengthening stakeholders’ institutional capacity to advance clean energy in the participating countries and 
help achieve climate targets; 
(c) strengthening the technical capacity of Technology Service Providers (TSPs) to cater to the needs of the 
market; 
(d) providing equal opportunity for women and other social groups (such as low-income households, women 
led households, etc.) to adopt clean energy solutions; and 
(e) providing simplified access to more affordable finance; etc. 
 
B.2a.7. The Theory of Change figure below elaborates the broader goals of the IUS programme.

Figure 12: IUS Programme Theory of Change

Outcomes  
B.2a.8.  To achieve the over-arching goal the Programme aims to deliver the following two main Outcomes 
and a corresponding intermediate Outcome: 
Outcome 1: Reduced GHG emissions through widespread adoption of RE, EE and other DER technologies.  
The Programme is expected to increase reliance on local energy resources to meet its energy needs. Since 
the local energy resources are primarily clean sources of energy, in the long-term the Programme is expected 
to continue to mitigate emissions from the energy sector. 
Outcome 2: Enhanced climate resilience and adaptation of beneficiaries to heat stress. The Programme will 
support energy efficiency solutions such as efficient ACs. The programme therefore provides direct long term 
heat stress adaptation benefits to the end beneficiaries. 
Outcome 3 (intermediate): Increased access to financing for clean energy investments, allowing utility 
customers to adopt RE, EE and other DER technologies at scale. 
 
 
Outputs 
B.2a.10: To achieve the two main Outcomes and one intermediate Outcome described above, the Programme 
will aim to deliver four Outputs. 
1. Output 1.1: Strengthened regulatory and institutional frameworks facilitating the deployment of distributed 
RE, EE and other DER technologies. The Programme will support the partner countries and the key 
stakeholders (i.e., energy ministry and the electricity regulator) to develop the necessary capacity and 
strengthen the regulatory policy environment in the country for smooth implementation and roll-out of the 
IUS programme. The programme will implement two Activities to support Output 1.1: – Activity 1.1.1: 
Immediately address regulatory barriers impeding the implementation of the IUS Model under Component 
2, and Activity 1.1.2: Address broader policy framework to strengthen prospects and incentives for 
investment in distributed RE, EE, and DERs – which are described in more detail in Section B.3 (below). 
2. Output 1.2: Enhanced capacity of customers to invest in distributed RE, EE and other DERs through the 
IUS Model. The Programme will also sensitize the customers in the participating countries to support the 
longer-term societal shift toward activities and investments that contribute to countries’ climate change 
goals and resilience. The Programme will implement three Activities to support Output 1.2: – Activity 1.2.1: 
Raise awareness of RE/EE opportunities and potential savings, Activity 1.2.2: Promote an inclusive and 
just IUS Model and associated services, and Activity 1.2.3: Provide customers with hands-on support to 
identify and develop distributed RE, EE, and DER sub-projects – which are described in more detail in 
Section B.3 (below). 
3. Output 1.3: Increased technical capacity of utilities, TSPs and other stakeholders to deploy and maintain 
distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies. The Programme will enhance regional capacity to promote 
and implement the IUS model. The Programme aims to achieve this through collaborative learning and 
knowledge exchange among participating countries, developing programme guiding documents like 
tailored implementation manuals, developing additional capacity for business development and cross-
cutting areas such as Environmental and Social (E&S) management, Monitoring and Reporting (M&R), 
Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI), etc. The Programme will implement five Activities to support 
Output 1.3: Activity 1.3.1: Facilitate knowledge exchange among the targeted BMCs, Activity 1.3.2. 
Enhance Executing Entities’ and other stakeholder capacities to implement the IUS Model in a just transition 
context, Activity 1.3.3: Training for qualified (technology) service providers, notably TSPs, to support 
customers, Activity 1.3.4: Support information exchange with other (non-partner) countries including 
government, regulators, TSPs, and utilities, and Activity 1.3.5: Create a Community of Practice –  which 
are described in more detail in Section B.3 (below).

=== PAGE 31 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 25 
 
 
 
4. Output 2.1: Installation of distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies among a critical mass of utility 
customers, including households, businesses and public sector entities. The Programme will support the 
execution of a critical mass of distributed renewable energy and energy efficiency sub-projects at customer 
level that successfully generate or save energy and create cost savings for customers (described in more 
detail in Section B.3 below). There are many financial and non-financial barriers inhibiting the flow of 
financing to local private sector stakeholders for such investments, which the Programme aims to address 
through an accessible and concessional climate financing (Component 2) and complementary technical 
assistance and capacity-building support (Component 1). It is therefore important for the Programme to 
contribute to measurable progress in relation to the provision of sub-loans and adoption rates of eligible 
technologies and practices. 
 
Root causes and barriers 
B.2a.11: Based on a detailed review of available literature, as well as stakeholder consultations that were 
organised during the programme preparation period (between Q3 2022 and Q3 2023), numerous key factors 
were identified that are affecting and limiting the uptake of distributed renewable energy and energy efficiency 
solutions in the residential and small to medium commercial groups in the participating countries. Detailed 
barrier assessment and analysis is elaborated in Section B.3 Project/programme description and Annex 2: 
Feasibility Study. These factors are grouped into a more concise set of barriers, which are described below 
and reflected in the Theory of Change diagram above: 
Figure 13: Key barriers hindering the growth of behind the meter services 
Source: CPCS 
B.2a.12. Figure 16 summarises root causes hindering the growth of behind the meter services like distributed 
RE and EE in the participating BMCs, which include:

=== PAGE 32 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 26 
 
 
 
• 
Unclear legal and regulatory framework: BMCs need clearer legal or regulatory guidance on issues 
like DG tariffs/FIT and whether utilities can offer on-bill financing. There is also a lack of a standardized 
application/interconnection process. 
 
• 
Limited customer awareness and capacity: Customers often lack awareness of the benefits of the 
proposed technologies, affecting implementation smoothness. 
 
• 
Limited availability of TSPs providing end-to-end solutions: While there are TSPs with the 
necessary credentials, few can deliver comprehensive solutions. 
 
• 
Limited access to low-cost finance: Difficulty in securing financing due to being considered high-
risk, cumbersome processes for loan applications, and a lack of equitable financial products are 
significant barriers. 
 
• 
Risk adverse stakeholders: Both small electricity customers and financial institutions are hesitant to 
invest in behind-the-meter solutions, partly due to unclear risk assessments and high interest rates. 
 
• 
Limited technical standards: There is a need for globally accepted technical standards to ensure the 
standardization and quality of services. 
B.2a.13. In addition to the typical behind the meter barriers as detailed above, there are other areas specific to 
the BMCs that may impact IUS Programme roll-out. 
 
• 
Capacity constraints and model understanding: Regulatory authorities face capacity issues and 
apprehensions about approving novel business models, requiring additional stakeholder training. 
 
• 
Limited locally-available financing options: High local financing costs and limited access to low-
cost finance make it challenging for utilities to support the financial aspects of distributed RE, EE, and 
other DERs. 
• 
Limited distributed RE and EE ecosystem: A supportive ecosystem development is critical, 
requiring technical assistance for regulatory frameworks, TSP certification, customer engagement, and 
overcoming other ecosystem development barriers. 
 
B.2a.14 The activities of the IUS Programme, described in detail in Section B.3, have been designed to address 
the above barriers by targeting the root causes that have hindered the adoption of RE and EE technologies in 
the targeted BMCs. The following table illustrates how the IUS Programme activities will help overcome the 
identified barriers throughout the 6-year programme implementation period.

The IUS model eliminates the need for households and businesses to 
secure a loan from (often risk averse) financial institutions to invest in 
DERs. The model also minimizes the investment risk to be borne by the 
customer.  The IUS model shifts investment risks from customers as the 
customers can now avail clean energy technologies or services with little 
to no capital investment. Interested customers can avail low-interest 
loans through utilities and install pre-assessed technologies from reliable 
technology providers, making DERs more affordable and secure. This 
reduces the financial burden and exposure to potential losses for 
customers.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) 
i. 
Programme objectives 
B.3.1 The IUS Model is a mechanism to directly implement demand-side renewable energy and energy 
efficiency through utilities in an open market system. It is a model to support renewable energy and energy 
efficient technology deployment through a financing program that is administered by electric utilities. This 
programme would have the utilities launch and operate the IUS at a country-wide scale by supporting energy 
service companies, educating customers, and financing the installation of renewable energy and energy 
efficiency interventions. This programme funding proposal constitutes the first of two phases for the overall IUS 
Programme in the region; the current Phase I proposal will support three countries (Barbados, Belize, 
Jamaica) to implement IUS, while Phase II will result in a broader implementation and scale-up to other 
borrowing member countries in the Caribbean Region. Throughout the remainder of this funding 
proposal, “IUS Programme” is used to refer specifically to Phase I. 
B.3.2 The Programme aims to address the need for democratizing energy policy, increasing customers 
access to clean energy, reforming the business models of traditionally vertically integrated utilities, 
and enhancing utility capabilities for commercial financing of DERs. The IUS Programme is not only 
expected to foster an ecosystem conducive to sectoral sustainability but also facilitate regional learning and 
knowledge sharing, crucial for a unified approach to energy challenges in the Caribbean. The IUS Programme 
helps develop the much-needed market ecosystem for a sustained behind the meter solutions market in the 
Caribbean. The graphic below summarizes how the IUS Programme can help overcome current challenges in 
the transition towards more sustainable sources of energy.

=== PAGE 35 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 29 
 
 
 
Figure 14: Summary of IUS programme rationale 
 
Source: CPCS 
 
 
Source: CPCS

=== PAGE 36 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 30 
 
 
 
ii. 
Programme approach 
B.3.3 The IUS Programme will include a “national project” in each of the three countries to be executed 
by the national electric utility and overseen by the Ministry and regulator responsible for oversight of 
the electricity sector. Each project will include a series of sub-projects that supports various renewable 
energy, energy efficiency, and other DER technologies. Utility customers will first receive education and 
awareness about the options available to them and then they will choose to implement the technologies through 
registered qualified service providers that work closely with their electric utility. The utility will provide the upfront 
financing and contract qualified TSPs to procure and install priority technologies at the premises of interested 
customers/consumers, who will then repay the amount owed to the utility for the installation through their 
monthly electricity bill. A major barrier for the uptake of these technologies has historically been the high upfront 
cost to install them, so this Programme is expected to alleviate that pressure. 
B.3.4 The IUS Programme is an innovative solution that fosters collaboration. A typical behind-the-meter 
transaction involving business models like the IUS, involves government ministries, departments and agencies, 
utilities, system operators, independent power producers, technology service providers, customer and 
business associations, other clean energy facilitators and outreach providers, and lenders, among others. The 
following graphic provides an overview of the operational elements of the IUS Programme, including the key 
actors and components.  
Figure 15: IUS Programme Schematic 
Source: CPCS 
As indicated in the above graphic, the Caribbean IUS transaction, like other behind the meter transactions, 
involves several actors. These actors are public entities like the government ministries, electricity regulators, 
and private entities like service providers, customers, etc. The CDB-led IUS programme however is unique 
given the regional nature of the Programme and the involvement of GCF. This uniqueness results in the 
involvement of multiple critical actors, as listed below, to the transaction unlike a typical behind the meter 
transaction.
