Figure 16: Programme governance arrangements 
iii. 
Programme description 
B.3.5 The IUS Programme is designed in a way that it not only addresses all the key barriers that are 
otherwise inhibiting investment in the transition to a more sustainable and distributed energy system 
(as outlined in Section B.2 and further elaborated in Annex 2: Feasibility Study), but also supports the 
much-needed ecosystem development for sustainable, scalable, and replicable growth of behind the 
meter energy solutions in the BMCs. Figure 20 below outlines the key components of the IUS programme. 
Figure 17: Components of the IUS Programme 
 
Component 1: Establish and promote IUS as a core element of electric utilities’ business models 
1. The Programme, through Component 1, aims to establish and promote IUS as a core element of 
electric utilities’ business models in the BMCs to underpin timely and effective delivery of country-level 
support and enable the BMCs to guide and support one another during and after the Programme. The 
support provided under this Component will build directly on the considerable progress that has already 
been made in the three participating BMCs (as outlined above in Section B.1 and further elaborated in 
Annex 2: Feasibility Study). As a result, Component 1 will address the pending regulatory and 
institutional barriers that need to be overcome to begin financing IUS sub-projects at scale. Component 
2 will then build on this foundation to scale up investments in distributed RE, EE and other DERs

=== PAGE 38 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 32 
 
 
 
through widespread use of (and financing of sub-projects through) the IUS model by electric utilities in 
the three participating BMCs. 
2. This component lays down the groundwork for the electric utilities in the three participating BMCs to 
implement IUS at scale. This will ensure that all the key stakeholders have the necessary tools and 
frameworks to facilitate the proposed investments under Component 2 of the IUS programme. 
This component is sub-divided into three Outputs: 
Output 1.1: Strengthened regulatory and institutional frameworks facilitating the deployment of 
distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies. 
3. The Programme will support regulatory authorities in the participating countries to enhance and 
strengthen their regulatory framework through legal and regulatory amendments, update or develop 
new regulatory frameworks specifically for the distributed renewable energy and energy efficiency 
sector, update or develop a timebound sub-project approval and administrative process, increase 
access to global experience and learnings, provide regulatory authorities the opportunity to apply 
lessons learnt for sub-project appraisal, among others. The regulatory authority will also leverage 
expertise of an international regulatory expert as well as a national regulatory specialist to build and 
strengthen its capacity to cater to the distributed renewable energy sector for long-term sectoral 
sustainability. Furthermore, the Programme will also support the relevant energy ministry in the 
participating countries to strengthen its national policy of climate change and renewable energy and 
develop additional incentive mechanisms to increase clean energy adoption. 
4. The CDB Technical Assistance (TA) Execution Team (a regional TA Executing Entity that will operate 
within CDB in a manner that is separate from the Accredited Entity team) will support key stakeholders 
from the BMCs to address any pending legal and regulatory and/or capacity issues at the institutional 
level that may inhibit the IUS roll-out. These pending matters will be resolved based on the IUS Pilot 
Programme experience and lessons learnt from similar utility-led behind the meter programmes 
delivered in comparable global jurisdictions. It is also informed by the detailed assessments of the 
current policy, regulatory and institutional context in each of the three participating BMCs, which is 
briefly summarized in Section B.1 (above) and further elaborated in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. This 
Output will include two activities. 
5. Activity 1.1.1.: Immediately address regulatory barriers impeding the implementation of the IUS 
model under Component 2:  This TA activity aims to assist national stakeholders in each BMC to 
finalize updates to their legal and regulatory frameworks such that they are fit-for-purpose to support 
effective roll-out of IUS. In particular, this activity will facilitate the process of preparing and approving 
legal and regulatory amendments that are needed to operationalize and institutionalize IUS in each 
country, while also strengthening the capacity of the regulators in each country to oversee and regulate 
IUS activities. This will be done by contracting external consultants/specialists who will directly assist 
national counterparts to address these pending barriers. Additionally, this activity will provide external 
expert advice to help the relevant stakeholders in each participating BMC to strengthen at least one 
aspect of their broader regulatory framework, such as Renewable Energy Feed-In Tariff (RE/FIT) 
regulations, grid codes, or other relevant areas. 
Furthermore, this activity will provide support for updating or drafting key documents related to 
administrative procedures of the utility to receive, review, and approve sub-project application, energy 
accounting and settlement processes of the customers electricity bill availing benefits under the IUS 
Programme. These procedural documents are necessary to ensure a streamlined process for all the 
customers irrespective of their customer group, demography, etc. Standardised processes flow also 
helps increase efficiency and transparency. For instance, once the regulator or utility publishes the 
administrative procedures, a customer becomes fully aware about all application processing stages, 
the official responsible to review and approve the application at each stage, among others. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing

=== PAGE 39 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 33 
 
 
 
6. Activity 1.1.2 Address broader policy framework needs to strengthen prospects and incentives 
for investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs: The activity will assist national stakeholders 
(including the energy ministry, regulatory authority, and others as needed) in strengthening the national 
policy and incentive framework to support investment in priority technologies, including those to be 
supported/financed under the IUS programme. This will be done by contracting external 
consultants/specialists to directly assist the national counterparts with these reform processes. This 
support will align with NDCs and promote sector sustainability. Additionally, under this the activity, the 
CDB TA Execution Team will contract external consultants/specialists to develop TSP qualification 
guidelines to ensure quality installation of clean energy technologies, working in close collaboration 
with the utilities, energy ministries and regulatory authorities. This is needed to ensure that a minimum 
technical competence benchmark could be established in the BMCs to help reduce/eliminate sub-par 
sub-project installations. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
Output 1.2: Enhanced capacity of customers to invest in distributed RE, EE and other DERs through 
the IUS Model. 
7. One key barrier inhibiting investment in sustainable energy and energy efficiency solutions is the lack of 
customer awareness, and limited outreach regarding the energy and cost saving potential associated 
with these technologies. Under this sub-component, each participating utility, National Designated 
Authority (NDA), the energy ministry, and other key actors will engage in customer outreach and 
knowledge sharing activities. Through the CDB TA Execution Team, these stakeholders will receive 
direct support from external service providers to assist in planning, preparing and delivering these 
outreach efforts. Customer outreach will be delivered through various mechanisms, for instance 
newspaper advertisements, webinars, public events, etc. The modalities of customer outreach and 
building awareness will be BMC- and stakeholder-specific. These awareness and outreach initiatives 
will serve multiple purposes, including: (a) informing customers about the benefits of behind the meter 
solutions; (b) explaining the modalities of the IUS Programme and how it is delivered; (c) the amount 
of public support available for sub-project enrolment, etc. As part of these outreach efforts, a particular 
emphasis will be placed on underserved customer groups like women, etc.   The support provided 
under this Output will include developing clean energy technology and IUS Programme specific 
marketing material, developing inclusive marketing, communications and outreach strategies, 
conducting in-person outreach sessions for customer awareness, facilitating customers’ access to 
available TSPs, helping customers understand the benefits of the priority technologies (i.e., lowering 
their electricity bill), helping customers understand the administrative/approval process, strengthening 
utility’s capabilities to handle customer queries, facilitating free/low-cost energy audits for eligible 
customers, among others. This Output will include three activities: 
8. Activity 1.2.1 Raise awareness of RE/EE opportunities and potential savings:  Under this activity, 
the CDB TA Execution Team will contract external consultants/specialists to work with national 
counterparts (led by the energy ministry) in each of the three countries to develop and execute a 
marketing and outreach strategy to bolster demand for sustainable energy technologies. The 
consultants will first assist the national counterparts in each country to define and develop their 
marketing and outreach strategy. Once the strategies have been prepared, the consultants will also 
(guided by the priorities identified in the strategy) develop engaging, technology-specific content 
(including infographics, posters, pamphlets and other multimedia materials) that will showcase the 
benefits and functionalities of clean energy solutions. The national counterparts will then use these 
materials to deliver the outreach activities outlined in their respective strategies. This approach aims 
to reach a broad audience, including vulnerable groups, ensuring accessibility and inclusivity. 
Under this activity, the CDB TA Execution Team will also (through a suitable service provider) work 
with the national counterparts to organize six outreach sessions (1 per country in PY1 and 1 per country 
in PY3) to educate end beneficiaries—both residential and commercial consumers—on the 
advantages/benefits of clean energy solutions. These sessions are also expected to cover information 
about the IUS Programme, including the sub-project preparation and application process. The 
outreach sessions will therefore contribute to Activities 1.2.1 and 1.2.2, but the costs have been 
clustered under Activity 1.2.1 to facilitate smooth implementation. These efforts will be championed by 
the national counterparts (with support from local NGOs and industry/business associations), and aim

=== PAGE 40 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 34 
 
 
 
to both catalyse demand for sub-projects under Component 2 as well as foster long-term clean energy 
market development and enhance Programme sustainability. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
9. Activity 1.2.2 Promote an inclusive and just IUS model and associated services: Through this 
activity, the programme will aim to increase demand for IUS services both during and beyond the 
Programme implementation period, thereby reinforcing sustainability. The CDB TA Execution Team 
will contract a suitable service provider to assist national counterparts (led by the electric utilities) to 
develop a Programme-/IUS-focused communications action plan. Similar to Activity 1.2.1, the service 
provider will then also develop specific outreach materials to be used by the electric utilities when 
delivering their respective communications action plans. Both the plans and the associated outreach 
materials will be inclusive and cater specifically to vulnerable groups to ensure wide-reaching benefits. 
By making the Programme's information accessible to everyone, including those who are blind, deaf, 
or from marginalized groups, the strategy seeks to raise awareness, encourage participation, and drive 
the Programme's long-term success. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
10. Activity 1.2.3 Provide customers with hands-on support to identify and develop distributed RE, 
EE and other DER sub-projects: Under this activity, each electric utility (as a national Executing 
Entity) will receive resources with which to contract a National Energy Consultant who will assist the 
utilities with business development and customer outreach/support, as well as other programme-
related responsibilities (e.g. monitoring and reporting). In so doing, the National Energy Consultants 
are expected to contribute to both fostering a pipeline of suitable sub-project proposals in each country 
as well as helping improve the quality of the submitted proposals. As part of these efforts, the CDB TA 
Execution Team will also engage a consultant to assist each utility to establish or strengthen their 
helpdesk/customer support services to efficiently address queries from customers related to sub-
projects applications. Among other responsibilities, the National Energy Consultants are expected to 
assist the utilities in responding to such help-desk requests in a timely manner. In addition, CDB-
contracted consultants will also assist the utilities to develop simple, user-friendly energy savings 
calculators that customers can use for self-screening purposes. In so doing, this activity will enhance 
accessibility to energy-saving information and empower program participants to make informed 
decisions about their energy usage. Finally, under this activity, the Programme will also develop 
eligibility criteria to be used to guide the selection of specific beneficiaries (lower-income residential 
customers and vulnerable groups) who will receive free energy audits. Once the eligibility criteria are 
developed, the Programme will facilitate access to such energy audits. CDB estimates that a total of 
300 low-income households will receive free energy audit services financed through the Programme, 
with 20 selected annually per country from PY1-5 based on the eligibility criteria. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
Output 1.3: Increased technical capacity of utilities, TSPs and other stakeholders to deploy and 
maintain distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies. 
11. Enhance technical and institutional capacity: Insufficient technical and institutional capacity has 
been identified as a key barrier. This is attributable to several factors, notably that IUS constitutes a 
material shift from the conventional electric utility business model to more innovative approaches which 
are not common in the BMCs. Post IUS roll-out, the BMCs will actively promote behind the meter 
solutions resulting in a radical shift from the status quo. This sub-component aims to improve capacities 
and information exchange at the national and regional level to enable the partner utilities (and other 
stakeholders, both in and beyond the targeted countries) to make this transition, institutionalize the 
IUS model and learn from one another as they work to scale up IUS and transform their energy 
systems. In addition to the CDB, regional stakeholders like the CARICOM Secretariat, CARILEC, and

=== PAGE 41 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 35 
 
 
 
the CCREEE would play an active role to promote regional learning and ensure programme replicability 
and scalability. This Output will include five activities: 
12. Activity 1.3.1 Facilitate knowledge exchange among the participating BMCs:  Under this activity, 
the CDB TA Execution Team will work with an external service provider and national counterparts to 
document and disseminate good practices and lessons learnt related to programme 
implementation/execution among the stakeholders in the three countries. This will include developing 
knowledge exchange materials, such as presentations and briefing notes, and holding online/virtual 
knowledge-sharing sessions. The knowledge materials will be developed in consultation with (and 
using inputs from) the utilities, regulators and energy ministries, allowing them to learn from one 
another and support/strengthen each other’s efforts to successfully implement IUS. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
13. Activity 1.3.2 Enhance Executing Entities’ and other stakeholders’ capacities to implement the 
IUS model in a just transition context:  The roll-out of IUS by the three participating electric utilities 
will require them to adjust their business operations and establish new (or adjust existing) internal 
systems and procedures. The associated costs – particularly in the start-up phase of IUS 
implementation – can be significant and a risk to the utilities. This activity therefore focuses on 
enhancing the capacities of the electric utilities (as National Executing Entities) to implement the IUS 
model. As part of this support, the CDB TA Execution Team will contract a service provider to work 
with each electric utility to develop a tailored operations manual. These country-specific operations 
manuals will build on the overall Programme Operations Manual (see Annex 21), and will include clear 
guidance on: financial management and organizational matters; KYC, AML/CFT and other compliance-
related risks; product/service offerings; reporting requirements; environmental & social risk 
management; and GESI -related actions. To further equip each utility to implement IUS, this activity 
will support the preparation of an IUS business development strategy for each national Executing 
Entity.  Furthermore, the CDB TA Execution Team will contract an IT firm to assist each utility to 
develop a web-based digital application system to facilitate the receipt and processing of sub-project 
applications, leveraging existing utility-operated platforms to the extent possible. As utilities are 
processing sub-project applications, they will also have access to external consultant support to assist 
during the appraisal process, particularly for sub-projects that propose to use non-preapproved 
technologies (described in more detail under Component 2, below). Finally, under this activity, CDB-
contracted specialists will support each utility to strengthen their internal policies, procedures and 
systems to better advance GESI goals and manage E&S risks – efforts that will be complemented by  
targeted trainings on E&S, GESI, and the mitigation/management of compliance-related risks (e.g. 
KYC, AML/CFT) for relevant utility staff (one for each utility in PY1, and a refresher training for each 
utility in PY2). This multifaceted approach aims to equip stakeholders with the necessary tools and 
knowledge for the successful execution of the IUS model. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
14. Activity 1.3.3 Train qualified (technology) service providers, notably TSPs, to support 
consumers: This activity aims to enhance TSPs’ capabilities through specialized training modules, 
designed to support long-term market growth within the IUS programme. The training will cover a wide 
range of topics, including IUS programme requirements, technical and legal aspects, E&S risk 
mitigation/management, GESI, and M&R. Additional subjects such as contracts, customer outreach, 
quality standards, and familiarity with third-party certifications may also be included. Tailored to the 
specific needs of each BMC, the trainings will be delivered by CDB-contracted service providers, who 
are expected to deliver one training session in each country in PY1 and one additional training in each 
country (with a particular focus on E&S risk management) again in both PY2 and PY4. These trainings 
will collectively boost TSP expertise and their contribution to the sustainable energy transition. Indeed, 
better-capacitated TSPs are key to the long-term success of the energy transition, as the efficiency 
and effectiveness of their services are important determinants of whether/to what extent sustainable 
energy solutions are cost competitive.

=== PAGE 42 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 36 
 
 
 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
15. Activity 1.3.4 Support information exchange with other (non-partner) countries, incl. 
governments, regulators, TSPs and utilities:  Under this activity, the CDB TA Execution Team (with 
support from external consultants) will develop and disseminate outreach materials to key stakeholders 
in BMCs across the region. These materials will share insights, challenges, and opportunities 
encountered in clean energy deployment through the IUS model. The goal is to provide BMCs across 
the Caribbean with a comprehensive understanding of the IUS model and its potential customization 
for their jurisdictions, thereby laying a strong foundation for scaling up and replication under Phase II 
of the IUS programme. To further support these efforts, under Activity 1.3.4 CDB will host four (4) 
virtual information exchange and knowledge-sharing sessions – two (2) sessions in PY2 and two (2) 
additional sessions in PY5. These sessions will offer an overview of the progress, challenges, and 
solutions identified in participating BMCs, enriched by firsthand experiences shared by key 
stakeholders. Each session will feature a Q&A segment to foster interaction and engagement. Finally, 
this activity will support three (3) representatives from each of the three (3) participating BMCs to attend 
an in-person knowledge sharing summit in PY3 to promote IUS and the Caribbean’s experience in 
implementing this innovative model, covering travel and logistics costs. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
16. Activity 1.3.5 Create a Community of Practice (COP) in the BMCs, inviting governments, 
regulators, utilities, TSPs and NGOs to participate: This activity focuses on creating a Community 
of Practice (COP) to enhance peer-to-peer and south-south learning among stakeholders of the IUS 
programme. The COP aims to bring together TSPs, Executing Entity staff, and technical experts from 
different jurisdictions to share knowledge and experiences, thereby facilitating the advancement of the 
IUS Programme. The initiative will include virtual sessions with international experts on distributed 
energy and utility-led distributed energy models akin to IUS. Furthermore, an in-person field mission 
will be organized, hosted by one of the participating BMCs for stakeholders from the other participating 
BMCs. This mission will feature site visits to key IUS sub-projects and enable in-person, multi-
stakeholder discussions, enhancing collaborative learning and the sharing of best practices within the 
IUS Programme framework. 
Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team) 
Source of funds: GCF grant financing 
Component 2: Scaling up investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs by implementing the IUS 
model at scale in the participating BMCs. 
17. This component builds on the work done under Component 1 to finance behind the meter technologies 
and services for participating customers. This component is also the core investment component of 
the IUS Programme. Participating utilities, in their capacity as national Executing Entities, are 
responsible for managing timely execution of the loan component of the IUS Programme in each BMC, 
including screening and approving sub-projects, disbursing to TSPs, collecting and processing 
repayments/reflows, monitoring and reporting, etc. In addition, the utilities/national Executing Entities 
will work in parallel to invest in the deployment of smart meters/advanced metering infrastructure (AMI) 
that is needed to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of customer-level investments in RE, EE 
and other DERs, and thereby support the broader transition toward a more sustainable, resilient and 
distributed energy system in the three countries. This component includes one Output. 
Output 2.1: Installation of distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies, as well as complementary 
smart meters/AMI, among a critical mass of utility customers, including households, businesses and 
public sector entities. 
Participating utilities, in their capacity as national Executing Entities, will receive, review, screen, and 
approve sub-project proposals, as well as finance the approved sub-projects and monitor (and report

=== PAGE 43 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 37 
 
 
 
on) their timely implementation. In parallel, they will invest in smart meters/AMI. This Output includes 
one activity: 
18. Activity 2.1.1 Prepare, finance and implement sub-projects utilising distributed RE, EE and 
other DERs among households, businesses and public sector entities, while supporting 
complementary investments in smart meters/AMI: This activity aims to deploy the loan component, 
enabling utility clients to obtain financing for sub-projects in BMCs. The national Executing Entities 
(electric utilities) will review sub-project proposals and only approve those that comply with the 
programme eligibility criteria, which are summarized in Paragraph B.3.11 (below) and further 
elaborated in Annex 21: Programme Operations Manual. If a sub-project proposal uses the pre-
approved technologies – namely LED/efficient lighting, efficient AC/cooling, solar rooftop PV (with or 
without storage), and solar water heaters – the utility will not have to assess the technology eligibility 
as part of its review. Utilities will also be able to finance sub-projects that utilize other (non-
preapproved) RE/EE/DER technologies, as per their local demand, but will have to undertake 
supplementary due diligence before approving such applications, as outlined in Annex 21. Note that 
CDB tentatively expects the funding under the lines of credit to be structured as follows: 
USD11,667,000 for BLPC in Barbados; USD9,667,000 for BEL in Belize; and USD15,666,000 for JPS 
in Jamaica. These figures include GCF financing and co-financing, and constitute initial estimates that 
are subject to change during the final CDB appraisal of each line of credit. 
In parallel to their support for sub-projects financed through the IUS model (using their blended GCF-
CDB lines of credit), the national Executing Entities (i.e. the three participating electric utilities) will 
make complementary investments in smart meters/AMI at the premises of their customers. This is 
expected to include customers that benefit from sub-projects under the IUS Programme as well as 
other customers that may not benefit from such sub-projects during the Programme implementation 
period. These investments in smart meters/AMI will be led by the electric utilities independently of the 
blended GCF-CDB lines of credit, and thus these contributions are reflected in the Programme as in-
kind co-financing. The deployment of smart meters/AMI will improve the efficiency and effectiveness 
of investments in RE, EE and other DERs, and are critical to the long-term success of the broader 
transition toward a more sustainable, resilient and distributed energy system that the Programme is 
helping to catalyze. By increasing the use of smart meters/AMI broadly in their respective jurisdictions, 
the utilities will lay a foundation for the long-term sustainability of the IUS model and the priority/eligible 
technologies. 
Executing Entities: Barbados Light and Power, Belize Electricity Limited, Jamaica Public 
Service Company 
Source of funds: 
Blended lines of credit for IUS sub-projects: GCF loan financing, CDB loan financing 
Investments in smart meters/AMI: In-kind contributions from the national Executing Entities 
(i.e. the three participating electric utilities) 
B.3.6 The IUS Programme design detailed above will directly address the root causes and barriers described 
in Section B.2a through a dedicate TA component that enables a market ecosystem development by 
strengthening the legal, regulatory, and procedural framework in the participating BMCs as well as increasing 
customer awareness and access to finance, among other benefits.

by the participating electric utilities (as national Executing Entities). The preapproved technologies are the most

regulatory framework, historical and envisaged demand, among others.

Preapproved technologies 
B.3.9 Based on the assessment, four technologies have been preapproved for the IUS Programme, namely: 
LED/efficient lighting, efficient air conditioning/cooling, solar rooftop PV (with or without storage), and 
solar water heaters. Based on the current market conditions and the applicable legal and regulatory 
frameworks as highlighted in the earlier chapters, the overall ecosystem is relatively conducive for these 
technologies in the participating BMCs. 
B.3.10 There are however associated services that may be bundled in a packaged service offering by the 
utilities with the four preapproved technologies. This includes surge protection, motors and drives, power factor 
correction and other ancillary power quality services, and building/energy management systems. As such, 
additional services/products could be unlocked for utilities by advancing the four preapproved technologies.

Indicative administrative process 
B.3.19 Sub-project applications will be received by the National Executing Entity from eligible beneficiaries 
seeking to access programme funding. The sub-project application is then appraised by the relevant 
department/unit of the Executing Entity that is leading the IUS programme. The Executing Entity may seek 
support or advice from the TA Implementation Support Consultant while reviewing / appraising the sub-project 
application. Each Executing Entity, however, may not engage their National Energy Consultant in the sub-
project application review / appraisal process. The three National Energy Consultants (1 per utility/national 
Executing Entity) will rather focus on supporting business development and sub-project origination, as well as 
monitoring and reporting. If these individuals were to also support the review/appraisal of submitted 
applications, this could lead to a potential conflict of interest as they may ultimately be supporting appraisal of 
applications that they helped originate and guide.  
B.3.20 The Executing Entities will review if (a) the applicant meets the applicable Know Your Customer (KYC) 
criteria for each country; (b) the applicant satisfies the definition of final beneficiaries; and (c) the technology is 
preapproved.   After the preliminary checks, the Executing Entities will undertake an in-depth review of the sub-
project application, including but not limited to, (a) is the application made for one or more than one eligible 
technology/service, (b) historical energy consumption and bill payment frequency of at least three preceding 
years from the date of application, (c) in case of grid connected renewable energy sub-project the Executing 
Entity needs to check if there is enough available hosting capacity in the distribution feeder connecting the 
applicant with Executing Entity’s grid, (d) has a TSP already been identified by the application, (e) assess 
creditworthiness of the applicant, (f) socio-economic background of the applicant (for inclusivity under the 
programme), (g) E&S and/or GESI considerations etc. A more detailed description of the eligibility criteria is 
included in Section 3.3.2 of this Operations Manual, refer to Annex 21. 
B.3.21 After this review, the National Executing Entity will decide to either approve, reject, or undertake 
additional review of the sub-project application.  The TA Implementation Support Consultant will assist the 
Executing Entity in sub-project application appraisal, as needed. Based on the results of the assessment at 
this stage, the Executing Entity will process the sub-project financing under the programme if all the conditions 
are fulfilled.  
The generic flow to guide the Executing Entities has been presented in Figure 21 below. Detailed, time-bound, 
and tailored step-by-step procedures, along with the personnel responsible at each stage, will be developed 
and included in the Executing Entity-specific Operations Manuals that will be developed during the inception 
phase (PY1) of the programme implementation period. 
Knowledge management 
B.3.22 CDB will be responsible for knowledge management under the IUS Programme. In addition, the National 
Energy Consultants will be tasked with collecting and transmitting all the documents developed in their 
respective BMCs to the TA Execution Team on a quarterly basis. The CDB TA Execution Team will then share 
these documents and information with the CDB AE Team. The CDB AE Team may then also develop a 
knowledge sharing portal to disseminate knowledge with other stakeholders.

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
Implementation arrangements 
B.4.1. The CDB is the Accredited Entity for this programme. CDB has been operating in the Caribbean Region 
for over 50 years and has a strong track record of providing financial and technical assistance to support 
the regional energy sector and sustainable energy transition. CDB has also worked with electric utilities 
throughout the region, including BLPC, BEL, and JPS. As the Accredited Entity, CDB will lead programme 
implementation, supervision, reporting, evaluation and closure. Different areas within CDB will contribute 
to fulfilling these functions, as outlined in Figure 22 (below). 
• 
TA component: CDB, through its dedicated TA Execution Team, will directly execute most activities 
(and associated contracts and consultancies) under the TA component. The only exceptions are the 
National Energy Consultants, which will be procured and contracted by the national Executing Entities 
using GCF grant financing. This grant financing will be allocated and disbursed to the national 
Executing Entities by CDB and governed by subsidiary grant agreements between the Bank and these 
Executing Entities.  
• 
Under this Component, the Programme will facilitate capacity building and execution capabilities of the 
regulatory body, the Executing Entities, and other key stakeholders through the TA grant financing.  
• 
It will do so by addressing capacity, knowledge and awareness gaps that are inhibiting the flow of 
financing towards climate investment. Among others, the key outcome of the TA support would be an 
established distributed clean energy market and business ecosystem in the three BMCs which is self-
sustainable beyond the GCF programme funding. The TA support will also aim to establish the IUS 
model as a viable option for accelerating utility led distributed clean energy deployment in other 
Caribbean nations. Furthermore, this Component will support the effective delivery of the financing and 
investments under Component 2 and help catalyse the broader process of transforming Caribbean 
finance – and the targeted productive sectors and energy systems – over the medium/long term.  
B.4.2. CDB will support the partner BMCs, Executing Entities, and other stakeholders to better understand 
the links between their business operations and climate change, as well as apply this understanding when 
developing, appraising, financing, and implementing climate change-related sub-projects. The flow of 
activities is depicted in the figure below.

=== PAGE 54 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 48 
 
 
 
Figure 19: Flow of TA funds 
 
Source: CPCS 
• 
Loan component: CDB, through its Accredited Entity Team, will be responsible for managing 
relationships and credit lines with the National Executing Entities. Administration of the lines of credit 
will be done in accordance with relevant CDB policies and procedures. As is standard operating 
procedures of the CDB, the GCF loan resources will be deposited in a CDB-managed bank account 
(IUS Programme Account) and a unique identification code assigned to those resources. Aligned with 
the terms and conditions of the FAA and CDB’s AMA, all transactions associated with GCF resources 
for the IUS Programme will be tagged with this unique identification code, allowing for ease for 
monitoring and tracking via an account ledger.  
• 
CDB will extend a concessional line of credit to each national Executing Entity that includes both 
concessional GCF resources and CDB co-financing. CDB will enter into a subsidiary agreement with 
each national Executing Entity that governs the use of the loan resources, including the GCF resources 
and CDB resources which are expected to rank pari-passu. 
• 
Disbursement requests from the national Executing Entities will be submitted to, and processed by, 
CDB in accordance with the Bank’s OPPM. All disbursements from CDB and the IUS Programme 
Account will be made to the Executing Entities in pari-passu terms (funding will be disbursed 
simultaneously in the agreed proportion).  The first disbursement of the loan will only be made once 
the Executing Entity has fulfilled all Conditions Precedent (CPs) as outlined in the relevant subsidiary 
agreement between CDB and the Executing Entity. Subsequent disbursements will be made once 
other programme-specific CPs have been satisfied. These may include, inter alia:  
• Compliance with Financial Covenants that CDB may require of Executing Entities; 
• Completion of programme-related reporting for the previous period; 
• Confirmation of the projected demand for financing for sub-projects in the forthcoming period.

=== PAGE 55 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49 
 
 
 
 
National Executing Entities will be responsible for channeling resources provided by CDB to support/finance 
eligible sub-projects for eligible beneficiaries. To this effect, the National Executing Entities will enter into 
agreements with both the eligible beneficiaries and the TSPs to be contracted to provide the requisite services 
to the eligible beneficiaries.  
B.4.3. The National Executing Entities will also be responsible for collecting repayments from the eligible 
beneficiaries, through an on-bill financing mechanism, where the amount of loan payment is collected by 
each Executing Entity through its regular billing cycle. The national Executing Entities will also be 
responsible for repaying their loans to the CDB (including CDB resources and GCF resources) in 
accordance with the subsidiary agreement.  
Figure 20: Flow of line of credit 
 
Source: CPCS 
B.4.4. In addition to the above-described flow of funds and support for the IUS sub-projects to be financed 
and implemented under Activity 2.1.1, the electric utilities will also deploy and install smart meters/AMI at 
the premises of their customers. This may include the customes that will benefit from IUS sub-projects 
and/or other customers that do not benefit from IUS sub-projects during the Programme implementation 
period. These investments are not captured in the flow of funds diagrams because the investments are 
expected to be in-kind contributions by the participating electric utilities. 
Contractual arrangements 
B.4.5. The relationship between CDB and the GCF will be guided by the Accreditation Master Agreement 
(AMA) between these two organizations, as well as the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) that will be 
developed specifically for this programme. The FAA will reflect the fact that the GCF loan financing would 
be provided to the three national borrowers/Executing Entities through a CDB-managed trust. CDB will in 
turn enter into a subsidiary agreement46 with each Executing Entity (i.e. electric utility) that will address, 
inter alia, their responsibilities as Executing Entities and their obligations as borrowers vis-à-vis their 
respective blended GCF-CDB lines of credit. 
 
46 This is tentatively expected to be two agreements per national Executing Entity: a subsidiary loan agreement for each line 
of credit; and a subsidiary grant agreement to provide the grant financing with which each national Executing Entity can 
procure and contract their National Energy Consultant.

=== PAGE 56 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 50 
 
 
 
B.4.6. For each sub-project that is financed by a national Executing Entity (i.e. utility), the Executing Entity 
will enter into an agreement the eligible beneficiary (i.e. utility customer) and the Technology Service 
Provider that is contracted to provide the requisite services (i.e. installation of eligible technologies) to the 
eligible beneficiary. These sub-project-level agreements will cover the obligations of all parties including, 
inter alia: installation of the eligible technologies and ensure they are in good working order (TSP); 
adherence to appropriate environmental and social standards when installing the eligible technologies 
(TSP); providing the data/information needed to verify the timely execution of the sub-project (TSP); 
providing the data/information needed to monitor benefits arising from sub-projects (customer); and 
repaying the investment cost to the utility (customer), among others. Based on consultations during the 
programme preparation phase, CDB generally expects that most utilities will opt to structure these as 
tripartite agreements between the utility, TSP and customer. 
B.4.7. Specifically with regards to the TA component, the CDB TA Execution Team will directly procure and 
enter into contracts with almost all service providers, and will do so in line with CDB’s Procurement 
Framework and using its standard forms of contract. The only exception will be the National Energy 
Consultants, one of which will be procured and contracted by each Executing Entity (i.e. utility). The 
nationally executed procurement processes will also be managed in line with CDB’s Procurement 
Framework. 
B.4.8. These contractual arrangements are summarized in the diagram below. 
 
 
 
Key programme actors 
B.4.9. Below we provide an overview of the role of each of the types of institutions which will be involved in 
the Caribbean IUS programme within each participating BMC. 
 
The GCF 
GCF is accountable to the Parties of the United Nations Framework Convention on 
Climate Change (UNFCCC) and is guided by the principles and provisions of the 
UNFCCC.

=== PAGE 57 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 51 
 
 
 
 
The CDB 
As the Accredited Entity, the CDB will provide financing, financial oversight, overall 
programme supervision, and ensure the executing entities abide by the policies and 
standards of the GCF and CDB. CDB will also manage the blended lines of credit, ensure 
timely credit recovery, and ensure robust programme monitoring, reporting and 
evaluation. 
Environmental Sustainability Unit (ESU): The ESU is the Focal Point for the Green 
Climate Fund in the CDB. It will bear overall responsibility for supervising all programme 
activities, including: the TA support to be executed by the national Executing Entities; the 
TA support to be executed by the TA Execution Team; and the execution of the Lines of 
Credit by the Executing Entities (for which the ESU will collaborate closely with other 
relevant CDB staff, notably from the SEU). The ESU will compile all relevant programme 
information to draft reports for the GCF and its stakeholders. 
Board: The Board of Directors of the Caribbean Development Bank is the ultimate 
supervisory authority within the Bank and has the responsibility to ensure Management is 
administering the Bank’s operations according to policy. The Board may, at its discretion, 
request/receive updates on the programme. Board decisions or resolutions will be 
executed by CDB management, who will then direct the ESU and other divisions/unit to 
follow through on Board decision or resolution. 
Management: Includes the President, as well as Vice Presidents, Departmental Directors 
and Division/Unit Heads. Management acts upon Board decisions or resolutions, as well 
as on-demand of internal areas. Management will take action on programme-related 
activities if the Board or the ESU require this level of decision. 
Other involved areas within CDB that will support the programme through oversight are 
the following: 
Office of Independent Evaluation: The CDB Office of Independent Evaluation will be 
involved in supporting/quality-assuring the independent mid-term and final evaluations of 
this programme. 
Procurement Policy Unit (PPU): The Procurement Policy Unit (PPU) will be involved in 
supporting CDB-managed procurement processes, including those managed by the TA 
Execution Team and Accredited Entity Team. The PPU also includes a Claims team that 
will support the processing of invoices and payments to consultants contracted by CDB 
(both in its Executing Entity and Accredited Entity roles). 
Risk Management: The Risk Management Department monitors the Bank’s exposure to 
operative, financial and portfolio risk. For the programme, the Risk Department will receive 
periodic reports on loan performance from the Accredited Entity Team and may request 
corrective actions to ensure financial good standing by the utilities. 
 
 
Government 
Government ministries, departments and agencies are pivotal in shaping energy policy 
and overseeing energy market participants. Decision-making authority is often delegated 
to respective ministries, oversight bodies like public utility commissions, etc. to ensure the 
underlying sectoral goals and objectives are met. For instance, in the case of DER, RE, 
and EE, the government establishes legal and regulatory frameworks for the sector, 
broader policy goals and objectives like the NDCs, etc., regulations for private sector 
involvement, sets renewable energy goals, defines codes and standards for energy asset 
operations, and determines tariff rules. 
The success of a behind-the-meter RE/EE market is grounded in the government’s 
policies that act as a facilitator for both market development and growth. Without

=== PAGE 58 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 52 
 
 
 
government intervention to eliminate obstacles, the underlying business models may be 
unviable.  
The governments of the BMCs, through their energy ministries and departments, will be 
responsible to ensure facilitative legal and policy support as well as address policy, legal, 
and regulatory hurdles.  
Relevant Government Ministries   
 
Ministry 
of 
Energy 
and 
Business 
 
Ministry 
of 
Public 
Utilities, Energy, and 
Logistics – Energy 
Unit 
 
Ministry 
of 
Science, 
Energy and Technology – 
Energy Division 
 
NDAs 
National Designated Authorities are government institutions that serve as the interface 
between each country and the GCF. NDAs provide broad strategic oversight of the GCF’s 
activities in the country and communicate the country’s priorities for financing low-
emission and climate-resilient development. 
The respective NDAs will ensure various programme requirements like (a) the 
programme’s alignment with the local context, (b) stakeholder engagement, (c) liaison 
between key stakeholders, (d) support with GCF M&R, etc.  
GCF NDAs 
 
Economic 
Affairs 
and 
Investment 
Division Ministry of 
Finance 
– 
Economic 
Affairs 
and Investment 
 
Ministry of Finance, 
Economic 
Development 
& 
Investment 
 
Ministry of Economic 
Growth and Job 
Creation 
 
Regulator 
Newer utility models such as the IUS create new lines of revenue for utilities. To integrate 
this revenue stream into the utility’s established regulated business, regulatory 
interventions and clear guidelines are necessary. The regulators in all the participating 
BMCs will ensure that (a) the regulated business model does not violate or contradict any 
existing legal and regulatory framework; (b) the appropriate regulatory framework such as 
DER regulations like net metering/billing, etc. are well defined; (c) there is clarity regarding 
energy transactions, accounting, and compensation; (d) a well-defined customer focused 
and business-oriented interconnection/application process is in place; and (e) robust 
technical and grid standards are followed for equipment; etc.  
Electricity Regulators 
 
Fair 
Trading 
Commission 
 
Public 
Utilities 
Commission 
 
Office of Utilities 
Regulation 
 
Utilities (Executing Entities) 
There are three participating national Executing Entities under the IUS programme who 
are already familiar with DER and behind the meter RE and EE to a varying degree. For 
instance, the BLPC has been delivering the “buy-all, sell-all” initiative. JPS is among the 
leading Caribbean utilities delivering net-billing models. As a result, the utilities are already 
experienced with DER initiatives. 
Under the IUS governance structure, the Executing Entities will ensure a fair and 
transparent programme delivery to their customers equitably. To support the programme 
roll-out the Executing Entities will facilitate (a) all the programme related regulatory

=== PAGE 59 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 53 
 
 
 
approvals, (b) changes in the billing system for on-bill financing, (c) adoption of a 
standardized and timebound application process, (d) programme outreach material, (e) 
customer’s creditworthiness, among others.   
Additionally, the Executing Entities will (a) review and approve sub-projects with the 
support of the TA implementation consultant(s), (b) administer their respective Line of 
Credit, finance eligible sub-projects to support eligible  beneficiaries, collect and process 
repayments/reflows from beneficiary customers and (in turn) to CDB, (c) procure, contract, 
and manage, a full-time support staff – including a National Energy Consultant to be paid 
through the IUS programme budget – with expertise in distributed renewable energy and 
energy efficiency to support on day-to-day activities, customer outreach, business 
development, etc. under the IUS programme, (d) provide periodic updates and information 
to the CDB as per the monitoring and reporting framework, (e) set up/strengthen customer 
support centres, etc. 
Each national Executing Entity will be required to nominate a programme focal point who 
will lead on communications with other programme stakeholders (notably CDB) and will 
share this individual’s contact information with the CDB Accredited Entity Team and the 
TA implementation consultant during the start of the programme implementation period.

Technology Service Providers 
TSPs are private entities in the sector capable of providing various services like EE, solar 
rooftop, solar water heaters, battery storage for optimizing time-of-use, and operation and 
maintenance initiatives geared towards lowering customers' energy expenses, among 
others.  
TSPs will play a wider role in the IUS programme. TSPs will not only provide both 
distributed generation installations and EE services but also support broader efforts to 
enhance customer outreach and assist customers to navigate the IUS application 
approval.  
TSPs will enter into an agreement with customers and utilities to provide their services as 
per the technical, regulatory, and commercial specifications of the respective BMCs.  
TSPs will also be receive additional training(s) on the technology, legal and regulatory, 
and commercial aspects of the programme through the TA support. By engaging with (and 
benefiting from) the IUS Programme, local TSPs will become capacitated to provide 
additional – and better – services, thereby transitioning toward being able to serve 
households and businesses in ways that are similar to the more comprehensive sets of 
energy services provided by ESCOs in other jurisdictions. 
 
Other regional stakeholders 
• 
CCREEE: It has been associated with the development of the IUS programme 
since it was conceived. During the programme implementation and roll-out, 
CCREEE will leverage its unique regional position to help the BMC institutions 
with expert inputs to develop IUS conducive regulatory policy frameworks and 
facilitate in programme outreach, among others. 
• 
CARILEC: The Caribbean Electric Utility Services Corporation is an association 
of electric energy solutions providers and other stakeholders operating in the 
electricity industry in the Caribbean region, Central and South America and 
Globally. The CARILEC Secretariat plays a leading role in electric utility advocacy, 
growth, and sustainability in the Region. As a regional organisation CARILEC 
members include utilities, service providers, and training institutes. Consequently,

CARILEC can be a regional platform for knowledge sharing, capacity building, 
and training to strengthen the IUS programme’s supply side ecosystem. 
 
Business Associations 
Association like Barbados Renewable Energy Association (BREA), Barbados Chamber of

providers, and business. Such organisations will be leveraged by the respective energy

Customers 
The key beneficiary of the IUS programme in all BMCs reaping benefits like self-
generation and energy independence, lower electricity costs, efficient use of energy, and 
contributing to BMCs’ climate goals, among others. Customers will prepare and submit 
sub-project applications to their respective electric utilities. 
 
TA Execution Team 
The TA Execution Team will be established within the CDB and will execute most of the 
TA activities under the programme (and by extension, manage most procurement 
processes). The only exception is the National Energy Consultant, which will be contracted 
directly by the National Executing Entities (utilities). The TA Execution Team will include 
a full-time TA Lead who will provide overall technical leadership with regards to the 
execution of the TA component and will also include a TA Support Specialist to assist the 
TA Lead. The TA Execution Team will procure, contract, manage, monitor, and report on 
progress achieved by the other consultants that will be contracted under the TA 
component. The TA Execution Team will be established within the CDB Sustainable 
Energy Unit and will report directly to the Unit Coordinator. The TA Execution Team will 
also maintain a close dialogue with the CDB Accredited Entity Team to keep them updated 
on progress that is relevant to their overall programme supervision responsibilities, 
including supervision of the national Executing Entities’ progress in executing their 
respective lines of credit. 
 
TA Implementation Support Consultant 
The TA Implementation Support Consultant will be procured, contracted, managed and 
monitored by the TA Execution Team. The TA Implementation Support Consultant will 
include a multi-disciplinary team comprised of international experts (tentatively expected 
to include a Technical/ Clean Energy Expert, Legal/ Regulatory Expert, Energy Policy 
Expert, Gender and Social Inclusion Expert, Environmental and Social Risk Management 
Expert, Monitoring and Reporting Expert, Capacity Building Expert, and Marketing and 
Outreach Expert) and national expertise (tentatively expected to include a National 
Regulatory Expert). 
The TA Implementation Support Consultant will work in close consultation with three key 
national stakeholders in each country, namely the energy ministry, the regulatory 
authority, and the national Executing Entity. They will directly report their progress and be 
directly accountable to the TA Execution Team. 
 
National Energy Consultants 
One National Energy Consultant will be procured, contracted, and managed by each 
national Executing Entity, with financial support from the programme’s TA budget. The 
National Energy Consultant will be stationed at the Executing Entity’s office and will report 
to the appropriate department/ unit/ or office of the Executing Entity that leads the IUS 
service delivery. The National Energy Consultant will support day-to-day activities related 
to the IUS programme, customer support, business development, etc. The National

Energy Consultant, however, is not responsible to support the national Executing Entity

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
B.5.1. While the Caribbean region has embarked on significant efforts to enhance financing options 
for RE and EE projects, access to financing remains constrained, highlighting notable challenges 
across Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. While some public and private financing exists in Barbados, 
Belize, and Jamaica, it is primarily focused on businesses and does not cover the diverse technologies 
included in the IUS programme. Additionally, there is a shortage of commercial banks offering loans for 
distributed RE, and those that do often charge high interest rates due to the perceived high risk of lending 
to local households and businesses (particularly the types of lower-income groups and smaller businesses 
that the IUS model and Programme are particularly well suited to support) to invest in these technologies. 
EE projects, in particular, are rarely considered for financing. One significant gap is the upfront cost barrier 
versus long-term savings, which is particularly challenging for lower-income households and businesses. 
Although RE and EE technologies promise substantial savings over time, the initial investment required 
can be prohibitively high. This financial challenge is a major deterrent for both public and private investors, 
who may not have the capital or willingness to invest heavily without immediate returns.  
On the public side, capital is often constrained and must be allocated to competing priorities, leaving limited 
resources for RE and EE projects. Governments in these countries face numerous pressing needs, making 
it challenging to prioritize and fund energy transitions without substantial external support. Additionally, the 
lack of a clear regulatory framework and successful demonstrative cases further deters private investment.  
Funding structure 
B.5.2. To make the proposed programme (and its underlying projects and sub-projects) feasible, CDB is 
requesting concessional financing from the GCF. These concessional resources are needed in part 
because the programme will assist electric utilities to launch and institutionalize IUS – a new and innovative 
approach to supporting investment in distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies/measures among 
the utilities’ customers – as part of their core business models. The process of launching an IUS scheme 
in each participating BMC and implementing it at scale involves risks and associated costs that the utilities 
and partner governments are not able to bear on their own. The following types of GCF financing are 
therefore critical to the success of this programme. 
 
B.5.3. Line of credit/Loans: To launch and sustain their initial IUS schemes, partner utilities will need 
concessional loans to make RE/EE sub-projects appealing to customers, and to enable a more inclusive 
set of sub-project beneficiaries (including lower-income and vulnerable/marginalized groups). Despite the 
strong savings and short payback periods associated with distributed RE, EE, and other DERs, Caribbean 
households and businesses, wary of risk and often financially constrained, might hesitate to invest— a 
situation exacerbated by COVID-19's economic fallout. Recognizing this, CDB aims to encourage 
investment in DERs by ensuring initial modest on-bill rates to demonstrate value. Given the region's utilities' 
similar aversion to risk, introducing the IUS model with affordable rates and favourable terms is crucial. 
CDB will thus facilitate the transfer of GCF's concessional benefits to utilities, ensuring they, in turn, offer 
customers more accessible costs for RE, EE, and DER technologies than market rates. Note that several 
mechanisms are included in the programme design to ensure transfer of this concessionality to the end 
beneficiaries, including by establishing maximum spreads that can be charged by the electric utilities. 
 
That being the case, CDB is proposing that the GCF Secretariat provide a concessional line of credit 
amounting to USD 20.8 million. CDB in turn expects to blend the GCF resources with USD 16.2 million in 
co-financing. As outlined in the Integrated Financial Model (see Annex 3), this would enable the Bank to 
extend a concessional line of credit to each electric utility/national Executing Entity. Even after the utilities 
have placed a spread on these resources to cover overhead costs and a risk premium – rates that will be 
determined jointly with national counterparts, notably the regulators during PY1 – the concessional GCF-
CDB blended lines of credit will enable the utilities to provide financing to customers/end-beneficiaries at 
rates (tentatively 4.5-6.0%) that are competitive relative to the financing provided by traditional/commercial 
banks (whose products on average start at around 6.5%) while remaining strategically balanced – low 
enough to support greater social inclusion and incentivize investment, yet not so low as to distort the local 
markets. Additionally, this financing will allow access to funding for EE technologies, which are not as 
readily available as those for renewable energy.

=== PAGE 64 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 58 
 
 
 
B.5.4. Grant financing: The programme will facilitate capacity building and strengthen the execution 
capabilities of the regulatory authorities, the Executing Entities, and other key stakeholders through the TA 
grant financing.  CDB will support the partner BMCs, Executing Entities, and other stakeholders to better 
understand the links between their business operations and climate change, as well as apply this 
understanding when developing, appraising, financing, and implementing climate change-related sub-
projects.  These non-revenue-generating activities are only viable at full concessionality. CDB is therefore 
requesting grant financing to support the implementation of these activities. 
B.5.5. The GCF is uniquely suited to provide the above-mentioned types of financing for the IUS programme. 
CDB is not able to provide the necessary package of financing through its own resources. The Bank has 
modest concessional financing at its disposal through its Special Development Fund (SDF) window, and 
cannot use these resources to provide the volumes of financing that the three participating countries need 
to launch and implement IUS at scale. CDB also works with other partners to mobilize funds for climate 
action – including bilateral development partners – but they are typically unable/unwilling to provide such 
large amounts of funding for new/innovative approaches such as IUS. Indeed, many partners have a lower 
risk appetite than is required for the proposed programme. Given its risk appetite, emphasis on innovation 
and ability to provide large-scale financing (including grant financing), the GCF is therefore uniquely 
positioned to provide financial support to the proposed IUS programme alongside CDB co-financing. 
Without the GCF’s support, the proposed programme could not be financed/approved – and without this 
programme, the participating countries would not be able to launch and implement IUS. Indeed, their fiscal 
space (while already historically constrained) has become even more limited since the onset of the COVID-
19 pandemic, as well as the subsequent geopolitical instability and macroeconomic developments – 
notably the worldwide increase in interest rates that have significant increased debt servicing costs in 
almost all developing countries. Discretionary resources with which to address the financial and non-
financial barriers outlined in Section B.2 are simply not available. 
B.5.6. Co-financing: The CDB expects to provide co-financing amounting to about USD 16.2 million as part 
of the blended line of credit facility that will be available to the BMCs for sub-project installations. 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
B.6.1. The programme includes several elements that will help ensure services, benefit streams and 
outcomes are sustained after programme closure. 
(1) Project level:  Through this programme, key stakeholders in the participating countries will have 
established and operated the IUS model at scale over a multi-year time horizon, thereby providing them 
with valuable experience and a track record that can be leveraged to continue administering IUS after 
programme closure. Through the TA component, the programme will also strengthen the capacity of 
national stakeholders – notably the electric utilities and regulators – to continue implementing the IUS 
model without the need for external support. Furthermore, the programme will connect the participating 
electric utilities with commercial financiers and sensitize them about the potential and benefits of lending 
to the utilities to enable further financing of IUS sub-projects after the GCF and CDB financing have been 
fully utilized. In this sense, the proposed programme is seen as a ‘bridge to bankability’. Having established 
a strong track record of successful projects that generate reflows and enable a profitable IUS business for 
the utilities, these key stakeholders will also have a strong incentive to continue implementing their 
respective IUS schemes – scaling up and out – after programme closure. 
(2) Sub-project level.  The programme promotes investments in financially viable renewable energy 
technologies and energy efficiency services that are economically beneficial for the end beneficiaries. 
Given the direct financial benefits that are expected to be accrued by the beneficiaries, these households 
and businesses are expected to continue utilizing these technologies and practices for their entire 
productive lifespan. This extent to which this expectation holds will be assessed by the national Executing 
Entities and broader programme implementation team, in line with the Theory of Change, logical framework 
and monitoring and reporting framework (see Annex 22). Given these direct financial incentives, the sub-
project beneficiaries are also expected to properly operate and maintain their programme-supported 
technologies/assets and cover the associated O&M costs The anticipated returns from these investments 
will further ensure that the end beneficiaries have the necessary resources to meet these O&M costs. 
(3) TSPs and other financial services providers.  The programme incorporates a comprehensive TA and 
capacity-building element designed to enhance the capabilities of TSPs and other financial service 
providers in all three participating countries. As a result, these entities will be able to better assess climate-

=== PAGE 65 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 59 
 
 
 
related risks and opportunities and engage in supporting/financing the sustainable energy transition – 
including by working with the electric utilities to continue administering IUS services after programme 
closure. By doing so, the programme aims to tackle significant knowledge and capacity barriers that 
currently hinder the financing flow for climate-related projects. This is especially crucial for certain customer 
segments, such as residential customers, who typically struggle to secure affordable financing for such 
investments.  
(4) End beneficiaries. The TA and capacity-building component of the programme places a significant focus 
on increasing awareness and capacity among potential sub-project beneficiaries (i.e. local households and 
businesses). This is expected address key barriers that are inhibiting demand for both the prioritized 
technologies and IUS-type services, thereby increasing the potential uptake of IUS-type services over the 
medium/long term. Notably, the programme intends to reach a wide range of eligible customers, both 
residential and commercial, through its outreach and awareness-raising activities. While the programme 
will only be able to finance sub-projects for a portion of these eligible customers, the broader group of 
beneficiaries is expected to be more aware of the benefits of the prioritized technologies, more inclined to 
borrow and invest in them, and have enhanced opportunities to do so. This heightened awareness is 
anticipated to stimulate demand for financing across a larger customer base during and after the 
programme. To reinforce this effect, the programme will also support eligible final beneficiaries in crafting 
high-quality sub-project proposals. This will enhance the overall quality of financed sub-projects, leading 
to better investment performance. Consequently, the programme aims to generate a strong demonstration 
effect in partner countries, especially considering their relatively small economies. Furthermore, the costs 
of many promoted technologies and energy efficiency solutions are expected to decrease over time, further 
driving demand. Coupled with the TA activities that aim to address supply-side barriers (i.e. by 
strengthening the capacity of electric utilities and regulators, as well as financial institutions and TSPs), the 
customer-focused support should help ensure that the benefit streams of the IUS model are sustained and 
scaled up after programme closure.

 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Mitigation impact potential 
D.1.1. The analysis on mitigation impact potential accounts for key differences in the national context in the partner 
countries, including i) the estimated carbon intensity of electricity generation based on the Harmonized Grid 
Emission Factor data set published by United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, as well as ii) the 
anticipated changes to the emissions trajectory based on the countries’ unconditional targets for climate change 
mitigation. A detailed analysis is provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study and Annex 3: Integrated Financial Model. 
Detailed methodology to assess the mitigation impact potential is provided in Annex 22: Assessment of GHG 
emission reductions and their monitoring and reporting. 
  
D.1.2. The climate change mitigation impact for the sub-projects may arise from the grid electricity 
consumption avoided due to the electricity generation of solar PV and the energy savings of the more 
energy-efficient technologies.  The IUS programme has the potential to avoid a total of 601,550 tCO2e in GHG 
emissions over the productive lifespan of the priority/preapproved technologies that were used as a basis for 
preparing the impact potential estimates. The assumed lifespans are: 25 years for solar rooftop PV; 7 years for 
batteries; 15 years for efficient A/Cs; 5 years for efficient lighting; and 20 years for solar water heaters. The following 
table shows the total GHG emissions avoided under the IUS programme dis-aggregated by country and type of 
sub-project/technology. Detailed calculations are further elaborated in Annexes 3 and 22.  
Table 10: GHG emissions avoided, dis-aggregated by country and sub-projects

Overall, the mitigation impact potential for commercial sub-projects is higher than that of residential sub-projects 
because of the larger size of sub-projects (e.g. larger system size of solar rooftop PV). The only exception is for solar 
water heaters where the size of commercial sub-projects is smaller than that of residential sub-projects based on water 
usage assumptions determined according to local contexts. The differences between countries are due to differences 
in the carbon intensity of grid electricity. 
 
Adaptation impact potential: 
D.1.3. The direct beneficiaries are expected to include the occupants of buildings in households, businesses and other 
utility customers who develop (and secure financing from their utility for) sub-projects that enable investments in 
efficient cooling systems. As a result of such investments, these utility customers will become more resilient to the 
increasing risk of heat stress that is outlined in more detail in Annex 2b: Baseline Assessment Report. The estimated 
number of direct beneficiaries is approximately 10,655, including approximately 5,328 women and 5,327 men. This 
is based on the estimated number of residential customers who are expected to implement sub-projects through 
which they would invest in efficient ACs (multiplied by the average household size in each country) and the number 
of commercial customers who are similarly expected to implement such sub-projects under the programme. A more 
detailed breakdown is included below.
