Indirect beneficiaries 
Indirect beneficiaries are those individuals or entities who, although not directly participating in the programme, 
demonstrate a significant interest in the priority adaptation technologies and are likely to invest in or adopt these 
technologies independently or engage with related initiatives post-programme. These beneficiaries reflect the broader 
impact and awareness generated by the programme, contributing to market development and demand stimulation. The 
estimated number of indirect beneficiaries is approximately 30,000. 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
D.2.1. The proposed programme aims to contribute to the process of transforming the electricity systems and 
energy sectors in the three participating countries toward low-emissions and climate-resilient 
development pathways. The IUS model serves as both an "implementation mechanism" for demand-side RE, 
EE, and DER projects and a novel business model for utilities within a rapidly changing landscape. In 
implementing this innovative model, the programme will support sector transformation by changing the way 
utilities and customers think about, engage in and shape the energy sector. In particular:  
 
a. Utilities: The programme will assist each of the three participating electric utilities to offer a new and 
innovative DER-focused service as part of their business model. The utilities will fundamentally change (and 
expand) their role in the energy sector of their respective countries, shifting from primarily being a provider 
of energy to also becoming a provider/facilitator of energy services. In so doing, the utilities will become 
catalysts in the transition toward a more decentralized electricity grid. This is a fundamental (and necessary) 
step in the broader process of decarbonizing the electricity system and propose alternative solutions to the 
increasing demand associated from cooling systems in the participating countries – a process that the 
programme will equip the utilities to champion in the years to come. 
  
b. Customers: The programme also aims to reshape attitudes about energy (and associated behaviours 
and decision-making) and energy resilience and increase adaptation to heat stress among utility customers 
in the participating countries. By working with customers to better understand their energy use/needs and 
the significant potential of distributed RE, EE and other DERs (i.e. to generate savings, contribute to 
mitigating climate change, increase resilience and improve local air quality), and provide them with a simple 
and low-risk way to invest, the programme will empower these individuals to play a more active role in their 
respective electricity systems. This shift toward more active and engaged households and businesses is 
fundamental to changing the broader patterns of electricity generation, transmission, distribution, and 
consumption such that they make the greatest possible contribution to decarbonizing and enhancing the 
resilience of the energy sector over the medium/long term. This requires that distributed RE, EE and other 
DER technologies/measures be regarded not simply as consumer goods but rather as critical elements of a 
country’s energy infrastructure – a shift in views and behaviour that the programme will actively support.  
 
D.2.2. While aiming to change the ways that utilities and customers view (including their own roles in) and support the 
energy transition, the programme also includes other measures to help ensure a profound and lasting market 
transformation. This includes: 1) strengthening legal and regulatory frameworks in the participating countries 
to better support IUS roll-out as well as enhance the broader enabling environment for DERs; 2) enhancing 
customer outreach and knowledge sharing to stimulate demand over the medium/long term; and 3) improving 
technical and institutional capacities of key energy sector stakeholders (including utilities, regulators and TSPs) 
to ensure IUS services can continue to be provided – with increasing efficiency – beyond programme closure.

=== PAGE 71 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65 
 
 
 
 
 
 
                                                           Source: CPCS 
More specifically, the programme will aim to address legal, regulatory, and operational challenges by 
supporting the preparation and amendment of legal and regulatory documents to facilitate IUS implementation, 
the development of necessary regulatory frameworks, the modernization of administrative processes, and the 
optimization of energy accounting procedures. It will also prioritize capacity building for regulatory authorities 
and national stakeholders, aiming to refine policies, incentive frameworks, and investments related to IUS. 
Furthermore, the programme will boost customer awareness, create customer-friendly tools such as energy 
savings calculators, and produce marketing materials. Critical efforts also include hosting knowledge sharing 
sessions, developing digital application platforms for IUS, ensuring adherence to environmental and social 
standards, and encouraging regional cooperation to exchange insights and best practices. 
 
D.2.3. The programme has considerable potential to be scaled up within the participating BMCs and replicated across 
the Caribbean region in a way that would allow each country to prioritize the RE, EE and other DER 
technologies that will be most beneficial within their local contexts. To actively support these efforts, the 
programme will facilitate regional learning and knowledge sharing among Caribbean governments, the 
CCREEE, and other regional organizations. As countries adapt the model to their specific contexts, they can 
exchange insights and best practices, accelerating the transition to sustainable energy solutions across 
the region and contributing to global efforts to combat climate change. Furthermore, CDB plans to develop 
and submit a ‘Phase II’ IUS programme proposal over the coming years through which the Bank will actively 
support additional BMCs to implement and operate IUS at scale.  
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
D.3.1. The programme is expected to deliver significant sustainable development co-benefits. It will not only make 
important contributions to achieving Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 7 (affordable and clean 
energy) and 13 (climate action), but also SDGs 3 (health and well-being), 5 (gender equality), 8 (decent 
work and economic growth), 9 (industry, innovation & infrastructure), and 17 (partnerships for the 
goals). These co-benefits are described in more detail below. 
 
D.3.1.1. Economic: The IUS model will enable utilities in the BMCs to diversify their revenue streams, engage in new 
business models, and adapt to a changing energy landscape. Sub-projects will generate savings for the 
utilities’ customers, which can in turn be used to repay the investment costs to the utilities and cover other 
consumption and/or investments needs as required. As outlined in Annex 3: Integrated Financial Model, the 
estimated net financial savings (NPV) for the final beneficiaries/utility customers are expected to amount to 
over USD230 million over the full productive lifespan of the technologies to be supported through 
programme-financed sub-projects. These sub-projects will also generate revenue for (and potentially lead to 
employment creation among) TSPs in the participating countries. The broader process of decarbonizing the 
electricity systems and energy sectors (to which the programme will make an important contribution) also 
holds significant potential to generate income and employment over the medium/long term. Indeed, available 
evidence suggests that considerable (generally local) labour will be required to support widespread 
installation and maintenance of distributed RE, EE and other DERs – jobs that will continue to be in demand 
as the utilities expand IUS after programme closure. Economic co-benefits will also be accrued by the central 
governments in the participating BMCs, as existing energy imports are a significant drain on foreign 
exchange (FX) reserves – a challenge that the programme will help address by reducing the demand/need 
for imported fossil fuels.

=== PAGE 72 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 66 
 
 
 
 
 
 
D.3.1.2. Environmental: The transition to an electricity system and energy sector based on renewables can 
significantly reduce the potential for environmental damage associated with the importation, handling, and 
combustion of fossil fuels. Moreover, this shift can greatly enhance air quality in participating countries, given 
that burning diesel and other fuels generates substantial particulate pollution. In this context, it is also 
important to note that the nature of the investments to be made under the programme are not expected to 
result in negative ecological impacts (e.g. from ground-mounted renewable energy installations). Due to the 
small-scale nature of the envisaged investments, sub-projects are expected to have little/no detrimental effect 
on land use and biodiversity. 
 
D.3.1.3. Social: Decreased particulate pollution, resulting from reduced reliance on diesel fuel for electricity 
generation, can improve health outcomes for residents of participating countries. These social co-benefits 
may, in turn, lead to additional economic advantages, as healthier populations are often more economically 
productive. In addition, the IUS programme presents a significant opportunity for groups that have had limited 
access to traditional financing opportunities, including entrepreneurs, youth, individuals facing gender 
discrimination, and low-income individuals. As current utility customers, the IUS programme will allow them to 
bypass these hurdles by leveraging their existing contracts with the utility. By utilizing on-bill financing, the 
IUS model will enable these otherwise marginalized groups to invest and obtain RE and EE technologies 
without the need for conventional credit-based financial products. This inclusive approach not only promotes 
widespread adoption of sustainable energy technologies but also fosters social equity and facilitates a more 
inclusive and ‘just’ sustainable energy transition. To further support these efforts at greater inclusivity, the TA 
component will offer free energy audits to eligible utility customers (e.g. low-income residential customers). 
 
D.3.1.4. Gender: The programme will take a proactive approach to supporting gender-equitable development. This is 
guided by the programme-level Gender Action Plan (GAP) and informed by a comprehensive Gender 
Assessment. Specific actions to be taken by the programme include: (i) working with policymakers to 
mainstream gender considerations into energy sector policy development and planning, including by 
developing gender mainstreaming guidelines, conducting gender sensitization training for policymakers and 
regulators, and establishing a working group among the participating countries’ gender bureaus to empower 
them to learn from one another as it relates to integrating gender more prominently into energy sector 
planning and development; (ii) supporting women and other vulnerable and marginalized groups to directly 
engage with/benefit from the Programme, including by administering free energy audits for low-income 
customers, and by preparing and delivering outreach and awareness-raising campaigns that are specifically 
tailored to the needs of marginalized groups; (iii) supporting the electric utilities/national Executing Entities to 
utilize the IUS model to better serve and empower women and other vulnerable and marginalized groups, 
including by conducting gender-sensitivity training for the utilities participating and TSPs, supporting the 
utilities to develop Ius products/service offerings that are specifically tailored to the needs of marginalized 
groups, and supporting the utilities to adopt/strengthen systems for capturing and analyzing beneficiary data 
disaggregated by sex, age, income, location and household type; and (iv) directly (and independently) 
monitoring the number of Programme beneficiaries who are female-headed households (FHH), persons with 
disabilities (PWD) and low-income households in accordance with the target set in the GAP. The emphasis 
on supporting gender-equitable development is also reflected in the logical framework for the programme, 
which includes gender-specific and gender-disaggregated indicators where relevant/appropriate. 
 
D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
D.4.1. The BMCs are acutely vulnerable to climate risks due to their geographic exposure to increasing temperatures 
and extreme weather events, and their economic dependence on climate-sensitive sectors like tourism and 
agriculture. These risks are exacerbated by high reliance on fossil fuels in their electricity sectors, contributing 
to significant emissions and compromising climate resilience. The IUS programme directly addresses this 
vulnerability by promoting the adoption of renewable energy and energy efficiency technologies, thereby 
aligning with each country's commitment to reduce emissions through their NDCs. Moreover, by advancing the 
installation of DERs, such as solar rooftops, the project contributes to enhanced energy security, resilience to 
heat stress, and grid resilience in the face of increasing climate threats. 
 
At an economic and social level, the three participating countries are grappling with significant fiscal constraints 
and high debt-to-GDP ratios. Although the countries are all classified as ‘middle income’, this categorization 
masks significant social and income inequality within the countries. Such income-focused classifications also 
fail to account for the countries’ pronounced vulnerability to climate change and other hazards, all of which can

=== PAGE 73 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 67 
 
 
 
 
 
leave the public and private sectors less well-resourced to address future needs/challenges (including the 
sustainable energy transition) than other middle-income countries that are less vulnerable. Indeed, in all three 
participating countries, governments' restricted fiscal space limits their ability to offer the necessary 
concessionality to launch and implement the IUS model – including by stimulating initial customer engagement 
and addressing other non-financial barriers. Furthermore, local capital markets lack the depth and history 
needed to support the sustainable energy transition (including through the IUS model) without external 
assistance. To address these challenges, the programme will provide external financial assistance to address 
the range of financial and non-financial barriers that (as outlined in Section B.1 and B.2) are inhibiting the launch 
and implementation of IUS. 
 
D.4.2. It is also important to note that within the participating countries, lower-income households, smaller (and more 
informal) businesses and marginalized groups have thus far mostly been unable to participate in (and benefit 
from) the sustainable energy transition. The programme has provisioned for over 300 free energy audits for 
such customer groups. These energy audits will ensure that the programme is able to provide a just energy 
transition opportunity for these customers. Furthermore, the countries are all classified as ‘middle income’ but 
are simultaneously grappling with significant social and economic inequality - dynamics that are described in 
more detail in Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAP). To the extent that households 
and businesses have thus far been able to invest in DERs, this has mostly been limited to those that are on the 
upper end of the socio-economic distribution. Unless concerted action is taken to address the specific barriers 
faced by lower-income and marginalized groups, this dynamic is unlikely to change. The IUS programme 
therefore responds to these challenges, in part by providing a more accessible and inclusive way for utility 
customers to access the financing needed to invest. The IUS model (and the way it will be deployed under the 
programme) is designed to overcome barriers to entry into the sustainable energy sector for marginalized 
groups, such as women, PWDs, the LGBTQIA+ community, and indigenous peoples. The Gender Action Plan 
(GAP) under this programme seeks to provide targeted interventions to improve governance, participation, and 
access to resources and information. This will enable these groups not only to benefit from the prioritized 
technologies but also to become key players and entrepreneurs in this space, fostering economic development 
and social empowerment. The programme will simultaneously provide training and capacity-building initiatives 
to: (i) ensure that energy sector stakeholders (including utilities) are more adept at identifying, mitigating, and 
managing environmental & social risks; and (ii) support the participating electric utilities to be inclusive and 
responsive to the needs of marginalized groups and to reinforce their policies for the successful rollout of the 
model. 
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Alignment with national priorities 
 
D.5.1. As outlined in Section B.1, the proposed programme is closely aligned with the priorities identified in the NDCs 
submitted by Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. All three countries assign considerable importance to reducing 
GHG emissions from the energy sector by scaling up investment in renewable energy and energy efficiency. 
The proposed programme will therefore make important contributions to achieving countries’ NDC targets and 
priorities, as well as those stipulated in the policies, strategies and plans that underpin their NDCs. This 
includes, inter alia: 
 
a. Barbados: National Energy Policy (2019-30); National Climate Change Policy Framework. 
b. Belize: Horizon 2010-2030; National Energy Policy Framework; Sustainable Energy Strategy and Action 
Plan (2014-2033); National Climate Change Policy, Strategy and Action Plan. 
c. Jamaica: National Energy Policy (2009-2030); Climate Change Policy Framework; Vision 2030 Jamaica – 
National Development Plan. 
 
D.5.2. National stakeholders have also been actively involved in efforts to get their countries ‘ready’ for IUS, including 
by working with GIZ and the CARICOM Secretariat to implement upstream (pre-investment) IUS institutional 
support activities over the preceding years. These same stakeholders – including the utilities, energy ministries, 
regulators, and National Designated Authorities (NDAs) – have been working closely with CDB and the 
programme preparation team to develop the programme proposal. This is indicative of their commitment to 
launch and implement IUS and contribute to programme implementation as outlined in Section B.4. 
 
Capacity of executing entities to deliver

=== PAGE 74 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 68 
 
 
 
 
 
D.5.3. All three participating electric utilities (as National Executing Entities) are already experienced with DER 
initiatives. The National Executing Entities will build on/draw from this experience when executing their ‘projects’ 
(portfolios of sub-projects) using their blended GCF-CDB lines of credit, and will do so in a fair, transparent and 
equitable manner. With support from the TA component, the National Executing Entities will also: (a) facilitate 
all necessary related regulatory approvals; (b) change their billing systems as needed to enable on-bill 
financing; (c) adopt and implement a standardized and timebound application process; and (d) utilize and 
distribute programme-generated outreach materials, among other responsibilities. CDB and the programme 
preparation team have assessed the utilities’ policies and procedures to confirm that they have sufficient 
capacity to fulfil their Executing Entity roles (see Annex 2: Feasibility Study). These assessments also identified 
specific areas where additional capacity and support would be needed – all of which has informed the design 
of Component 1 of the programme. 
 
The role of NDAs and other (regional) stakeholders  
D.5.4. Similar to other 'behind the meter' transactions, the IUS programme involves a complex process with multiple 
actors. These include public entities such as government ministries and electricity regulators, as well as private 
entities like service providers and customers. However, the IUS programme facilitated by the CDB stands out 
due to its regional scope and the involvement of the GCF. This uniqueness necessitates the participation of 
various critical actors not typically found in standard ‘behind the meter’ transactions. For example, NDAs will 
play a pivotal role by ensuring the programme's alignment with the local context, engaging stakeholders, and 
acting as liaisons between key parties, among other roles’. The NDAs have also been closely involved in the 
programme preparation process, often directly supporting data gathering and stakeholder consultations. All 
NDAs firmly support the final programme proposal, as evidenced by the No-Objection Letters that are included 
in Annex 1. 
D.5.5. Moreover, the programme places a strong emphasis on engaging with regional stakeholders such as CCREEE 
and CARILEC, along with other national stakeholders including national energy agencies, business 
associations, and customers. Particular emphasis is placed on engaging underserved communities, as 
described in prior sections. 
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
D.6.1. The IUS programme is poised to significantly reduce greenhouse gas emissions in the three participating BMCs, 
with an estimated avoidance of 601,550 tCO2e. This results in an estimated total cost of about USD 70 per 
tCO2e avoided for the overall programme (including GCF financing and CDB co-financing).48 The cost-
per-tonne for the GCF is lower, at approximately USD 43 per tCO2e avoided49 as outlined in Table 8 below. 
These are considered to be competitive cost-per-tonne rates in a SIDS context, where emissions 
reductions are often more costly than in larger countries.

D.6.2. The programme will also deliver important adaptation benefits. As outlined above in Section D.1, 
approximately10,655 people, including approximately 5,328 women and 5,327 men, are expected to directly 
benefit from enhanced resilience to the increasing risk of heat stress. The cost per direct beneficiary would 
therefore be about USD 3,938. Although this figure seems high when compared to adaptation-focused projects 
financed by GCF, it needs to be evaluated within the context of the broader programme. In particular, it is 
important to bear in mind that: (i) the programme is not strictly focused on climate change adaptation, and will 
also produce considerable mitigation benefits as outlined above; and (ii) the programme focuses not only on 
scaling up investment during its implementation period but also aims to catalyse (and equip national

stakeholders to lead) the sustainable energy transition over the medium/long term, during which the utilities will 
continue to deliver IUS services and thereby support many more customers to invest in technologies that 
enhance resilience and reduce GHG emissions. Moreover, the programme is expected to develop or strengthen 
awareness on technologies to increase resilience to heat stress for approximately 30,000 indirect beneficiaries 
 
D.6.3. Another important metric is the level of co-financing mobilized under the programme. As outlined in Section C, 
the financial contribution from the GCF is expected to directly mobilize additional resources from the CDB. In 
particular, the GCF’s USD 20.80 million contribution to the loan component of the programme is expected to be 
blended with approximately USD 16.2 million in financing from the CDB. Furthermore, the participating electric 
utilities/national Executing Entities are expected to mobilize an additional USD 25 million as in-kind contributions 
in the form of investments in smart meters/AMI. This constitutes a favourable co-financing ratio, particularly for 
this type of programme (wherein new and innovative business models are being established) in a SIDS context 
– all of which requires a higher overall level of concessionality than may be needed for other types of projects 
in other jurisdictions.  In this context, it is also important to note that metrics such as the co-financing ratio do 
not fully capture the benefits of the IUS programme. As outlined above, the sub-projects to be financed during 
programme implementation are simply an initial round that will provide the utility with a strong track record while 
also providing a strong demonstration effect within the local markets. Considerable additional investments are 
expected to occur downstream from the programme, which is regarded as a ‘bridge to bankability’ that will equip 
the utilities to secure other (less concessional) sources of financing for follow-on rounds of IUS sub-projects 
after the programme implementation period has ended.

may be achieved through the programme, which are based on reasonable assumptions about the number

D.6.5. Furthermore, as detailed in Section B.3 and further elaborated in Annex 2: Feasibility Study, the technologies 
that have been preapproved under the programme were prioritized because they fulfil the following important 
performance-related criteria: they provide direct mitigation and adaptation benefits; their capital cost is low

and monitor co-benefit indicators under the “Project/programme co-benefit indicators” section in table below. Add rows as needed.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
E.7.1. The implementation of the IUS programme will involve: 
• 
Monitoring and reporting on progress in relation to the programme’s logical framework, as outlined section E.5; 
• 
Monitoring and reporting on financial progress (i.e. approvals/commitments, disbursements, reflows);

=== PAGE 111 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 105 
 
 
 
 
 
 
• 
Monitoring and reporting on sub-project compliance with environmental and social risk management requirements, guided by the Environmental and Social 
Management Framework (ESMF) that is included in Annex 6; 
• 
Monitoring and reporting on the implementation of the GAP that is included in Annex 8; 
• 
The consolidation of the above information in regular progress reports; and  
• 
Mid-term and final evaluations to assess implementation progress and challenges and propose corrective actions. 
E.7.2. Additional information about the monitoring and reporting processes is included in Annex 21: Programme Operations Manual. Further information about the 
monitoring and reporting frequency and distribution of roles & responsibilities is also included in Annex 11: M&E Plan. Meanwhile, a detailed overview of the 
methodology for preparing the ex-ante estimates of emissions to be reduced/avoided, as well as the methods to be used to calculate actual emissions reductions 
during the implementation of the IUS programme, is included in Annex 22: Assessment of GHG Emission Reductions and their Monitoring and Reporting. 
E.7.3. Monitoring 
Under Component 1, monitoring will be led by the CDB TA Execution Team, which will be overall responsibility for procuring and contracting almost all consultants 
and other service providers that will deliver support under the TA component. The CDB TA Execution Team will ensure that the consultants/service providers complete 
their assignments in a timely and efficient manner and will gather the data and documentation needed to demonstrate progress (in line with the logical framework for 
the programme) during the implementation of the consultancy and non-consultancy contracts. This will include monitoring to ensure that the ESMF and GAP and are 
being fully and effectively implemented. 
Under Component 2, the monitoring of sub-projects will be led by the electric utilities (as National Executing Entities). Initial data on sub-project implementation (i.e. 
installation of eligible technologies) will be sourced from the contracted TSPs, as will information about adherence to necessary environmental and social risk 
management standards. Specifically with regards to sub-project performance, the electric utilities (with support from their programme-financed National Energy 
Consultants) will utilize the customer’s electricity meter to establish a customer-specific baseline for each sub-project that they have decided to finance. The utilities 
will then continue to use the customers’ meters to monitor progress after the technologies have been installed and throughout the programme implementation period, 
in line with the methods outlined in Annex 22. 
To supplement the meter-based monitoring, the CDB TA Execution Team will also contact a firm to assist with gathering additional data/information from sub-project 
beneficiaries as needed, including through telephone interviews, surveys and site visits. This will enable the utilities and CDB to cross-check the data from the meters 
at regular intervals throughout the programme implementation period, and thus validate the meter data and/or explain anomalies. The firm will gather data from a 
random sample of beneficiary customers each year while also following up with those whose meter data deviates from the changes/benefits that the utility would 
have projected at the time of sub-project approval. 
E.7.4. Reporting 
Reporting from electric utilities (National Executing Entities) to the CDB Accredited Entity Team. CDB will enter into a subsidiary agreement with each electric 
utility that clearly specifies the utility’s reporting obligations. This will include both financial reporting (information on disbursements, reflows, etc.) and progress 
reporting (information on the sub-project pipeline, approvals, implementation status and progress vis-à-vis performance metrics), covering each utility’s full portfolio 
of sub-projects financed using the blended line of credit provided under the IUS programme. The utilities will be required to prepare and submit these reports to the

=== PAGE 112 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 106 
 
 
 
 
 
 
CDB Accredited Entity Team at regular intervals to be specified in the subsidiary agreements. The utilities’ reporting to CDB will also include information on compliance 
with environmental and social risk management requirements, as well as GESI requirements, for the portfolio of sub-projects. The utilities will be expected to 
gather/collect the data needed for these reports, including using the methods/processes outlined above. 
Reporting from CDB to GCF. Using the data contained in the reporting provided by the National Executing Entities and the CDB TA Execution Team, as well as 
other sources of data/information as relevant, the CDB Accredited Entity Team will provide the GCF Secretariat with: 
• 
Annual Performance Reports (APRs) that provide detailed information on the status of the programme throughout its implementation period; 
• 
A mid-term evaluation (interim evaluation) report at the midpoint of the implementation period of the programme, which will be prepared by an independent 
external evaluator contracted by CDB; and 
• 
A final evaluation report at the end of the implementation period of the programme, which is expected to be prepared by the same independent external 
evaluator as the mid-term evaluation. 
 
E.7.5. Evaluation 
In line with GCF requirements, CDB will contract an external evaluator to conduct independent evaluations halfway into the programme implementation period (mid-
term review) and at the end of the programme (final evaluation). The CDB Accredited Entity Team will procure the evaluator using an open tender process and will 
supervise and quality-assure the work of the evaluator in close collaboration with the Bank’s Office of Independent Evaluation (OIE). The mid-term and final 
evaluations will be conducted in line with, inter alia, the following principles: independence; competencies and capacities; impartiality; credibility; transparency; integrity 
and gender equality. 
The evaluation methods for the mid-term and final evaluations are to be determined, but are tentatively expected to include: (i) key informant interviews with beneficiary 
customers and institutional beneficiaries (i.e. utilities, regulators, TSPs); and (ii) detailed review of relevant programme documentation, including monitoring data 
provided by the electric utilities and CDB TA Execution Team, additional monitoring data (from interviews, surveys and site visits) collected by the data gathering firm 
contracted by the CDB TA Execution Team, APRs and other project reports, based on an agreed list of evidence to be provided by CDB to the contracted evaluator.

 RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)  
Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing 
(ML/TF), sanctions, prohibited practices, and other risks that might prevent the project/programme objectives from 
being achieved. Also describe the proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.  
 
For probability: High has significant probability, Medium has moderate probability, Low has negligible probability 
For impact: High has significant impact, Medium has moderate impact, Low has negligible impact 
Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistleblowers or witnesses, as 
well as fraudulent, coercive, collusive, and obstructive practices 
Selected Risk Factor 1 
Challenges in establishing efficient systems and procedures for IUS service 
delivery 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Credit 
Medium 
High 
Description 
F.1.1. There is a moderate likelihood that utilities may encounter challenges in putting in place systems and 
procedures to deliver the IUS programme. For instance, there is a risk that utilities may not complete the start-up 
phase during PY1 as envisaged. 
 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
F.1.2. To mitigate the challenge of establishing efficient systems and procedures for IUS service delivery, the TA 
component focuses on comprehensive support and capacity building for utilities. This includes addressing regulatory 
barriers by assisting national stakeholders in updating legal and regulatory frameworks and strengthening regulatory 
capacity. External consultants will provide direct assistance and expert advice on regulatory enhancements. A digital 
application portal will streamline processes, while training will cover best practices and effective procedural 
frameworks. 
Selected Risk Factor 2 
Capacity constraints among TSPs 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
F.1.3. There is a risk associated with the quantity and technical capabilities of technology service providers (TSPs) in 
the participating countries to fully address the high/increasing demand for RE and EE technologies.  
 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
F.1.4. While this risk is noteworthy, the feasibility assessment did not identify it as a major concern. Additionally, the 
TA component includes training for TSPs to help them improve their services/products and enhance the efficiency 
with which they deliver them. The TA component will also help establish a Community of Practice within the BMCs 
through which the TSPs can share knowledge and best practices with one another, as well as with government 
officials, regulators, and utilities. 
Selected Risk Factor 3 
Regulatory and Policy Barriers 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Governance 
Medium 
High 
Description 
F.1.5. Inconsistent regulatory frameworks and policies across the Caribbean can impede the implementation of the 
IUS programme. Challenges include slow permit processes, lack of supportive policies for renewable energy, and

=== PAGE 114 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 108 
 
 
 
 
 
varying standards across countries. Utility regulators in several of the participating BMCs have also indicated that they 
would need additional training and capacity to effectively regulate IUS implementation in their respective countries. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
F.1.6. The IUS programme is a comprehensive initiative that involves collaboration with local and regional authorities 
to create spaces that contribute to the harmonization of energy policies and regulations supporting the deployment of 
RE and EE. Additionally, the TA component will assist the participating countries to develop and implement 
supportive policies. Finally, the programme will provide support to: (i) address pending regulatory barriers in each 
participating country; (ii) strengthen the capacity of the utility regulators in the participating countries to understand 
and regulate IUS; (iii) directly assist the utilities to apply for regulatory approval in PY1 to enable them to implement 
IUS sub-projects starting in PY2; and (iv) establish a process for regular regulatory reviews will be established to 
promptly identify and address potential barriers, ensuring that regulatory frameworks remain aligned with the 
programme’s goals and the evolving needs of the energy sector. 
Selected Risk Factor 4 
Risk aversion/reluctance to engage among potential beneficiaries 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Other 
Medium 
High 
Description 
F.1.7. Some potential beneficiaries may be reluctant to adopt/invest in new RE and EE technologies. This often 
stems from concerns over high initial costs, operational disruptions and cultural resistance to change, as well as 
regulatory uncertainties. Potential beneficiaries may also be aware of/interested in sustainable energy technologies 
but (initially) reluctant to invest in them through their electric utility given that the IUS model is new and thus not 
(yet) well established/known. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
F.1.8. The IUS programme includes a robust strategy to increase stakeholder engagement and buy-in. This will 
involve targeted educational campaigns to raise awareness of the benefits and practical applications of RE and EE 
technologies, as well as enhance awareness of (and interest in) the utility-led IUS model. Additionally, workshops 
and seminars will be facilitated to allow stakeholders to interact directly with these technologies, addressing 
concerns and demonstrating efficacy and safety. These sessions will also provide an opportunity for interested 
customers to learn more about the IUS service offering and address any questions/concerns they may have. 
Regular feedback sessions will also be implemented to ensure that stakeholder concerns and suggestions are 
continually incorporated into programme implementation, thereby enhancing acceptance and minimizing 
resistance. 
Selected Risk Factor 5 
Risk related to compliance, including money laundering/terrorist financing 
(ML/TF) 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
ML/FT 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
F.1.9. The electric utilities will be establishing new business models through which they will interact with 
customers (as beneficiaries) and TSPs (as contractors) in new ways. There are numerous compliance issues to 
be addressed in this context, including KYC due diligence, AML/CFT and environmental & social risk 
management, among others.  
Mitigation Measure(s) 
F.1.10. CDB and the programme preparation team have conducted a detailed assessment of the electric utilities’ 
policies, procedures and capacities with a view to determining/confirming their ability to serve as Executing 
Entities without posing unacceptable/unmanageable risks related to compliance issues. These assessments 
included reviews of the entities’ governance and management structures, financial management and accounting 
systems, internal controls and policies/procedures related to prohibited practices and AML/CFT. The two entities 
for which detailed assessments were completed (BLPC and BEL) were all found to be satisfactory in this regard, 
as outlined in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. JPS will be subject to the same type of review during PY1.

=== PAGE 115 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 109 
 
 
 
 
 
 
In addition, CDB will enter into subsidiary agreements with the utilities (as National Executing Entities) that 
include provisions related to AML/CFT, as well as other compliance-related risks. This is guided by CDB’s own 
policies and procedures in this area, which were reviewed during the GCF accreditation process and found to be 
satisfactory and consistent with GCF standards and requirements.  
  
In this context, it is also important to note that Jamaica is the only country from the BMCs that is currently in the 
“Increased Monitoring” list (grey list) (FATF (2024). Link) 
Selected Risk Factor 6 
Risks related to default/non-payment rates by participating utility customers. 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Credit 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
F.1.11. Based on the experiences of stakeholders in other jurisdictions that have implemented IUS-type models 
(i.e. utility-based financing for RE, EE and other DERs), CDB has estimated a very low default/non-payment rate 
among Programme end beneficiaries. This is partly because the use of the on-bill financing model ties 
repayment to the electric utilities/national Executing Entities to customers’ regular utility bills, which are a priority 
for most customers given the potential consequences associated with non-payment (i.e. being disconnected). 
Nevertheless, there is a risk that non-payment rates in the three participating countries may be higher than 
expected, particularly in the event of an exogenous shock such as a broader economic downturn. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
F.1.12. To mitigate the risk of higher-than-expected default/non-payment rates, the Programme has prioritized 
technologies that deliver direct and immediate financial benefits to the participating customers. This will ensure 
that savings from these investments help offset the repayment charges immediately. In addition, the use of on-
bill financing will enable customers to see both the savings that they have accrued because of the investment 
and the additional charges associated with financing the investment on the same bill, which will help 
beneficiaries – including those with more limited financial literacy – to understand the real-time benefits of 
continuing to pay these charges and thus meet their repayment obligations. Furthermore, the Programme TA 
component includes activities to support enhanced customer awareness raising and outreach, through which 
customers will be able to better understand the costs and benefits of the investments and how they will produce 
significant economic benefits over the medium/long term as long as they meet their repayment obligations over 
the short/medium term. The user-friendly energy savings calculators to be supported through the TA component 
will similarly help reinforce these efforts to enhance understanding among customers and thereby mitigate the 
risks associated with higher-than-expected default/non-payment rates.

=== PAGE 116 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 110 
 
 
 
 
 
 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
G.1.1. An ESMF was developed to ensure that the programme adequately identifies, mitigates, manages and monitors 
environmental and social risks and potential impacts during programme implementation, and complies with the 
relevant national legislation and guidelines in the participating countries (Belize, Barbados and Jamaica). By 
extension, the ESMF will ensure compliance with the environmental and social standards of both the GCF and 
CDB.  
G.1.2. The programme is classified as Category B. This is because the nature of the investments to be made through 
the sub-projects have the potential for limited adverse environmental and social impacts and/or risks, all of which 
can be readily identified, mitigated, and managed. Screening was first done at the conceptual phase of the 
programme by the CDB. A more in-depth environmental and social risk assessment and management analysis 
was done during the programme preparation stage, which was also informed by detailed consultations with relevant 
stakeholders in the three countries. The risks and associated risk mitigation/management measures outlined in the 
ESMF will serve as a guide for the National Executing Entities, which will (with support from the Implementation 
Support Consulting Firm) each prepare a utility-specific Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and 
corresponding waste disposal plan during the first year of programme implementation (PY1). The ESMPs will 
provide more detailed guidance to the national stakeholders – notably the electric utilities given their roles as 
national Executing Entities – on how to identify, assess, mitigate and manage E&S risks throughout the sub-project 
workflow/cycle. They will also provide/include: 
• 
detailed guidance on the specific E&S risks associated with each type of eligible technology; 
• 
a detailed checklist to be used by the electric utilities when reviewing sub-project applications to ensure such 
risks are clearly identified and sub-projects can be properly categorised, which will be an adapted version of 
the draft checklist that is appended to this ESMF; 
• 
actionable guidance on which associated risk mitigation/management measures should be implemented in 
each country, how they should be implemented, and by whom; 
• 
templates and associated guidance to support the development of the corresponding Environmental, Social, 
Health and Safety (ESHS) plan for any sub-project for which the screening identifies notable E&S risks and 
thus is categorised as a Cat B sub-project; 
• 
templates and associated guidance for TSPs on how to adhere to the E&S requirements outlined in the ESHS 
plans, and how to report to the utilities to demonstrate compliance with such requirements; 
• 
guidance for utilities on how to integrate the ESHS plan implementation and reporting requirements into their 
contracts with TSPs; and 
• 
a utility-specific disposal plan that specifies the requirements associated with the collection, storage and final 
disposal of waste (e.g. old AC units and other technologies that are removed during installation, as well as 
programme-supported RE, EE and other DER equipment after their productive lifespan has ended). 
 
G.1.3. The ESMF that is attached at Annex 6 also includes an initial overview of the sub-project workflow (with clearly 
defined roles and responsibilities for E&S management) to demonstrate when/how the identification and 
assessment of E&S risks, as well as the implementation and monitoring of associated risk mitigation and 
management measures, will be implemented. 
G.1.4. In addition to the ESMF, an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) has been developed to guide the 
development of an Indigenous Peoples Plan (IPP) in Belize and Jamaica – see Appendix C to Annex 6. The IPPs 
will be developed in tandem with the ESMPs for Belize and Jamaica, and in close consultation with indigenous 
groups in the country, and (once finalized and validated) will guide all relevant actors to ensure the execution of 
programme activities in Belize and Jamaica is done in an inclusive and culturally appropriate manner. 
G.1.5. Although the E&S risks and potential impacts associated with the IUS programme are likely to be limited and 
manageable, it will be important to strengthen and supplement the capacity of the National Executing Entities and 
other relevant stakeholders (e.g. TSPs) to enable full and effective compliance with the ESMF (and later, ESMP) 
requirements. The TA component of the programme therefore includes key support measures, including training 
for electric utilities and other relevant stakeholders (e.g. TSPs) on E&S risk identification, mitigation, and 
management monitoring, and reporting during programme implementation. The TA component of the programme 
will also supplement the capacity of the electric utilities (in their role as National Executing Entities) to fulfil their

=== PAGE 117 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 111 
 
 
 
 
 
E&S-related responsibilities, notably by contracting: (i) a National Energy Consultant to work with/in each electric 
utility; and (ii) an Implementation Support Consulting Firm that will include an E&S Specialist.  
G.1.6. To enhance accountability and thereby help ensure that E&S risks are properly mitigated and managed, the 
programme will provide easily accessible avenues for national stakeholders (including sub-project beneficiaries 
and non-beneficiaries who are affected by the programme and/or sub-projects) to submit complaints/grievances. 
This includes: (i) working with each national Executing Entity to establish their own channels through which 
grievances/complaints can be submitted; (ii) CDB’s Projects Complaints Mechanism (PCM), which is further 
described below; and (iii) the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism, which is also elaborated below. 
CDB Projects Complaints Mechanism 
The CDB PCM is managed by the CDB’s Office of Integrity, Compliance and Accountability (ICA). Through this 
PMC, complaints can be submitted through six channels: a webform; a hotline; email; telephone; mail; and office 
visits. Guided by the CDB Projects Complaints Mechanism Policy54, such complaints are reviewed and handled 
independently by the CDB ICA in accordance with its detailed PCM Procedures.  
Allegations may be made by a variety of sources including staff, consultants, contractors, and other people who 
may consider themselves participants in, witness to or victims of wrongdoing. Detailed information about the 
channels through which complaints can be filed anonymously are included in the ESMF and also outlined below 
for ease of reference:  
• 
online through a portal: https://secure.ethicspoint.com/domain/media/en/gui/55678/index.html 
• 
through a whistle blower hotline (770-409-5029), via telephone (246-539-1777), 
• 
via email (ica@caribank.org), and  
• 
via mail to: 
Head, Office of Integrity Compliance and Accountability  
Building A 
Caribbean Development Bank 
P.O. Box 408 
Wildey 
St. Michael 
Barbados, W. I. BB11000 
Complaints 
related 
to 
the 
programme 
can 
also 
be 
filed 
to 
anticorruption@caribank.org, 
Projectscomplaints@caribank.org  
 
GCF Independent Redress Mechanism 
The GCF Independent Redress Mechanism can be used by project workers, communities and other affected 
stakeholders as needed to submit complaints/grievances directly to the GCF. More information about the GCF 
Independent Redress Mechanism is accessible at the following link: 
https://irm.greenclimate.fund/  
 
Specific complaints can be submitted to the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism via the following link: 
https://irm.greenclimate.fund/case-register/file-complaint 
 
G.1.7. The ESMF was developed in close consultation with relevant national stakeholders. The programme 
preparation team consulted with, inter alia, government officials, utility regulators, and the electric utilities via a 
questionnaire-based survey. The team also organized virtual face-to-face interviews with TSPs, whose insights 
were of critical importance given their central role in implementing and monitoring (and reporting on) risk mitigation 
and management measures. Consultations were organized between April and May 2023. Apart from responses 
obtained through questionnaire-based surveys, during interviews, notes were taken, and each interview lasted for 
about thirty-five minutes. To minimize the potential for biased responses, the programme preparation team avoided 
leading the interviewees during the interviews and reacted in a neutral manner by not signalling preferred answers.  
 
 
54 This policy remains in effect and is publicly accessible here: https://www.caribank.org/about-us/policies-and-
strategies/projects-complaints-mechanism-policy.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 112 
 
 
 
 
 
G.1.8. Stakeholder engagement will be similarly important during programme implementation. The ESMF provides 
clear guidance to the Executing Entities in this regard. The above-described trainings for electric utilities and TSPs 
will also further strengthen their capacity to support inclusive stakeholder engagement in line with ESMF 
requirements. the programme will further supplement the electric utilities’ capacities in this area by contracting (i) 
a National Energy Consultant to work with/in each electric utility; and (ii) an Implementation Support Consulting 
Firm that will include an E&S Specialist. 
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
G.2.1. The design of the programme has been informed by a detailed Gender Assessment, which focused on four 
key groups as relevant per country i.e. women, persons with disabilities (PWDs), the LGBTQIA+ community, and 
indigenous peoples (specific to Belize). The universal challenge experienced by all groups was the limited access 
to finance and financial services. Although the ways in which this challenge may manifest for each group often 
differ due to unique circumstances such as locality (more specific to indigenous people), discrimination, low 
economic power and lack of capital, the resulting effect is the same across the board - namely that financial and 
non-financial barriers are inhibiting the extent to which marginalised groups can participate in/benefit from the 
sustainable energy transition, and thus will not automatically benefit from the services and opportunities presented 
by this programme.  
 
G.2.2. An additional barrier observed primarily in Belize and Jamaica is the weak enabling environment for the 
adoption of renewable energy technologies. These countries have seen less work and campaigning done around 
promoting the use and uptake of said technologies. This can be a deterrent as persons may be unaware of the 
benefits and may perceive these technologies to be more costly and burdensome to implement. Additionally, 
current attitudes on these technologies may not be the most conducive to the successful implementation of this 
programme.  This information and empowerment gap is a critical one to be tackled through the TA component on 
several levels.  
 
G.2.3. An additional and unique barrier facing indigenous people is that their traditional homes may not be compatible 
with some of the technologies on offer. PWDs and the LGBTQIA community also face access and affordability 
barriers including those shaped by bias and discrimination and that take the form of inaccessibility of services, 
though the reasons for these between the two groups.

=== PAGE 119 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 113 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 22: Summary of barriers and constraints experienced by marginalised groups attempting to utilise or 
engage with RE technologies55 
 
G.2.4. Through the Gender Action Plan (GAP), the programme will address the following gender needs specific to this 
programme: governance and participation (through targeted action focused on policies, the utilities and other 
key actors), and access, education and assets (through targeted action focused on the marginalized 
beneficiaries). More specifically, the GAP – the contents of which are coherent with, and have been integrated into, 
the broader programme (notably the TA component) – will support the following types of actions: 
 
(a) Improving the enabling environment: The programme will support electric utilities and energy ministries to better 
understand gender and social inclusion issues in the context of the energy transition and integrate this 
understanding when supporting them to strengthen relevant policies, planning and procedures. In addition, training 
will be provided for utilities, TSPs and other key energy sector stakeholders to (i) improve their understanding of 
the programme GAP, its requirements, etc.; and (ii) enhance their awareness of broader GESI and Sexual 
Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) issues in the energy sector. In so doing, these GAP measures will 
improve the extent to which local (sustainable) energy services are responsive to the needs of marginalized groups. 
 
(b) Enhancing awareness among marginalised groups: The programme will support the development of marketing 
and outreach strategies to promote the prioritized technologies (to be led by the energy ministries) and the IUS 
programme (to be led by the electric utilities). These strategies will place a specific emphasis on reaching, and 
raising awareness among, marginalized groups. The materials to be developed through the programme TA 
component to support implementation of these strategies will similarly emphasize inclusivity, as outlined in Section 
B.3 (above). Furthermore, the programme partners (including energy ministries and utilities) will work with local 
NGOs to implement their marketing and outreach strategies, particularly to help disseminate awareness-raising 
materials among marginalized groups with which NGOs are better connected. In so doing, these GAP measures 
will ensure that marginalized groups are aware of both the prioritized technologies and how the programme can 
assist them to invest. 
 
(c) Promoting a more inclusive model: Complementing the two above-described areas is the fact that the IUS 
model itself provides a more inclusive way to support sustainable energy investments among marginalized groups. 
By eliminating the need for beneficiaries to secure loan financing from a local commercial bank, and by allowing 
repayment on favourable terms through on-bill financing, the IUS model will directly address several of the financial 
barriers that otherwise often prevent marginalized groups from investing in sustainable energy technologies. 
 
(d) Direct sub-project support: While the above-described areas are expected to make meaningful contributions to 
improving the inclusivity of the IUS programme, lower-income households and other marginalized groups may still

=== PAGE 120 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 114 
 
 
 
 
 
find it difficult to identify and develop sub-project ideas/proposals that would be eligible to be financed by their 
electric utility. To help address this, the TA component will: (i) provide free (simple) energy audits for up to 180 
eligible (low-income/marginalized) utility customers in each of the three participating countries; (ii) assist each utility 
to establish and operate a user-friendly help desk and energy savings calculator to support customers with sub-
project application troubleshooting (which is expected to be particularly beneficial for lower-income and more 
marginalized groups that may have more limited technical capacity and financial literacy); and (iii) provide the 
funding needed for each utility to contract a full-time National Energy Consultant who can support business 
development and customer outreach efforts, with a particular emphasis on reaching and supporting more 
marginalized groups. 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
  
Legal arrangements 
G.3.1: CDB will sign a subsidiary agreement with each of the National Executing Entities that will govern the use of 
funds throughout the programme implementation period, as well as the terms and conditions for repayment of their 
respective lines of credit. The agreements will reflect CDB standards and requirements as well as any supplementary 
standards and requirements that may have to be added to account for the provisions in the Funded Activity Agreement 
to be signed between CDB and the GCF. The process of preparing such subsidiary agreements will be led by the CDB 
Legal Department in co-ordination with the CDB Accredited Entity Team. 
Fiduciary standards 
G.3.2: Each electric utility (as a National Executing Entity) will bear overall responsibility for executing its respective 
line of credit under this programme in line with the subsidiary agreement that it will sign with CDB. When doing so, the 
utilities will administer resources using their internal systems and procedures (e.g. for financial management, 
accounting, internal controls, etc.) while also accounting for supplementary programme-specific requirements. These 
are captured in the overall Programme Operations Manual and will be elaborated in more detail in the utility-specific 
Operations Manuals that will be developed during PY1. As outlined in Annex 2: Feasibility Study, the programme 
preparation team has already assessed two of the utilities’ capacities to fulfil their respective Executing Entity roles and 
found them to be satisfactory. A similar assessment will be completed for the third utility during PY1, the satisfactory 
completion of which will be a condition precedent to first disbursement of the line of credit to that entity. 
G.3.3: The overall financial management of the programme will be done in accordance with relevant CDB policies, 
procedures and standards. These policies, procedures and standards are consistent with international standards, as 
evidenced by CDB’s accreditation to the Adaptation Fund and Green Climate Fund, as well as its track record of 
managing resources from bilateral partners (e.g. Government of Canada, Government of Italy, European Commission) 
and other Multilateral Development Banks (e.g. European Investment Bank, Inter-American Development Bank). The 
CDB OPPM will therefore guide all aspects of financial management under the programme, including disbursements 
to each electric utility/National Executing Entity under their respective lines of credit which are generally expected to 
use the CDB ‘float’ approach. 
Procurement 
G.3.4: Procurement under this programme will be managed in accordance with the standards and requirements 
outlined in the “Procurement Policy for Projects Financed by CDB” (2019)56 as well as the “Procurement Procedures 
for Projects Financed by CDB” (2021)57. The CDB Procurement Policy and Procedures are aligned with international 
standards for public procurement, and cover the procurement of Goods, Works, Non-Consulting Services and
