Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP275 
Proposal name: 
Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (IUS) to 
Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean (Phase 
1) Programme 
Accredited entity: 
Caribbean Development Bank 
Country/(ies): 
Barbados, Belize and Jamaica 
Project/programme size: 
Medium 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Overview  
1. 
This funding proposal, submitted by the Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) as 
accredited entity (AE), seeks to establish and scale an Integrated Utility Services (IUS) 
Programme in three Caribbean countries – Barbados, Belize and Jamaica – with the goal of 
accelerating decarbonization and enhancing energy resilience. The proposal represents Phase I 
of a larger regional initiative and is focused on enabling regulated national utilities to evolve 
their business models to provide distributed renewable energy (RE), energy efficiency, and 
other demand-side energy services directly to customers. 
2. 
The IUS model builds on the concept of utilities acting as energy service companies, 
bundling financing, installation and maintenance of clean energy solutions, such as solar 
photovoltaic (PV), battery storage, efficient lighting and appliances, into a service offer that can 
be repaid through on-bill financing. The utilities would serve as key delivery agents, supported 
by regulatory reforms, technical service providers and capacity-building activities. CDB serves 
as the central coordinating entity and lender, channelling GCF resources and co-financing from 
its own balance sheet. 
3. 
The programme is structured around two interlinked components: 
4. 
Component 1 (Technical Assistance) focuses on enabling conditions. It provides grant 
financing to support the creation of regulatory frameworks, institutional readiness, customer 
awareness, and technical capacity among utilities, regulators and technology service providers. 
CDB will establish a dedicated technical assistance team, supported by three national teams, to 
oversee implementation, stakeholder engagement, and knowledge transfer. These upstream, 
non-revenue-generating activities are essential to market formation and require full 
concessionality. 
5. 
Component 2 (Loan Component) provides a concessional financing facility for the actual 
deployment of distributed RE, energy efficiency, and distributed energy resources (DERs) at the 
customer level. A USD 20.8 million loan from GCF will be blended on a pari passu basis with USD 
16.2 million from CDB to establish credit lines to the participating national utilities. Utilities, 
acting as executing entities (EEs),1 will on-lend these resources to eligible subprojects – 
 
1 Barbados Light and Power Limited is a regulated electric utility in Barbados. It is a limited liability company 
incorporated under the laws of Barbados and is a wholly (100 per cent) owned subsidiary of Emera Inc., a Canadian 
utilities conglomerate. 
Belize Electricity Limited is a regulated electricity utility in Belize. It is a limited liability company incorporated under 
the laws of Belize, and is majority owned by the Belize public sector. The Government of Belize owns 32.58 per cent, 
while the Social Security Board owns 31.27 per cent. The remaining shares are owned by Fortis Cayman Inc. (33.3 per 
cent) and 1,500 small shareholders (2.85 per cent).

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 133 
 
 
 
 
residential, commercial and public sector – covering capital expenditures for clean energy 
technologies. The subloans are expected to be offered at interest rates in the 4.5 to 6.0 per cent 
range, which is below prevailing commercial rates and made possible through the concessional 
GCF-CDB blend. 
6. 
Each utility will retain the credit risk associated with the customer loans and is expected 
to mobilize an additional USD 25 million in in-kind contributions, largely in the form of 
advanced metering infrastructure. CDB has committed to entering subsidiary agreements with 
each utility to ensure proper fiduciary controls, eligibility criteria and mechanisms for 
monitoring and reporting. Regulatory engagement will be critical, as the design and terms of the 
on-lending will be finalized in collaboration with national regulators during the first programme 
year. 
7. 
While the Caribbean region has significant technical potential for distributed RE and 
strong public support for energy affordability, uptake has been hampered by high upfront costs, 
credit access constraints, and underdeveloped regulatory environments. This programme 
attempts to address these barriers by empowering the utilities that are trusted and well-
established actors, to become the primary facilitators of behind-the-meter clean energy 
deployment. By integrating concessional financing, technical assistance and regulatory reform, 
the programme is designed to create a replicable, utility-led delivery model for the clean energy 
transition in the context of small island developing States (SIDS). 
8. 
The proposal also positions this initial phase as a pilot for a broader regional roll-out, 
with Phase II expected to extend the IUS model to other CDB borrowing member countries. 
Tools, data systems, regulatory approaches and institutional lessons developed under Phase I 
will be leveraged to reduce costs and streamline future implementation efforts. Knowledge-
sharing and regional cooperation are embedded into the design, with the intention of promoting 
replication and scale. 
9. 
While distributed RE, energy efficiency and other DERs often offer compelling savings 
and relatively short payback periods, uptake in the Caribbean remains constrained. Many 
households and businesses are risk-averse and face limited access to affordable finance. 
Similarly, utilities in the region have historically been conservative in adopting new business 
models. The IUS Programme seeks to address this by ensuring that financing is offered on 
favourable terms, with modest initial on-bill repayment rates that help demonstrate value to 
end users. A key element of the programme design is the structured transfer of concessionality 
from GCF to utilities, and from utilities to customers. Several mechanisms are included to 
safeguard this pass-through, including caps on the spreads that utilities can apply to the 
blended GCF-CDB credit lines. This is essential to making clean energy technologies more 
accessible than traditional financing channels, while maintaining market discipline. 
10. 
In summary, the IUS Programme proposes an innovative model to unlock distributed 
clean energy investments through utility leadership. Its success will depend on several factors, 
including the utilities’ operational readiness, the effectiveness of the regulatory and institutional 
reforms, and the extent to which concessionality is passed through to end users. While the 
approach is novel for the region, it rests on a sound rationale and responds directly to the 
structural barriers facing clean energy deployment in Caribbean SIDS. 
1.2 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
           Scale: Medium
 
 
 
 
  
11. 
The proposed IUS Programme seeks to deliver both mitigation and adaptation benefits 
through the utility-led deployment of distributed RE, energy efficiency and other demand-side 
 
Jamaica Public Services Company Limited is a regulated electricity utility in Jamaica. It is a limited liability company 
incorporated under the laws of Jamaica, and is majority owned by a consortium comprising the Marubeni 
Corporation in Japan and the East-West Power Corporation in the Republic of Korea (combined 80 per cent). The 
Government of Jamaica owns 19 per cent of the shares while a small group of minority shareholders control the 
remaining 1 per cent.

=== PAGE 134 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 134 
 
 
 
 
energy services in Barbados, Belize and Jamaica. These three Caribbean SIDS face increasing 
climate risks such as intensified storms, prolonged droughts, sea level rise and rising average 
temperatures, which are already affecting the reliability and cost of power generation and 
distribution. The programme responds to these structural challenges by enabling households 
and businesses to access and finance distributed energy solutions that are otherwise difficult to 
adopt due to cost, credit constraints and regulatory barriers. 
12. 
Mitigation Potential: The mitigation impact is based on avoided grid electricity 
consumption through the installation of rooftop solar PV devices, solar water heaters, efficient 
air conditioning, lighting and battery systems. The AE estimates that the programme could 
avoid approximately 601,550 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂ eq) over the lifetime of 
these technologies, using grid emission factors published by the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change Harmonized Grid Emission Factor data set and adjusted for each 
country’s national context and nationally determined contribution trajectory. 
13. 
This figure reflects differentiated assumptions about technology lifespans, ranging from 
5 years for lighting to 25 years for rooftop solar installations, and incorporates projected 
technology uptake across both residential and commercial users. Importantly, the mitigation 
potential varies significantly by country due to differing grid carbon intensities and market 
baselines. Jamaica, which has a relatively carbon-intensive grid, accounts for over half of the 
projected greenhouse gas savings (341,197 t CO₂ eq), followed by Barbados (215,219 t CO₂ eq) 
and Belize (45,134 t CO₂ eq). 
14. 
The independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) finds the mitigation estimation 
methodology to be sound and in line with Independent Fiscal Institution best practices, as 
detailed in annexes 3 and 22. However, as with many distributed energy programmes, realized 
emissions reductions will depend heavily on the rate of actual subloan uptake, sustained use of 
the technologies, and ongoing maintenance over time. Reliance of the IUS Programme on 
utilities to manage origination, installation and monitoring provides a plausible delivery 
channel, but performance should be closely tracked in implementation. 
15. 
Adaptation Potential: The IUS Programme also articulates a clear, though modest, 
adaptation impact. The primary adaptation impact is framed around increasing access to 
efficient cooling technologies ̵ particularly air conditioning systems ̵ which can help mitigate the 
health and productivity risks associated with intensifying heat stress across the region. Based 
on uptake projections, the AE estimates that approximately 10,655 individuals (5,328 women 
and 5,327 men) will benefit directly from these adaptation measures, primarily through 
improved thermal comfort and energy cost savings in homes and small businesses. 
16. 
An additional 30,000 indirect beneficiaries are projected, based on broader awareness, 
demonstration effects and potential market spillovers. While the indirect beneficiary figure is 
not backed by rigorous market modelling, it is a reasonable proxy for the broader ecosystem 
impacts expected through regulatory reform, stakeholder engagement and replication. 
17. 
The iTAP notes that the adaptation logic is credible but would benefit from a more 
detailed vulnerability analysis and a clearer articulation of the causal pathways linking cooling 
access to resilience outcomes, particularly for marginalized or climate-sensitive populations. 
Annex 2b provides some baseline data but monitoring and evaluation at the implementation 
stage will need to track adaptation benefits more rigorously, ideally with disaggregated 
reporting and engagement of health or resilience-related institutions. 
18. 
Enabling Environment and Structural Impact: Beyond direct mitigation and adaptation 
impacts, the IUS Programme contributes to long-term market development by supporting policy 
reform and institutional strengthening under Component 1. This includes establishing a 
regulatory framework for behind-the-meter solutions, enhancing utility and technology 
provider capacity, and building tools and systems that can be leveraged for future DER 
deployment. The emphasis on local stakeholder outreach and regional knowledge-sharing is

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 135 
 
 
 
 
appropriate and consistent with the goal of scaling the model across additional borrowing 
member countries in a potential Phase II. 
19. 
The AE has also proposed several mechanisms to ensure that concessionality from GCF 
is passed through to end beneficiaries, including limits on utility spreads and regulatory 
oversight of retail interest rates. This is particularly important given the AE’s own limitations in 
offering long-tenor, low-interest financing from its balance sheet. The concessionality is 
expected to bring end-user loan rates down to approximately 4.2 to 6.0 per cent, below 
prevailing commercial offerings in the region, thereby enabling access for customer segments 
that would otherwise be excluded. 
20. 
Risks to Impact Delivery: There are several risks to impact realization. First, the 
financial profile of many potential end users, especially low-income households, raises the 
possibility of loan default despite the economic case for investment. While utilities are 
experienced in customer screening, repayment performance will need to be monitored 
carefully, particularly in the context of inflationary pressure and income volatility. The iTAP felt 
that the responses of the AE concerning the ability of the EEs to perform the role of credit 
underwriters were not entirely convincing. We note that low-income households ̵ those who 
would benefit most from energy efficiency and solar solutions ̵ may present greater credit or 
payment risks. Utilities are not structured like banks and generally do not have strong 
underwriting capabilities. This dynamic can lead to higher default rates and, ultimately, 
regulatory pushback. 
21. 
To mitigate the risk of defaults at higher rates than expected, or non-payment, the 
programme places emphasis on technologies that offer clear, near-term financial benefits to 
customers. This design choice is efficient, as it increases the likelihood that energy cost savings 
will offset repayment obligations from the outset. The use of on-bill financing also provides a 
degree of transparency by presenting both the savings and the repayment charges on the same 
utility bill, which may be particularly useful for customers with limited financial literacy. That 
said, it remains to be seen whether this level of transparency alone will be sufficient to ensure 
sustained repayment, particularly in lower-income segments. The technical assistance 
component includes customer outreach and awareness-raising activities, including the 
development calculators of energy savings, intended to reinforce repayment behaviour by 
illustrating the medium-term financial advantages of participation. These are helpful features, 
though their actual effectiveness in reducing credit risk will depend on the quality and reach of 
implementation. 
22. 
In addition, political changes could disrupt alignment between national designated 
authorities, regulators and utilities. Although upstream work by development partners (e.g. 
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit and the Caribbean Community) has 
helped build institutional readiness, the IUS Programme remains exposed to shifts in public 
sector commitment or regulatory support. This risk is partially mitigated by the programme 
design, which includes technical assistance to regulatory bodies and deep utility engagement. 
23. 
The proposal presents a robust mitigation case, a plausible adaptation narrative, and a 
credible enabling environment for long-term structural transformation in energy service 
delivery. While actual outcomes will depend on effective execution, customer uptake and 
institutional continuity, the programme design provides a sound foundation for achieving 
meaningful and scalable climate impact in the Caribbean SIDS context. 
24. 
The iTAP assesses the impact potential of the IUS Programme as medium.  
1.3 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
                             Scale: Medium     
25. 
The proposal offers a well-considered approach to addressing entrenched structural 
barriers in the Caribbean energy sector and exhibits several characteristics consistent with the 
GCF definition of paradigm shift. The IUS Programme is designed to catalyse systemic

=== PAGE 136 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 136 
 
 
 
 
transformation by altering the roles of utilities, consumers and regulators in how distributed 
energy services are delivered, financed and valued. 
26. 
The proposed IUS Programme aims to advance structural transformation in the energy 
sectors of Barbados, Belize and Jamaica by embedding distributed RE, energy efficiency and 
other demand-side technologies within utility operations. The central innovation of the 
programme lies in positioning regulated utilities as facilitators of customer-level DER 
deployment, blending financing, installation and service provision into a single utility-led 
offering. This shift from the traditional supplier model towards a more service-oriented role is 
both operationally novel and aligned with the broader decentralization of energy systems. 
27. 
The IUS Programme also intends to empower customers through simplified access to 
clean energy technologies and behavioural tools, enabling greater participation in the energy 
transition. By integrating on-bill financing and user-facing applications (e.g. energy savings 
calculators), the programme seeks to normalize investment in DERs among households and 
micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) that have historically faced high barriers 
to participation.  
28. 
In parallel, the IUS Programme includes a robust enabling environment component 
aimed at updating the legal, regulatory and administrative frameworks governing behind-the-
meter DERs. This includes support for permitting processes, energy accounting modernization, 
and the development of performance-based incentive structures. These reforms are critical for 
anchoring the model in long-term institutional practice and ensuring it can be sustained and 
replicated without ongoing external concessional support. 
29. 
The iTAP notes that while the programme’s absolute scale is relatively modest, both in 
terms of greenhouse gas abatement and financing volume, its design is intentionally structured 
to serve as a model for replication. A future Phase II is envisioned to extend the approach to 
other CDB borrowing member countries. Knowledge-sharing efforts, digital platforms, and 
regional institutional engagement (e.g. through the Caribbean Centre for Renewable Energy and 
Energy Efficiency) are embedded in the design to support regional uptake. 
30. 
However, while the IUS Programme is well-conceived from a distributed energy market 
development perspective, its paradigm-shifting potential must be assessed considering broader 
sector planning trends in the region, particularly in Barbados. Many SIDS, including the target 
countries, have articulated highly ambitious national RE targets. Barbados, for instance, has set 
a goal of reaching 100 per cent RE generation by 2030, and currently has over 500 megawatts of 
new RE capacity in various stages of development. This figure is more than double the country’s 
current installed capacity and over three times its peak demand of ~141 megawatts. 
31. 
In this context, there is a legitimate concern that a programme focused on customer-
level DERs, particularly rooftop PV installations and efficiency, may draw attention and capital 
away from utility-scale RE investments that, in theory, offer greater scale and grid-level impact. 
Moreover, there is a risk that the most financially viable customers – those with 
creditworthiness and high consumption – will exit the grid or reduce their net load via DERs, 
potentially undermining the financial case for centralized RE investments and straining utility 
revenue models. 
32. 
When asked about this, the AE acknowledged that many of the utility-scale RE plans 
articulated by national governments are aspirational and may not materialize due to permitting, 
financing and political constraints. In this light, the IUS Programme is presented as a more 
grounded pathway to climate action with greater ease of implementation. While this may be 
pragmatically valid, it nonetheless raises questions about coherence between distributed and 
centralized planning efforts, and whether the programme might inadvertently exacerbate 
systemic tensions within the power sector, particularly around grid stability, cost recovery and 
equitable allocation of subsidies and incentives. 
33. 
The IUS Programme does include design features intended to mitigate some of these 
risks, such as utility ownership of the DER deployment process and integration with national

=== PAGE 137 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 137 
 
 
 
 
regulatory bodies. However, these alone may not be sufficient to reconcile the divergence 
between bottom-up distributed energy approaches and top-down utility-scale targets.  
34. 
The long-term success of the IUS Programme will depend on how effectively it is 
integrated into broader sector planning, including decisions around tariff structures, capacity 
payments, and the sequencing of new utility-scale capacity additions. For instance, the iTAP 
notes that the distributed approach advanced here can address a distinct set of policy and 
infrastructure objectives that bolster paradigm shift, listed below. 
35. 
Resilience: Distributed systems provide redundancy in the face of climate-related 
disruptions and offer a more granular pathway to energy security, particularly in storm-prone 
regions. 
36. 
Inclusivity: Many Caribbean households and MSMEs have historically lacked access to 
financing for clean energy investments. The IUS model is specifically designed to address this 
gap, offering more equitable participation in the energy transition. 
37. 
Land-use efficiency: In land-constrained island contexts such as Barbados, where siting 
large-scale RE assets is increasingly difficult, rooftop deployment is not only desirable but 
necessary. 
38. 
Energy efficiency: The IUS Programme prioritizes not just RE but also efficient cooling 
and lighting – technologies that are typically underserved by both public procurement and 
private financial intermediaries. 
39. 
Local ownership: Unlike many utility-scale RE projects, which are typically led by 
foreign investors, the distributed model has the potential to foster greater domestic ownership 
of clean energy assets. 
40. 
Taken together, these attributes reinforce the programme’s contribution to broader 
systemic change in how energy is produced, delivered and consumed. However, the success of 
this transformation will depend, to a significant extent, on the capacity of the participating 
utilities to adapt to new roles and business models, the regulatory follow-through in each 
country, and the eventual cost-effectiveness of scaling the model. 
41. 
 For that reason, the iTAP assesses the paradigm shift potential of the IUS Programme as 
medium as it introduces a promising and replicable utility-led model for distributed energy 
deployment and has the potential to alter how utilities, customers and regulators approach 
clean energy investment. However, these gains must be weighed against unresolved tensions 
with national utility-scale RE ambitions, particularly in contexts like Barbados where DER 
proliferation may affect the bankability of planned large-scale projects. Absent clearer 
articulation of how the IUS model complements broader grid expansion strategies, its 
contribution to systemic transformation remains promising but not assured. 
1.4 
Sustainable development potential  
           Scale: Medium to high       
42. 
The funding proposal outlines a range of expected sustainable development co-benefits, 
many of which are plausible and well-integrated into the IUS Programme core design. These 
span environmental, social, economic and gender dimensions, and align with multiple 
Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), notably SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy) and SDG 13 
(climate action), as well as secondary contributions to SDG 3: good health and well-being; SDG 
5: gender equality; SDG 8: decent work and economic growth; SDG 9: industry, innovation and 
infrastructure; and SDG 17: partnerships for the goals. While some outcomes will depend 
heavily on effective implementation and uptake, the programme includes mechanisms that 
could support meaningful and measurable development co-benefits. 
43. 
Environmental co-benefits: The IUS Programme supports a shift away from fossil fuel-
based generation through the deployment of distributed RE and energy efficiency technologies. 
Unlike utility-scale RE installations, the proposed subprojects – rooftop PV, efficient cooling and 
lighting, and solar water heaters – are small-scale and embedded in existing structures,

=== PAGE 138 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 138 
 
 
 
 
minimizing risks of ecological disruption or land-use conflicts. This is particularly relevant in 
island contexts such as Barbados, where land-use constraints limit the feasibility of large-scale 
RE development. 
44. 
In addition to emissions reduction, the transition to cleaner distributed generation is 
expected to improve local air quality, particularly in urban areas reliant on diesel-based 
generation. These environmental benefits are reasonably substantiated and consistent with 
regional energy and health policy objectives. 
45. 
Economic co-benefits: The IUS Programme presents a credible pathway to delivering 
financial benefits to both utilities and consumers. On the customer side, the estimated net 
present value of savings across subproject lifetimes exceeds USD 230 million, according to the 
AE’s integrated financial model. These savings could be reinvested by households and MSMEs or 
used to offset consumption-related costs. Utilities may also benefit from the diversification of 
revenue streams and the opportunity to develop service-based business lines that may prove 
more resilient in the face of structural changes in the electricity market. 
46. 
At the broader macroeconomic level, the IUS Programme is expected to reduce fossil-
fuel imports, which remain a significant pressure on foreign exchange reserves across the three 
participating countries. Local job creation is another potential benefit, particularly through the 
installation, maintenance and support services associated with DER deployment. While exact 
employment figures are not specified, the focus on distributed solutions may support a more 
geographically dispersed and locally embedded labour market than utility-scale alternatives. 
47. 
Social co-benefits: The IUS Programme aims to improve household-level access to clean 
energy technologies through inclusive financing mechanisms, notably on-bill repayment. This 
feature is particularly relevant in the Caribbean, where access to commercial credit is limited 
and many potential users, especially low-income households and informal businesses, are 
effectively excluded from the clean energy transition. By anchoring repayment in existing utility 
relationships, the programme removes the need for traditional collateral or credit history, 
potentially widening participation. 
48. 
Health-related co-benefits: Albeit indirectly, health co-benefits are also likely from 
reduced diesel usage which may lower particulate matter and other pollutants, with attendant 
benefits for respiratory health. The technical assistance component also includes targeted 
support for low-income households in the form of free energy audits, which could enhance 
equity and ensure greater inclusion in benefit distribution. 
49. 
Gender equality and social inclusion: The proposal is supported by a detailed Gender 
Action Plan and gender assessment and includes gender-disaggregated indicators in its results 
framework. The IUS Programme commits to concrete actions across four areas: mainstreaming 
gender in policy and planning; tailoring outreach to women and other marginalized groups; 
developing inclusive IUS product offerings; and strengthening utility systems for sex-
disaggregated beneficiary tracking. 
50. 
These measures are designed to improve both participation and outcomes for women, 
youth, persons with disabilities, and other underserved populations. However, as with other co-
benefits, their impact will depend on the depth and consistency of implementation. The plan to 
monitor uptake among female-headed households and vulnerable groups is appropriate, though 
this data should be regularly reported and independently verified. 
51. 
The proposal integrates credible pathways to social inclusion, local economic benefits, 
and environmental health improvements, with appropriate attention to gender and equity 
considerations. The inclusion of customer-facing support tools and technical assistance for 
marginalized groups is a strength. While many co-benefits are contingent on implementation 
quality and uptake, the programme design creates a strong enabling framework for their 
realization.  
52. 
The iTAP assesses the sustainable development potential of the IUS Programme as 
medium to high.

=== PAGE 139 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 139 
 
 
 
 
1.5 
Needs of the recipient             
                                           Scale: High    
53. 
Barbados , Belize and Jamaica, the participating countries, face well-documented climate 
and development challenges that align with the GCF prioritization of SIDS and climate-
vulnerable populations. All three are highly exposed to climate risks such as extreme weather 
events, sea level rise, heat stress, and disruptions to key economic sectors like tourism and 
agriculture. At the same time, their energy sectors remain heavily reliant on imported fossil 
fuels, with limited local capacity to self-finance the transition to more resilient and sustainable 
systems. 
54. 
Despite being classified as middle-income countries, this status belies substantial 
underlying vulnerabilities. High debt burdens and restricted fiscal space limit public investment 
capacity, while shallow domestic capital markets constrain private sector financing of 
distributed energy technologies. The IUS Programme directly addresses these structural 
limitations by providing concessional financing and technical assistance that the national 
governments and utilities would not otherwise be able to mobilize at scale. This is particularly 
relevant considering the countries’ stated commitments under their nationally determined 
contributions, which include ambitious emissions reduction and energy access goals but often 
lack credible, funded implementation pathways. 
55. 
Importantly, the IUS Programme targets persistent equity gaps within these countries. 
While DER adoption has accelerated among higher-income customers, lower-income 
households and informal MSMEs, which are often those most exposed to climate and economic 
shocks, have been largely excluded from participation in the energy transition. The 
programme’s inclusive on-bill financing model, combined with free energy audits for lower-
income customers, offers a pragmatic response to these access barriers. By leveraging the 
existing utility-customer relationship, the model bypasses traditional credit-based lending 
channels that have historically disadvantaged marginalized populations. 
56. 
The IUS Programme also integrates a social inclusion and gender framework. Through 
the Gender Action Plan and associated training and outreach, its aim is to improve governance, 
participation and responsiveness among energy sector institutions – including utilities – 
towards groups that have been traditionally underserved, such as women, persons with 
disabilities, LGBTQIA+ communities, and Indigenous Peoples. While the implementation of 
these measures will require close monitoring, the aim  to position these groups as active 
participants and not just passive recipients is well-aligned with the GCF emphasis on a just and 
inclusive transition. 
57. 
The iTAP notes that the geographic targeting is limited to three countries in this first 
phase, and its reach in absolute terms remains modest relative to national populations. 
However, the selection of countries is appropriate given both their acute vulnerability and the 
potential for institutional readiness and scale-up through a planned Phase II. The programme 
design reflects an awareness of the constraints faced by the governments and utilities in 
launching new delivery models without external support, particularly in a high-risk, resource-
limited context. 
58. 
The programme responds directly to structural vulnerabilities in climate exposure, fiscal 
capacity and financial access, particularly for low-income and marginalized groups. While 
limited in geographic scope and initial coverage, the model is appropriately targeted and well-
aligned with GCF priorities for SIDS and inclusive development. The success of the programme 
will depend in part on its ability to meaningfully reach underserved populations and support 
institutional capacity-building that persists beyond the life of the programme. 
59. 
The iTAP assesses the needs of the recipient as high. 
1.6 
Country ownership 
  
 
                                           Scale: High

=== PAGE 140 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 140 
 
 
 
 
60. 
The IUS Programme demonstrates strong alignment with the national climate and 
energy priorities of the three participating countries – Barbados, Belize and Jamaica – all of 
which have issued no-objection letters. The objectives and design of the programme are 
consistent with the countries’ nationally determined contributions, as well as sectoral strategies 
and policy frameworks, including the National Energy Policy (2019–2030) of Barbados, the 
Sustainable Energy Strategy and Action Plan (2014–2033) of Belize and the Vision 2030 
Development Plan of Jamaica. The focus on scaling up distributed RE, energy efficiency and 
DERs corresponds with each country’s stated intention to reduce emissions and enhance energy 
resilience. 
61. 
The proposal reflects broad-based engagement with national stakeholders. Regulators, 
energy ministries, national designated authorities, and utilities were closely involved in 
programme preparation. Previous upstream work, supported by partners such as Deutsche 
Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit and the Caribbean Community Secretariat, has 
also contributed to country-level readiness for programme implementation. These engagements 
signal meaningful institutional support and suggest that the programme has been shaped by 
local priorities and context. The regional scope is further strengthened by engagement with 
organizations such as the Caribbean Centre for Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency and the 
Caribbean Electric Utility Services Corporation, which are well positioned to support knowledge 
exchange and promote replication. 
62. 
The iTAP also notes positively that all three utilities proposed as EEs have prior 
experience with DER deployment and have undergone a capacity assessment to confirm their 
suitability. Under the programme, the utilities will implement billing reforms, oversee 
subproject execution, and support customer engagement, with targeted technical assistance to 
address identified institutional capacity gaps. 
63. 
That said, the iTAP has some reservations regarding the level and nature of the national 
co-financing contribution. The proposal identifies an in-kind contribution of USD 25 million 
from the utilities, in the form of investments in advanced metering infrastructure (AMI). While 
AMI can support grid modernization and enable more granular DER integration over time, it is 
not directly linked to the financed subprojects nor to the programme’s estimated climate 
impact. As such, there is a legitimate question as to whether this investment constitutes true co-
financing or rather reflects a continuation of business-as-usual capital expenditure. The IUS 
Programme documents assert that the AMI deployment is complementary to the IUS objectives 
and forms part of the broader enabling ecosystem. However, from a concessionality standpoint, 
the iTAP typically looks for more direct financial commitment, ideally with measurable risk-
sharing or matching funds, when substantial concessional financing is requested. 
64. 
While this does not undermine the overall alignment with country priorities, it does 
point to an opportunity for deeper national financial participation in future phases of the 
programme. In particular, there may be scope for governments or utilities to take a more active 
financial role as the IUS model matures, and implementation risks decrease. 
65. 
The iTAP assesses country ownership as high, reflecting strong policy alignment, 
inclusive stakeholder engagement and the active involvement of national institutions in 
programme design and execution.  
1.7 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
          
           Scale: Medium to high         
66. 
The IUS Programme presents a reasonably strong case in terms of cost-efficiency, 
alignment of financial instruments, and structuring of concessionality. This is particularly 
notable given the geographic and structural constraints associated with SIDS, where distributed 
climate mitigation and adaptation solutions often entail higher marginal costs than in 
continental or higher-density settings. 
67. 
Cost-effectiveness metrics: The programme is expected to achieve mitigation benefits of 
approximately 601,550 t CO₂ eq avoided, resulting in an estimated cost of USD 70 per tonne for

=== PAGE 141 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 141 
 
 
 
 
the overall programme and USD 43 per tonne for the GCF contribution. These figures are 
broadly consistent with GCF precedents for distributed energy interventions in SIDS contexts 
and are deemed competitive when compared to mitigation programmes with similar technical 
profiles. 
68. 
On the adaptation side, the estimated cost per direct beneficiary is approximately USD 
3,938, based on the 10,655 direct beneficiaries targeted through improved resilience to heat 
stress. While this is relatively high by GCF adaptation standards, it must be understood within 
the broader context of the dual objectives of the programme. The costs are associated not only 
with direct technology uptake but also with the establishment of institutional models, digital 
tools and customer-facing systems expected to deliver benefits beyond the initial 
implementation window. 
69. 
Importantly, the programme also estimates over USD 260 million in financial savings for 
participating customers over the productive lifespan of the financed technologies. These net 
benefits are expected to accrue through avoided electricity costs from distributed solar energy 
and savings from energy-efficient appliances and systems. This represents a meaningful value 
proposition for both customers and the utilities, although, as CDB acknowledges, actual results 
will depend on technology performance, repayment behaviour and subproject uptake. 
70. 
Concessionality structure and co-financing: The financial structure involves a USD 20.8 
million GCF concessional loan blended with USD 16.2 million from CDB on a pari passu basis. 
Participating utilities will on-lend these funds to end users through structured credit lines, with 
interest rates anticipated to fall in the range of 4.5 to 6.0 per cent, depending on local regulatory 
determinations and risk-sharing discussions during the first year of the programme. These 
terms compare favourably to prevailing market rates in the region, which typically begin at 6.5 
per cent or higher for comparable unsecured loans. 
71. 
In-kind contributions of USD 25 million from the utilities in the form of AMI are also 
included in the co-financing total. While CDB maintains that AMI is a critical enabler of the IUS 
model and aligned with the programme’s theory of change, the iTAP notes that this investment 
may have been pursued independently of the IUS Programme and appears to reflect business-
as-usual utility modernization. As such, its inclusion as co-financing, while defensible, may 
overstate the degree of additionality and direct financial commitment from national 
stakeholders. 
72. 
The IUS Programme is nonetheless likely to catalyse further investment downstream. It 
is framed explicitly as a “bridge to bankability”, designed to establish proof of concept and 
performance track record for utility-led DER delivery, thereby enabling the utilities to secure 
additional  finance with less concessionality in future cycles. This ambition is credible, 
particularly given the integrated capacity-building support and the regional focus on 
replication, but it remains contingent on execution quality and early success. 
73. 
Risk allocation and incentive alignment: CDB will act as both lender and programme 
manager, receiving lending returns while benefiting from substantial GCF-funded technical 
assistance, which comprises over 13.5 per cent of the total GCF contribution. While this 
structure may appear to raise moral hazard risks, whereby CDB captures upside returns while 
GCF bears a disproportionate share of de-risking, the AE has provided a clear rationale. It argues 
that the technical assistance is essential to establish a novel delivery model in the Caribbean, 
where such utility-led energy service offerings have not previously been implemented. 
Furthermore, grant-financed activities include compliance with GCF safeguards, social inclusion 
outreach and programmatic knowledge dissemination, all of which extend beyond direct 
support to the utilities. 
74. 
That said, the iTAP would have preferred to see a more differentiated risk structure, 
such as a subordinated tranche, wherein CDB exposure would be more explicitly aligned with 
performance outcomes. The current pari passu structure may be acceptable, but the rationale 
rests heavily on the need for both financiers to maintain equivalent tenors and exit points. 
While CDB emphasizes that its own lending is not commercially priced and is adjusted to reflect

=== PAGE 142 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 142 
 
 
 
 
its borrowing costs, the absence of a more risk-sensitive capital stack reduces the strength of 
the incentive alignment framework. 
75. 
To mitigate the risk of utilities over-capturing the benefits of concessional financing, 
CDB has indicated that spreads charged to end users will be capped in the subsidiary 
agreements signed with each utility. Regulatory authorities will also play a role in rate-setting 
during the programme’s first year, with CDB monitoring subloan terms through regular 
reporting. These are positive design features, but enforcement over time will require sustained 
regulatory engagement and transparent monitoring of on-lending terms and customer-level 
impact. 
76. 
Customer uptake and delivery risk: A key determinant of programme success will be the 
ability to generate sufficient customer uptake. In other IUS contexts, customers have rejected 
offers when savings from installed technologies did not clearly outweigh the costs of loan 
repayments, or when participation processes were too complex. The programme attempts to 
address these challenges by providing: 
(a) 
On-bill financing mechanisms that combine costs and benefits in a single, transparent 
billing statement; 
(b) 
User-facing tools, including energy savings calculators and online portals, to demystify 
technology performance and financial implications; 
(c) 
Technical assistance for low-income customers, including free energy audits and help 
desk support; and 
(d) 
Prequalification processes and performance standards for Technology Service Providers 
(TSPs) to ensure service quality. 
77. 
While these are thoughtful features, the multi-stakeholder structure and regulatory 
dependencies introduce a degree of complexity that may affect implementation. Customer 
satisfaction and referral effects will be critical to achieving scale, particularly given the 
relatively modest direct beneficiary target (10,655 people) in the initial phase. The programme 
includes provisions to delay or suspend further technical assistance disbursements if uptake 
stalls – an important accountability mechanism that improves delivery discipline. 
78. 
Coordination with other initiatives: The iTAP notes that the IUS Programme does not 
appear to be meaningfully coordinated with other major climate finance initiatives in the region, 
including the GCF-supported Barbados Blue Green Bank . While CDB maintains that these 
initiatives are complementary and focus on more traditional, bank-led financing models, it 
would have been helpful to include more detail on how pipeline sharing will be managed to 
avoid duplication or market distortion, especially in relatively small economies like Barbados. 
79. 
Accredited entity fees: The AE fee of 6.7 per cent is within the range of comparable 
programmes, though slightly higher than the iTAP typically expects for an intervention with a 
large technical assistance component. CDB justifies the fee through a bottom-up costing model 
and points to higher personnel and travel costs in the Caribbean. While these factors are valid, 
the iTAP notes that the programme’s relatively high cost of coordination should be monitored 
and assessed alongside actual disbursement and performance metrics over time. 
80. 
The design reflects a strong understanding of regional delivery constraints and 
incorporates several safeguards to promote cost-effectiveness, manage concessionality and 
enforce performance-based disbursement. However, questions remain around the depth of 
national co-financing, the absence of differentiated risk-sharing between lenders, and the need 
for clearer coordination with parallel financing mechanisms. The programme is well-structured 
to catalyse downstream investments and shift market norms, but sustained execution and 
oversight will be critical to realizing its full potential. 
81. 
The one area we would have preferred to see more ambition involves the feasibility of 
introducing a more risk-sensitive capital structure in the funding proposal, such as a 
subordinated tranche for CDB financing. This would enhance incentive alignment by ensuring 
that CDB retains greater exposure to performance outcomes. If the pari passu structure is

=== PAGE 143 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.17 
Page 143 
 
 
 
 
retained, we would encourage the Secretariat should seek a more robust justification, including 
an assessment of whether alternative structuring options could better achieve alignment while 
preserving the IUS Programme financial viability and implementation timeline. 
82. 
The iTAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness potential of the IUS Programme as 
medium to high. 
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
83. 
The funding proposal makes a solid attempt to address a significant climate finance 
need in the Caribbean. The iTAP finds that the IUS Programme presents credible climate 
rationale, addresses structural financing gaps and demonstrates potential for long-term 
paradigm shift.  
84. 
The iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

=== PAGE 151 ===
1 
Introduction 
 
The Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) is in the process of implementing the “Scaling up the 
Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the 
Caribbean” (Phase I) Programme in the Caribbean, which is a Green Climate Fund (GCF) 
funded activity. This Phase of the project will have three Borrowing Member Countries (BMCs) 
participating in the programme and they are as follows: Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. These 
three BMCs have pledged to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions through the adoption of 
renewable energy (RE) to facilitate energy transition and expanding the scale of energy 
efficiency (EE) measures. 
The GCF is committed to maximising the impact of its funding for adaptation and mitigation while 
ensuring environmental, social, economic and development co-benefits are advanced and a 
gender-sensitive approach is taken1. With its updated 2020 gender policy, the GCF is committed 
to promote and integrate gender equality in its investment criteria. The Gender Policy has three 
main objectives which are as follows:  
1. Utilise a comprehensive gender approach applied by the institution and its network of 
partners which include accredited entities (AEs), national designated authorities (NDAs) 
and focal points to support climate change interventions and innovations. 
2. Promote climate investments that advance gender equality and minimise social, gender 
and climate related risks through climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.  
3. Contribute to shrinking the gender gap caused by climate change-exacerbated social, 
economic and environmental vulnerabilities through mainstreaming gender equality in 
climate investments.    
The gender policy’s application to the IUS Project would entail: 
 
Gender inequality is addressed/reduced and through enhanced stakeholder engagement 
can generate sustainable livelihoods opportunities, promote health and wellbeing, and 
increase resilience to climate-induced shocks and risks for men and women.  
 
An enabling environment among GCF stakeholders built on existing policy commitments, 
social and environmental standards and redress mechanisms is supported and sustained in 
order to achieve gender equality. 
 
The comprehensive, systematic, and participatory collection of sex-disaggregated data and 
the execution of qualitative and quantitative gender analysis is supported to effectively 
monitor, report and evaluate project impact.  
 
1 “Gender Policy 2020-2023” retrieved from https://www.greenclimate.fund/document/gender-policy  
In accordance with GCF gender policies and guidance, the gender assessment is done as a critical 
element of the project proposal to outline how the project while attempting to meet its goal of 
supporting the target countries’ transition to renewable energy states, will benefit vulnerable groups 
and how in particular will vulnerable groups be able to participate in this project. The gender 
assessment will elucidate on factors that will impair vulnerable groups’ ability to benefit fully and 
identify areas of entry to address these issues while advancing gender equality and supporting a 
Just Transition.

=== PAGE 152 ===
The Assessment builds on the previous work in the baseline assessment and seeks to provide 
a deeper understanding of how the proposed technologies (solar water heaters, rooftop solar 
photovoltaic, efficient cooling and HVAC, and LED lighting) will impact populations of the target 
countries as well as identify barriers to uptake.  
The Assessment is expected to inform: 
 
the gender mainstreaming approach for the GCF proposal; 
 
capacity-building activities in the overall proposal; 
 
gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation tools that may be needed; and 
 
Institutional policy, programming and operational actions/steps that may be needed in the 
short, medium and or long-term to support the IUS model implementation in an equal, 
inclusive and just manner.  
1.1 
Limitations 
This assessment utilised a mixed method approach i.e. a desk-based research and stakeholder 
engagement. However, this process was still hindered by a few factors including:  
 
Outdated data – the region has limited human resources funding and infrastructure available 
to execute frequent data collection and analysis activities which results in the use of data at 
least 5 years old in this assessment.  Despite the recent efforts of Caribbean Centre for 
Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency (CCREEE) to narrow this gap, some of the 
essential questions for the gender assessment still are not generally covered or readily 
available.  
 
Data availability – access to data is a weak point in the Caribbean as many governments 
and agencies possess updated data but have issues with processing, analysing, and 
publishing said data for public access, also due to limited human resources and 
infrastructure. Due to demand also for consultations and engagement, there was limited time 
to follow up in detail with the Statistics Departments of each country to inform this draft 
assessment.  
 
Limited access to critical documents – throughout various engagements with government 
stakeholders, we became aware of policies in development that could affect and possibly 
inform this project but despite our best efforts, we were unable to secure access to these 
documents (drafts included).  This includes the draft Energy Policy for Jamaica as well as 
the new access policy for Belize, amongst others.  
 
Inaccessibility of key stakeholders – some of the Executing Entities (the utilities) did not 
participate in the stakeholder engagement process and in some cases cited other priorities 
or the need to see a project document prior to their engagement on questions relating to 
gender and social issues. The latter suggests a lack of understanding of the requirements 
and the process flow for GCF proposals.  Their engagement, as the proposed executing 
agencies, is critical to obtaining information that would inform aspects of the gender action 
plan that follows the Gender Assessment including technical assistance and capacity- 
building actions as possible elements of more than one Component of the proposed project. 
 
As it stands therefore, the Gender Assessment reflects a few key limits and limitations which are 
also of value to the Assessment itself in terms of indirectly assessing knowledge, practice and 
behaviours on gender, gender mainstreaming, women’s empowerment and social inclusion in 
the sector as well as for this specific project.  
1.2 
Challenges  
This assessment was meant to be informed by desk-based research and stakeholder 
engagement which would give insight into the enabling environment in each country by

=== PAGE 153 ===
identifying the current circumstances the IUS would be operating in while also gaining knowledge 
on key stakeholders’ capacities and practices on gender equality and social inclusion 
mainstreaming in energy sector activities and transformations. Executing the stakeholder 
engagement aspect of the process within the given tight timeframe was greatly hindered by 
various factors. Engaging with key stakeholders was inhibited by time conflicts, limited personnel 
availability and the clearance of engagements of key government stakeholders. Due to the 
difficulty in scheduling meetings and the limited time available to continue consultations and 
complete the assessment, stakeholder engagement was closed off on June 9th to facilitate the 
preparation of this Draft Report.

=== PAGE 154 ===
2 
Methodology 
 
This assessment used a two-step approach. The first step entailed the desk-based research 
which involved gathering existing analysis on energy, renewable energy, energy and gender, 
and renewable energy transformations to obtain information that could shed light on how this 
project may impact persons, identify any possible lessons learned in previous works and 
analysing the existing enabling environment for renewable energy. The research applied a 
renewable energy lens to the current socio-economic conditions within each country to 
understand how varying factors affect renewable energy usage, uptake and appetite for 
renewable energy technologies.   
A gender analysis rating tool was used to assess relevant policies based on how gender-
responsive they are (see Figure 2-1). This diagnosis involved reviewing policies and identifying 
the elements that speak to gender, women, and other marginalised groups and the 
commitments/intent made by those polices on issues of (i) engagement, (ii) equity and justice, 
(iii) inclusion, (iv) protection/mitigation of risks/impacts. This is an important perspective in 
addressing issues of a just transition and in meeting GCF requirements for mitigation 
investments including those with adaptation co-benefits.  
Figure 2-1: Policy Gender Rating Tool 
Gender Rating  
Description  
Gender Neutral (no mention) 
The policy makes no mention or does not reference gender or 
women.  
Gender Aware  
Gender specific norms, roles and issues are acknowledged 
within the policy.  
Gender Sensitive  
Measures are outlined within the policy to ensure gender 
inequalities are not exacerbated as a by-product of the policy’s 
actions.  
Gender Responsive  
The policy acknowledges and identifies differences and 
inequalities between men and women and sets out actions which 
seeks to rectify these issues.  
Gender Transformative  
The policy seeks to resolve fundamental structural inequalities 
and challenges social and cultural norms.   
 
The second step builds on the first step and used those findings to shape the stakeholder 
engagement to follow. Key stakeholders were identified, and a stakeholder engagement plan 
was developed (see Annex A). The findings were used to develop PowerPoint presentations to 
be used with stakeholders that help guide the conversation by providing context and key 
questions for stakeholders to answer (see presentations here). These questions were tailored 
for engagement with non-governmental organisations (NGOs) as well as government 
stakeholders.  Similar presentations were prepared for engagement with utilities. Engagement 
with diverse stakeholders sought to gain an understanding of their engagement on gender and 
Key chapter takeaway  
A diverse range of stakeholders were met, yielding responses from 50/50 split of male and female 
voices. The insights and information gained from these engagements specifically information 
centered around engagement and processes observed and performed by key stakeholders. 
Stakeholder engagement also captures the lived experiences of the stakeholder and raised 
awareness on ongoing work that was not available to the public.

=== PAGE 155 ===
energy and with energy activities, their capacities and experience with gender and gender 
mainstreaming particularly within a renewable energy project and their perspectives on issues 
that need consideration in the proposed IUS model approach. In addition to this, engagement 
with government stakeholders helped to identify existing, past or possible approaches and 
policies used when responding to the needs of the general public and vulnerable groups.   
Executed in three phases, we consulted with twenty-eight organisations in total (13 NGOs and 
09 governmental organisations) across the three countries (see Annex B for the list of 
stakeholders engaged). Phase 1 entailed engaging NGOs who worked with vulnerable groups 
and/or in renewable energy. In Phase 2, outreach was made to government stakeholders 
including energy ministries/agencies, public utilities commissions and gender bureaux. Phase 3 
was intended to target the utilities (the Executing Entities of the project) but was ultimately 
postponed due to a lack of response.  Twenty-eight (44) persons, in total, were consulted – 14 
men and 14 women (see Figure 2-2).

Stakeholder Engagement Focus  
From the desk-based research, issues of finance and access to finance were identified as 
barriers to this project, and the engagement sought to explore additional factors that might limit 
a person’s access to finance that may not have been covered in the desk-based 
review/research? (see Figure 2-3). Obtaining information would help to inform possible financial 
strategies that the project may need to adopt to ensure access to those who need financial 
assistance.

Key to ensuring the project meets its GCF gender requirements was to identify the existing 
capacities of those who will be charged in delivering and supporting this project and their

partners to support gaps. This insight will be used to determine whether there will be a need for 
gender sensitisation and capacity-building in the gender action plan to empower key project

made on their behalf. Acknowledging this and unique circumstances of PWDs, it was critical to

=== PAGE 159 ===
3 
Country Level Analysis  
 
This section explores the gendered usage and implications of the technologies of focus in this 
project across the three countries and identifies existing and potential barriers that would affect 
the uptake and adoption by these technologies by women and other marginalised communities. 
The assessment will explore the current state of usage of LED lighting, solar rooftop photovoltaic 
(PV), efficient air conditioning (A/C) and refrigeration differentiated by gender and any specific 
gender-differentiated needs. Noting from the project’s TOR that more equitable access to these 
technologies is a goal of this project, the assessment considers/explores factors that may impact 
this outcome.   
In so doing, it considers the following questions:   
 
Are the usage and uptake of these technologies differentiated between men and women? If 
there is a difference, is it related to gender roles?  
 
In what ways do they affect the daily activities of women, and can they bring increased quality 
of life?  
 
Will the increased distribution and adoption of these technologies support a just transition for 
all? 
 
What institutional, social and economic barriers may influence the rate of uptake of these 
technologies by women and other marginalised communities? Will these factors affect the 
benefits women and other marginalised communities will be able to receive from this project? 
Differences based on sex and age are considered as well as other intersectional attributes such 
as income, geography, (dis)ability and ethnicity where relevant. IP issues are expected to be 
considered there in the context of a vulnerable group in the general population rather than the 
specific needs for indigenous and hinterland communities as it may apply in Jamaica and or 
Belize.  
This section also reviews the enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in the energy 
sector and existing efforts to apply a gender lens to renewable energy and energy efficiency 
transformations. The review of existing legal, institutional, policy, strategy and planning 
frameworks that are summarised individually and collectively exposes any major gender gaps, 
barriers and constraints. Additionally, relevant international commitments and regional policies 
are mentioned.  Furthermore, comparisons between countries, key issues as well as common 
and differentiated needs are summarised including any evidence or likelihood of a gender 
inequality of risk that target beneficiaries may be exposed to.  
 
Key chapter takeaway  
Although, the three countries have their own individual cultures, economies and other aspects of 
society, the renewable energy sector in each country and their populations’ interactions with the 
sector are affected by similar factors and barriers. The adoption of the designated renewable energy 
technologies will reap numerous benefits for persons, though barriers that reduce their ability to 
implement do exist.

=== PAGE 160 ===
3.1 
Barbados 
3.1.1 
Policy Environment 
Barbados is a Caribbean leader in the energy sector. Its leadership has seen progress in the 
sustainable transformation of the energy matrix in its ambitious goals of being 100% renewable 
by 2030. This goal has resulted in the acquisition of 49 electric buses and the installation of 
rooftop solar photovoltaic systems across various government buildings.  In achieving its goal to 
be 100% renewable, the Government of Barbados has enacted legislation and policies to 
improve efficiency with LED Lighting and harness the sun’s energy ultimately reducing the need 
for fossil fuel-generated electricity. 
One programme being implemented is the Roof to Reefs project as noted in the country’s 
Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC). The project accounts for the use of IUS Model 
technologies such as rooftop solar photovoltaic on homes and hopes to improve the social 
development of vulnerable communities through resilience-building, post-disaster cost 
deduction and improving the standard of living and quality of life2. Though this programme 
promotes equity and addresses resilience concerns for vulnerable groups such as women and 
girls, several other policies do not enhance equity and a just transition and transformation of the 
energy sector under the IUS Model. This is a result of policies being gender neutral, or simply 
gender aware, with no mention or reference to gender or women or the acknowledgement of 
gender-specific norms (See Figure 3-1).  
Take, for example, the Barbados National Energy Policy which establishes the roadmap for the 
renewable energy transformation in the country. This policy has no specific mentions of gender 
equality, or how the different uses of energy by women would be considered and or have 
influenced within the roadmap. Figure 3-1 highlights relevant legislation and policies to the 
landscape of Energy and Gender within Barbados, and the gender content and rating of each 
policy.

Of the 9 policies assessed, 6 are gender-blind, 1 gender aware and 1, the Barbados NDC, 
gender responsive. Those most directly relevant to the country’s energy sector were all found to 
be gender blind. The NDC, subject to international requirements for gender mainstreaming, 
includes greater provisions for gender than the other policies reviewed. 
As noted in the 2021 NDC for Barbados, there is a commitment by the government towards 
“equality for all, especially as it relates to gender”, as recognised in Barbados’ Constitution. The 
NDC notes this commitment through emphasis on facilitating and supporting female 
entrepreneurship, business development to address unemployment and economic recovery, 
post-pandemic.7 In terms of a more direct focus on the energy sector in Barbados, the NDC 
references gender within the context of the Roofs-to-Reefs Program (R2RP), with a view to the 
positive social and gendered impacts from rooftop solar PV installation programmes. Aiming to 
provide support to low- and medium-income households to become more resilient to climate 
change related events, the programme seeks to protect both private and community roofs from 
the impacts of extreme weather and promotes renewable energy use, including solar, as well as 
rainwater harvesting, and nature-based solutions aimed at reducing runoff into coastal 
environments.8  
 
6 This action of this policy was made possible through the 2019-2020 Electric and Power Act (Amendment) 
7 “Barbados 2021 Update of the First Nationally Determined Contribution” retrieved from

=== PAGE 163 ===
Beyond overarching national and international policies and agreements, a recently implemented 
policy, at the Ministry level through the Ministry of Energy and Business, is the ‘Right to 
Renewable Energy Policy’. This policy gives householders, more generally those intending to 
use domestic renewable energy systems, the right to own and install renewable energy systems 
(up to a total of 10kW) without requiring a formal application and license under the Electric Light 
and Power Act (ELPA). The purpose of the policy is primarily timesaving, with a simplified and 
assured process for individuals wishing to use this service. The act makes no notable mention 
of female-headed households or provisions for women using the service, however, the direct 
link toward time saving and improved renewable energy systems can indirectly positively benefit 
women in terms of household tasks, time allocation and potential cost savings depending on the 
return from investment in adopting renewable energy grid technologies.  
3.1.2 
Usage 
Patterns and Trends  
Culturally, women carry out three times (3x) as much unpaid care and domestic work as men, 
including household chores and cooking; both unpaid and invisible9. These culturally defined 
responsibilities are closely tied to energy and energy access. Energy access saves time and 
allows for some of these tasks to be carried out simultaneously and with planning and 
preparation while still ensuring safety. Moreover, through energy access, technologies employed 
by many women reduce the physical burden that may be otherwise required for these tasks. 
When electricity is not available either due to a blackout or to affordability challenges these daily 
required activities are more likely to become a burden to women.10  
Men, on the other hand, carry out protective as well as economic and decision-making roles 
within the household. These are linked to gender stereotypes and norms which dictate that a 
man should: (i) not do unpaid domestic work, (ii) have the final say in household decisions, (iii) 
control household assets, and (iv) protect and exercise guardship of women in the household.11 
In the Barbadian context, often times these classical gendered roles are mixed with those of 
women (cooking and cleaning).Such traditional norms, provide only a shared level of control 
over the acquisition of energy resources and energy access, as equal acquisition of assets is 
noted with the country scoring 100 in the WBL Index 2023.12 
LED Lighting  
Culturally defined responsibilities (CDRs) such as caretaking, may lead to an increased use of 
LED Lighting in matrifocal households. LED lighting enables women to perform these CDRs 
alongside their economic responsibilities, such as employment), often taking up these CDRs 
during times of inactive work (before or after work, during periods of no sunlight e.g. early 
morning or late evening)13 or even during remote or hybrid work arrangements.  
As seen in Figure 3-2, the increase in consumer price inflation exceeds the amount of household 
disposable income real growth in developing countries like Barbados.

This translates into an increase in the need for employment as a means of (additional) income 
to meet basic needs (food, clothing, shelter, essential services, access to drinking water, 
sanitation, education, health care facilities and public transport).14 The increased demand for 
employment can lead to the increase in non-traditional working hours (outside of 8 AM – 5 PM) 
which would, in turn, increase the demand for LED Lighting for safety and security as women 
(and others) journey to and from work or school, whether using public transportation, or private 
vehicles.  
As women generally feel less safe in a public environment than most cis-heterosexual men 
(especially when it is dark outside), women often adjust their schedule, considering ways of 
transport and daily routes with safety in mind.15 There is a need for better lighting on Barbadian 
roads, on which 24, 250 existing high-pressure sodium (HPS) streetlights have been replaced 
with LED streetlights in a partnership with the Government of Barbados and the Barbados Light 
and Power Limited.16 Additionally, the Government’s acquisition of electric buses, can not only 
improve access to public transportation at non-traditional times of work but provide a 
comfortable, monitored and Wi-Fi-accessible place for women and members of the disabled 
community to traverse during these non-traditional working hours.17 
Men share some similarities in LED Lighting usage when compared to women18.  The 2010 
Census notes that there are 41,335 male-headed households, with 48, 036 children dependents. 
Similar safety concerns are a factor in the provision of LED Lighting, whilst journeying after 
daylight hours. Road safety is a possible area of need for the usage of LED Lighting by men, 
with the island reporting an upward trend in road fatalities (before the COVID-19 pandemic) of

which men are often the victims. However, the use of LED Lightning is far more expanded than 
just the use of road fatalities.  
Solar Water Heating 
The island is commonly applauded for its use of solar water heating in households and 
businesses, with over 50,000 solar water heaters (SWH).19 SWH technology combines the use 
of energy, and water in a highly water-scarce country to provide heated water used within 
households for bathing, cleaning, cooking, and washing. Outside of the benefits hot water has 
for CDRs such as cooking, cleaning and caretaking such as easy removal of stubborn stains 
such as dirt and grease and aid in the provision of safe and nutritious meals for the household, 
the use of SWH improves the sanitary hygiene of women.20  
The ageing population (60+) in Barbados amounts to 18% of the population, according to the 
2010 Census. This group within the population remains vulnerable to illnesses such as 
diabetes21 and rheumatoid arthritis.22 Consequently, warm water baths generate added health 
benefits for the island’s seniors giving them the added ability to combat low-grade non-chronic 
inflammation, reduce blood sugar, better circulation and soothing of the muscles and joints.23 
better circulation and soothing of the muscles and joints.  Particularly, as it relates to blood sugar, 
Hoekstra et al noted within their study that, observations of a lowering in fasting glycosylated 
hemoglobin and blood glucose concentrations following hot water immersion (HWI) in individuals 
with type 2 diabetes support the notion of improved insulin sensitivity following HWI (33), the 
mechanisms that underlie this beneficial effect are currently unclear. better circulation and 
soothing of the muscles and joints.24 Particularly, as it relates to blood sugar, Hoekstra et al 
noted within their study that, observations of a lowering in fasting glycosylated hemoglobin and 
blood glucose concentrations following HWI in individuals with type 2 diabetes support the notion 
of improved insulin sensitivity following HWI (33), the mechanisms that underlie this beneficial 
effect are currently unclear.25   
  These benefits can allow the elderly who have fixed incomes to continue to support positive 
health practices at a reduced cost (as the island benefits from yearly sunshine). These savings, 
in turn, allow for further investments in their diet (particularly, healthy food consumption) which 
can be costly to these individuals as the region experiences the highest cost of a healthy diet 
when compared to other world regions.26 
Rooftop Solar Photovoltaic  
Rooftop solar photovoltaics remain niche across genders, because of the economic barriers for 
a rooftop solar photovoltaic system (See Economic Barrier Section) as well as physical barriers 
due to rooftop types. With no specific data on how rooftop solar photovoltaic may be used 
differently across vulnerable groups within the country, it is generally presented as a cost-saving 
benefit to local households across the island.

The installation of a rooftop solar PV system provides collective savings to Barbadian users, 
over USD 2.5 million a year on their electricity bills (equivalent to 5,000 barrels of oil, or over 
2,000 tonnes of CO2 emissions).27 As women reinvest 90% of their income in their families and 
communities, this extra income can aid in the reduction of poverty locally28 and expand the scope 
of investment in other forms and more complex RE, EE and DER technologies. 
Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration 
The island has two weather seasons due to its geographic location. These include the dry 
season from December to May and the wet season from June to November. Increasing rates of 
climate change have caused intensified storms and extreme heat during dry periods29, but the 
use of cooling remains mostly equal between men, women, and the elderly population. During 
these high dry periods, temperatures can be high temperatures with the Barbados Met Office 
predicting temperatures of 34 to 35 degrees Celsius in September 2023.30 his extreme heat 
causes an increased need for hydration and cooling.  
Figure 3-3: Estimate Annual Energy Consumption of Room Air Conditioners in 2040. Source: 
United for Efficiency31 
 
Under three scenarios, the UNEP United for Efficiency programme estimates that room air 
conditioners will consume 600 GWh with business as usual, 472 GWh with minimum ambition 
and 359 GWh with high ambition.32 Business as usual considers that the energy efficiency of the 
technology improves at 1% per year, further increasing market share of variable speed models 
is assumed to improve overall efficiency at an additional 2% per year until new sales are entirely 
from that product type (see Figure 3-3).33

The usage of technologies such as efficient air conditioning and refrigeration can aid in women’s 
cooking responsibilities and within their natural ageing journey including menopause. The use 
of an efficient refrigerator, for example, allows women to better balance their CDRs and job 
responsibilities by facilitating meal preparation in advance and reducing the time burden of these 
activities.  At home, efficient air conditioning and cooling also stand to benefit 28 to 63% of 
middle-aged women who are affected by sleep disturbances such as hot flashes and night 
sweats.34 This technology allows women to get a good night’s sleep at home, which is key to 
physical and mental health. Within the workforce, air conditioning can aid in the management of 
job tasks allowing women to better cope with menopausal symptoms at work and provide these 
women with fulfilment, self-esteem, identity, and social needs.35 
With benefits from using efficient air conditioning and cooling during menopause (which 
traditionally occurs between the ages of 45 to 55), the use of these technologies creates an 
important demand cohort of over 20,000 thousand women within the age range of 45 to 59 years 
old, based on the 2010 Census, that may not currently be considered in terms of energy use 
and demand.  
3.1.3 
Demand 
The demand for energy in Barbados over the last 10 years is characterised by a declining trend, 
with an average demand for primary and secondary energy estimated at 11,297 BOE per day.36 
This trend has been partially attributed to the improvement of household energy efficiency 
through the usage of IUS Model technologies like SWH and rooftop solar PV as seen in Figure 
3-4.37 
Figure 3-4: Household Energy Efficiency Trends 
 
At the macro level, demand for IUS Model technologies is influenced by the Government of 
Barbados’ efforts to reduce the import fuel bill to improve the country’s energy security and 
stability in achieving two of the following goals: (1) To convert 75% of the island’s energy supply 
to renewable sources by 2030 and (2) reduced consumption of electricity by 22% by 202938. 
Though there is no data measuring the degree to which barriers noted in the Barrier Section of
