this report have influenced demand, it can be noted that barriers noted in the section below have 
impacted macro demand to some degree.  
Notably, the island is also prone to natural hazards such as hurricanes. Using a mix of RE, EE 
and DER technologies can offer resilience, particularly through self-efficiency. For example, the 
Reefs to Roof Programme (noted in the Policy Environment Section) where the installation of 
rooftop solar PV systems can provide electricity independent of the grid, which provides 
households with electricity in the event of power outages caused by hurricanes or any other 
natural disaster39. With these capabilities, households can maintain a source of energy that may 
be required for the care of members of the disabled community and enable key functions such 
as lighting and communications to (reliable sources of) media in a post-disaster scenario. 
3.1.4 
Barriers 
This small island developing state (SIDS) has been taking steps in reducing its dependence on 
fossil fuel-generated electricity since the uptake of solar water heating in 1974. In its formal 
declaration to be 100% renewable, the Government of Barbados, and its local (private sector 
e.g., Barbados Light, and Power Limited), bilateral and multilateral partners (e.g., Inter-American 
Development Bank) have been actively reducing some barriers in accessing the renewable 
energy and reducing the country’s reliance on fossil fuel generated electricity. 
These barriers range from gaps in legislation addressing inefficient air conditioning to social and 
cultural influences on women’s education and experience within the renewable energy sector 
and should be addressed to promote an equitable and just transition in this goal. 
Institutional 
The island has reduced institutional barriers in the uptake of the target technologies of the IUS 
project, for the most part. Legislation and policies have provided some reduced barriers for solar 
water heating LED Lighting, and solar rooftop photovoltaic, as the Government of Barbados 
journeys towards its goals through the NDCs and the Barbados National Energy Policy.   
Though tax incentives and the streamlining of services have been used as tools to reduce 
barriers, other barriers exist. Tax incentives through the Fiscal Incentive Act of 1974, caused a 
30% increase in the cost of electric water heaters 30% and a 20% decline in the cost of solar 
water heating.40 However, this tax rebate failed to address the consumer awareness (consumers 
were not aware of the SWH technology, as it was new to the region) at the time. 
The streaming line of services is addressed with the Electric and Power Act (Amendment) 2019 
and the Household Right to Renewable Energy41 with an online application process for 
householders. Though still a form of licensing, the online application process - which requires 
roof dimensions, the type and size of the renewable energy system and PV Diagrams42 - 
improves the rate of approval for a landowner to have their 10 KW system within the Barbados 
Grid.  This process, however, brings about a CDRs-related barrier linked to gendered 
stereotypes (See Social Barrier Section).  Specifically in reference to the skillset required for 
the technical creation of blueprints being predominantly held by men as seen in the 
Barbados Labour Survey 2022 where there are 10.9 thousand men within the construction, 
mining and quarrying industry in comparison to 0.4 thousand women, enabling men to 
benefit more from this barrier than women. 
 
39 “More Than Just Cost Savings: How Rooftop Solar Energy Can Improve the Resilience of Local Energy Systems” Retrieved 
from https://energy5.com/more-than-just-cost-savings-how-rooftop-solar-energy-can-improve-the-resilience-of-local-energy-
systems  
40 “Seizing the sunshine: Barbados’ thriving solar water heater industry” retrieved from

=== PAGE 169 ===
Another barrier at the institutional level is the absence of legislation requiring landlords to 
implement IUS Model technologies such as solar water heating, and rooftop solar on their 
properties. Currently, as noted from stakeholder engaged as part of this assessment, renters 
who may be willing to install and use rooftop solar PV, solar water heating and efficient air 
conditioning can be blocked by the landlord from installation and usage. As of  2010, the 
Barbados Census reported 16,316 renters, with an increasing need for rental units43 seeing the 
National Housing Corporation receiving over three thousand applications for rental 
accommodations during a seven year period.44 Such legislation would require conversations 
between representatives of the community, landlord, IUS model technology experts and 
government to determine best practices accounting for any unforeseen cost, such as water 
damage and fire.  
In addition, the absence of legislation governing the use of inefficient air conditioning and 
refrigeration sector is also a barrier, as noted by the Barbados Renewable Energy Association 
(BREA). Additionally, a lack of enforcement of the existing legislation and regulation also create 
a barrier. These barriers currently enable the use of air conditioning systems which are 
consuming heavy amounts of electricity (as noted in the Usage Section). This gap in legislation 
and lack of enforcement could hinder the adaptation of the IUS Model and reduce mitigation 
efforts on fossil fuels with inefficient systems using more energy than an efficient system. 
Notably, the Government of Barbados has been making strides to implement projects that 
improve access and investment incentives within the island. Two of these actions and projects 
include the HOPE Project and the acquisition of electric buses. Figure 3-5 shows partnerships 
and projects implemented with or by the Government of Barbados which can aid in the transition 
to and within the IUS Model.  
The introduction of Electric Mobility into Public Transportation Project is one such example. 
These buses attract riders with air-conditioning, wi-fi, and wheelchair lifts, all of which the old 
diesel buses lack. There are now 49 electric buses, with about a dozen on the way, and the 
government has requested pricing for even more.45 This project aims at reducing the fossil fuel 
reliance on the transportation sector (which is responsible for 33% of energy consumption46) 
whilst providing more accessibility in the island’s transportation system for members of the 
disabled community.

The Home Ownership Providing Energy Project (HOPE) Project seeks to protect households 
with a gross net income of around USD 2,500 (BBD 5048) per month4849, however, the most 
vulnerable and those who are more vulnerable to poverty and those in poverty unfortunately are 
unable to benefit from such a project as the average person makes just 10% of the amount 
required to be a part of the HOPE Project. Concern for young people, in this context, arises from 
their lack of adaptive capacity – economically and financially – due to low-income status or 
underemployed status.  
More generalised vulnerability is further exacerbated by post-disaster shocks such as rapid 
increases in house insurance premiums and increased house costs from damages and loss of 
homes. An example is the passing of Hurricane Elsa in 2021, with reports indicating 1,333 roofs 
were damaged, 326 other houses damaged otherwise, and 145 houses collapsed.50 
Economic 
Due to its geo-political position i.e., Latin America and the Caribbean, Barbados like other 
Caribbean SIDS has been identified as being at risk to money laundering and the use of funding 
for and from terrorism and gangs. Consequently, the number of protocols and requirements for 
access to finance and to international funding flow create an even higher barrier to both men 
and women in applying for and establishing a local bank account. This is also conditioned by the 
fact that most banks in the region are of Canadian origin with some of these pre-conditions built 
in. These layers of review and risk factors act as a potential barrier to accessing financing for 
the installation and usage of all technologies under the IUS model, as an account will most likely 
be required at a recognised financial institution.  
This problem of creating a bank account further intensifies when there is no law mandating equal 
remuneration for work of equal value.51 With no law, employers can discriminate based on 
gender within the workforce, which may lead to women being paid less. This is noted in Figure

This provides difficulty in having the required income level required to be considered for a loan 
from these financial institutions as was confirmed within stakeholder engagements.  
Although LED Lightning is generally affordable, with the bulb costing approximately USD 4.42 
to USD 17.70 at one of the main hardware stores on the island and streetlighting remaining a 
public good,53 the use of solar water heating, rooftop solar photovoltaic and air conditioning is 
more of a middle-class phenomenon. Persons within this economic and social class usually 
more easily take up and sustain the use these technologies (SWH and rooftop solar PV) within 
their homes. A 3.78 Litres solar water heating system costs USD 2,000 (BDS 4,000),54 a rooftop 
solar photovoltaic system55 starts at around USD 5,000 (BDS 10,000)56 and a 12,000 BTU air 
conditioning unit costing at about USD 546 (BDS 1,092).57 
This high cost of entry is more likely to serve as a barrier to uptake for low-income women and 
women-headed households – limiting their capacity in accessing these technologies – as well 
as vulnerable households within the low-income category of USD 100 to USD 250 a week. These 
are the realities for the 21% of females who are experiencing poverty and the 24, 800 women 
whose income is USD 100 (BDS 200) to USD 250 (BDS 500) per week or less.58  
Though financing through commercial banks is an option, the interest rates and the biases 
towards stable income sources when granting loans act as a determinant to financing outside of 
credit unions. Credit unions have lower interest rates than commercial banks and its member-
based organisational structure allows members to have a greater say in its operations.  
It is known that commercial banks tend to favour stability, and secure employment for loans, 
excluding, or having less favourability to the 3,800 female entrepreneurs on the island.59 These

Heavy economic barriers affect a just transition and have been reinforced by key stakeholders' 
consultations with the Bureau of Gender Affairs – Barbados, the Business and Women’s 
Professional’s Club, the Ashley Lashley Foundation, and the Ministry of Energy and Business – 
Energy Division. Today, a large portion of Barbados’ energy transition remains in the hands of 
those who are not low-income earners.  
Social 
The 2010 Census highlights that 48,860 out of 79,300 or 61.61% of women had children, with 
73.5% of children found in households experiencing poverty, where females are the 
breadwinners. Moreover, many of these women make up most low-income workers across many 
sectors (see Figure 3-8) with 21% of women facing poverty.63

Figure 3-8: Vulnerable and Poor Incomes Segregated by Industry and Sex 
 
Education allows people to emerge from poverty, reduces inequalities and creates a climate of 
peace and tolerance in each society. Education remains subsidised by the Government of 
Barbados up to the tertiary level providing equal access to all, however, there are some areas, 
or fields of study that may require some cost inputs from the student. This subsided cost enables 
vulnerable communities to have a chance at accessing education up to tertiary level. However, 
with limited data on poverty, the 21% of women facing poverty in Figure 3-8, will experience 
some level of opportunity cost should they opt to pursue any form of tertiary level education. A 
woman facing poverty may have to reduce payments on a bill or defer a payment.64 
Figure 3-9: Persons Employed in the Energy Sector.65 
Another factor of consideration is the volatility of the industry, women are mostly employed in. 
Take for example, the accommodation and food services industry which employ the second 
highest number of women after wholesale and retail totalling 9.100 thousand women, exposed 
 
64 However, it must be noted that they are scholarships available at tertiary levels to offset or reduce this cost.

=== PAGE 175 ===
to the external shocks of the global economy, for example, the COVID-19 pandemic.66 This 
volatility increases financial stress for women, particularly those in matrifocal households to 
provide for their dependents in down seasons of the industry.  
Gendered stereotypes are a barrier to the uptake of these technologies as women continue to 
be underrepresented within the technical capacities of these technologies67 (See Figure 3-9) and 
enrolment into courses and programmes for these technologies are under-enrolled by young 
women and women (as noted within Stakeholder Engagements).  
Gender stereotypes affect the enrolment of women in technical areas related to these 
technologies seeing 1,500 men within the electric, gas, steam, water, and air conditioning supply 
in comparison to 1,000 women of the 2022 Labour Force Survey (LFS). Moreover, they limit 
women’s employment opportunities, access to resources, and agency in decision-making 
processes.68 These stereotypes act as a major barrier for women being able to benefit from the 
employment opportunities that arise with the increased use and demand for these technologies. 
This reduces the ability for a just transition within this sector, as men continue to disproportionally 
be able to reap the rewards of employment and knowledge of these technologies.  
Women in male-headed households can find themselves dependent on men for the 
maintenance and upkeep of these technologies. This could lead to an increase of gender-based 
violence cases due to the power dynamics within these households as the male head of 
household takes on more financial stress by purchasing these relatively high upfront costs for 
RE technologies wherein they are the sole income generator.69 Such dynamic is highlighted 
amongst several men who thought that women’s lack of economic power made them vulnerable 
to abuse.70  
The use and demand for technologies under the IUS model, i.e., solar water heating, rooftop 
solar photovoltaic, efficient cooling, and refrigeration, remain differentiated by gender due to 
gender norms such as caretaking and cooking.71 Though there is a notable need for the 
technologies and the movement of the Government of Barbados to reduce barriers to these 
technologies, there are some gaps in legislation, high economic barriers and gender 
stereotypical barriers affecting the adaptation and the just transition of these technologies.  
Some of these gaps illustrate the following key issues within the adaption and just transition for 
the IUS Model: 
 
Indirect gender inequality within loan accessibility for women, persons with disabilities and 
other vulnerable groups with a low income. 
 
The absence of gender-responsive and transformative policies and capacity strengthening 
in gender mainstreaming across government and other key stakeholders. 
 
Strong gender stereotyping affects women’s participation at technical levels within the 
energy sector. 
 
Suitable roofs for the installation of these technologies, i.e., solar water heating and rooftop 
solar photovoltaic.

To ensure a just and fair transition in adapting these technologies, there is a need for the 
strengthening of centralised gender mainstreaming institutions in Barbados, improving the 
organisational and institutional gender mainstreaming capacities. Educational and economic 
empowerment is also needed for women to take up these capacity-building and decision-making 
level opportunities. This empowerment can address the culturally defined roles and 
responsibilities such as caretaking, better-improving participation, and engagement of women 
within the sector, which may act as a barrier to participation. 
3.1.5 
Institutional Gender Mainstreaming Capacity  
Institutionally gender mainstreaming capacity remains limited. The main organisation/body with 
established gender mainstreaming capacity the Bureau of Gender Affairs in Barbados, has been 
faced with limited capacity and has taken efforts to address this gap by the hiring of consultant(s) 
and through non-governmental organisation (NGO) partnerships. Presently, the two ways of 
boosting capacity at the Bureau has intersected with a member of an NGO being hired as a 
consultant for the Bureau.  
Similarly, most stakeholders also highlighted the use of consultant(s) in strengthening their 
gender capacity due to their current limited or no existing capacity. This becomes an area of 
concern, particularly in achieving an equitable and just transition of the IUS Model as gender 
mainstreaming in energy projects supports business models that aid women and other 
vulnerable groups to go beyond their traditional roles and work; as job-creators, employees, 
entrepreneurs, and leaders, in the energy sector.72  Such support at a macro level enables equal 
participation and equal distribution of benefits of women and vulnerable groups would see the 
reduction in poverty especially the feminisation of poverty from unequal access.

3.2 
Belize  
Belize, a small developing country, is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Adapting 
to and mitigating the impacts of climate change will require large expansive financial investments 
at the governmental level and individual level. In 2022, Belize achieved a gross domestic product 
(GDP) of 6.7 billion USD, noting recovery from the financial impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic 
but despite this recovery, the country is experiencing an inflation rate of 6.3% which is almost 
double the inflation rate of 2021 (3.2%).76. This has implications for the capacity and ability of 
the population to implement climate change adaptation measures as the cost of food and other 
essentials will rise. This means also that persons will have tough decisions to make as they 
balance having their needs met while still investing in mitigation measures. Female- headed 
households, particularly those in rural communities, will be hit harder by the rising cost of living 
due to various factors such as women earning 10% less than men77, these women bearing the 
brunt of childrearing on their own and being employed in the informal sector.  
In 2021, the Belize Statistical Office released the findings of a poverty study it undertook in 2018 
and it found that 52% of the population is living in poverty which is an 11% (41% poverty rate) 
increase from the last study done in 2009.78  The study notes that 9% of the population is critically 
poor and 11% of the population is vulnerable to falling into poverty. The Maya and Garifuna 
ethnic indigenous groups saw the highest rates of poverty at 78% and 52% respectively and 
these groups primarily reside in the Stann Creek and Toledo districts (noting that these two 
districts have the lowest electricity connection rates as seen in Figure 3-11). 79,80  Toledo 
possessed the highest poverty rate at 82% while the remaining districts rate ranged from 45-
57%. The findings of the study suggest there is a link between the level of education attained 
and poverty status as households with the head of the household having no formal education 
saw a rate of 64% while households with the head of household possessing tertiary or post-
secondary education having a rate of 12%.  
In 2020, 97.1% of the population enjoyed access to electricity81 which indicates significant work 
being done to provide all Belizean with reliable access to electricity because as seen in the 
UNICEF 2015 Multi-Indicator Cluster Survey (MICS), only 91.8% of the population had access 
to electricity with 96.9 of the urban population and 87.5% of the rural population having electricity 
access.82 As seen in Figure 3-11, of the 6 six districts Toledo district had the lowest rate of 70.8% 
in 2015.83

Noting Toledo as the area with the lowest connection, the district has been the target of many 
projects aimed at increasing access. Renewable energy is playing an important role in tackling 
this problem as renewable energy technologies are being utilised as the means of providing 
energy to rural communities while playing a part in the country's efforts to address and adapt to 
climate change. For example, in 2020, a solar plant was installed in Indian Creek Village in 
Toledo District which will also benefit Medina Bank and Golden Stream villages as well. This 
work was a part of the emPOWERing Rural Electrification Project funded by the government of 
the United Arabs Emirates.85  
3.2.1 
Policy Environment  
Energy policies in general, in Belize, did not possess strong content that spoke to empowering 
women in the sector or implementing measures to address their needs; however, mention is 
made of health risks faced by women with the usage of fuelwood. At the same time, women’s 
low participation within the sector is acknowledged (see Figure 3-12). The national gender policy 
seeks to expand opportunities for all and could serve as a good starting point when initiating any 
revisions or policy developments that would need to be specific to the energy sector.

=== PAGE 181 ===
Seven policies were analysed, those most directly relevant to the energy sector and including 
the National Gender Policy (all are summarised in Figure 3-12). Of the 7, 2 are transformative, 
1 responsive and transformative, 1 responsive, 2 aware and one blind. The existence of the 
Gender Policy and the NDC in combination provide a solid foundation or defining gender and 
social dimensions for a just transition in the context of the IUS model implementation in Belize. 
The updated NDC86 of 2021 for Belize, was developed using a gender and vulnerable group 
analysis which provided recommendations for improving the gender sensitivity of both medium 
term and long-term strategies that exist within the commitments and activities outlined within the 
NDC. NDC targets and actions in the document were developed with the participation of the 
public, including consultations with local communities and indigenous persons, in a gender-
responsive manner. Actions and targets in the updated NDC were submitted after undergoing a 
gender and vulnerable group scoring analysis. This aided in providing recommendations for 
improved gender sensitivity in NDC implementation and the longer-term low-emissions strategy. 
While gender responsiveness is imbedded within the strategy for the updated NDC, there exists 
only one direct reference to women in the document which falls under the targets outlined for 
the agricultural sector. This mention directs specific action toward increasing public awareness 
that targets women, youth and communities on reducing GHG emissions through alternate 
production methods.  
In terms of consideration for indigenous persons within the actions of the NDC beyond 
consultations, specific references to indigenous groups include promoting the monitoring and 
stewardship of indigenous lands, as well as indigenous coastal lands, to be sustainably 
managed toward the goal of producing net carbon sink areas in over 10,000 hectares of 
indigenous land. The document also notes the development of area-specific strategies for 
adaptation that pay attention to indigenous communities more generally while also promoting 
and enhancing policies and frameworks that consider climate (and water) sensitive development 
and use of lands. In doing so, the NDC notes the need to enhance and encourage current 
practices in land stewardship that exist in indigenous communities. The document also notes 
the need to be inclusive of gender, indigenous communities, PWDs, and youth in a way that is 
balanced and inclusive in terms of climate financing. These efforts are supported by climate 
finance strategy developed and implemented for the period 2021 – 2025.  
Belize’s Revised National Gender Policy87, updated in 2013, is focused on building upon and 
continuing the country’s efforts to achieve and sustain gender equality and equity. It includes 
objectives for supporting efforts toward ‘human-centered development’, with equal participation 
of men and women, with equal co-benefits being distributed. The policy also includes the 
objective to encourage and facilitate the equal access to, and control over productive resources, 
services, and opportunities between men and women.  The revised national gender policy is 
based on the vision, whereby there exists:  
“A society in which all men and women, boys and girls are able to achieve their full potential 
through the enjoyment of their human rights; live together in mutual respect, dignity and 
harmony; and are equal partners as they participate in services and resources for realizing and 
sustaining their economic, social, political, and cultural development for equal enjoyment of all.” 
 
86 “Belize Updated Nationally Determined Contribution” retrieved from https://unfccc.int/sites/default/files/NDC/2022-
06/Belize%20Updated%20NDC.pdf  
87 “Revised National Gender Policy” retrieved from https://oig.cepal.org/sites/default/files/belize_2013national_gender_policy.pdf

=== PAGE 182 ===
Two additional policies were reviewed that give mention to women and vulnerable persons, 
specifically children within the country. These were the National Gender-Based Violence Plan of 
Action 2010-201388 and the Belize National Child Labour Policy and Strategy 2022-2015.89   
Both policies were shown to be gender transformative, while the GBV Plan of Action provides 
several specific gender-responsive actions in its framework. The policy outlines four key goals 
and associated objectives for responding to GBV and supporting survivors of GBV. The Plan 
addresses provisions for legal support to survivors and legal action toward perpetrators. It also 
addresses the need for greater public awareness of the root causes of GBV and greater 
awareness and understanding by men of the root causes of GBV. The National Child Labour 
Policy and Strategy (2022-2015) utilises significant gender disaggregated data in its 
development of actions and denotes the need to build awareness of legal access and support 
while tackling both gender and social norms that place boys at much higher risk for child labour 
participation. The policy and the plan provide a framework in which the protection and safety of 
these two vulnerable groups can be safeguarded in the project and avoided possible exploitation 
and harm that may arise intentionally or unintentionally.  
A new energy policy is currently in development for the country as the previous energy as noted 
in the table above has limited mentions of gender and there exists opportunities to improve on 
that area for the new policy (access to the draft has not been received yet)90.  Additionally, New 
Service Access policy91 is a new policy being launched by Belize Electricity Limited (BEL) which 
aims to reduce the cost of electricity connections by removing the additional connection setup 
fees when connecting homes in areas with existing connections. This helps support low-income 
persons to gain access to electricity. In cases of areas with no existing connections, the policy 
has provisions for persons to pay a deposit instead of the entire connection setup cost upfront. 
3.2.2 
Usage  
Rooftop Solar Photovoltaic 
Within the Maya indigenous communities in the Toledo district (found in the southernmost part 
of the country) exist technical capacity to deploy solar energy technologies. Three Maya women 
were trained in solar energy technology at the Barefoot University in India.92 From their 
experience, the benefits of women being involved in the field could be seen.  With this capacity 
gained and material support, they were able to provide the community of Santa Teresa solar 
power over a two-year timespan. Additionally, through a project called Indigenous Female Solar 
Engineers Scaling up Solar Energy to Machakilha and Graham Creek, a collaboration between 
Plenty Belize and the Ministry of Rural Transformation, these women will be helping to provide 
these communities with solar powered electricity while also equipping these communities with 
the knowledge to maintain the technologies on their own.  
This ongoing project has a strong emphasis on ensuring all residents of the community are 
empowered through skills-building and other capacity building workshops made available to all. 
Another emphasis of this project is the promotion of and support women in non-traditional roles 
with women who were trained at Barefoot University serving as models for other women and 
girls of the nation. The solar electrification provided by this project generate several direct 
household benefits for all members. These benefits address children’s need for education 
through the provision of light and power for technologies that aid in education. For the adults in

the household, access to electricity provide them with new opportunities for their livelihoods or 
enhancements to their livelihoods which in turn, enhances their ability to make decisions to 
increase their quality of life and implement climate change adaptation measures.93 Alongside 
the direct household benefits, the project provides these communities with communal benefits 
such as 1) small shops with the ability to run a refrigerator/freezer ran by one or two systems 
and these refrigerators serve to enhance food security and food safety through the ability to store 
foods and extend food’s shelf life, 2) a medium size power system installed at the primary school 
which would enhance the school’s ability to provide quality education to these children,  and 3) 
a solar water pump to provide potable water which would improve the quality of health in these 
areas by providing clean water and reduce the time spent by primarily women in fetching water 
for household use.  
Access to electricity provides numerous benefits to those living in rural communities. It provides 
cleaner energy to use for lighting and other tasks and reduces exposure to smoke and other 
pollutants released from the burning of coal, firewood and kerosene that are the typical biomass 
or fossil-fuel-based energy sources typically used in rural communities. This allows them to save 
money as the need to purchase fuel is reduced and the savings can be used to increase the 
quality of their lives in other ways. With reliable access to electricity, persons in very remote 
areas are now able to utilise mobile/satellite phones and this provides many benefits to the 
community as it increases their security to an extent as calls for help can be sent out faster than 
persons having to journey out of the community, and this also applies to requesting medical aid. 
Additionally, having enhanced communication increases their access to information which would 
ideally expand their capacity for participation and to exercise rights in the context of energy as 
well as climate change mitigation.  Similarly, the Garifuna communities in the Stann Creek 
District also utilize the combustion of fuels to meet their energy needs as well and face similar 
issues.  
The abovementioned issues and concerns primarily affect those living in Toledo and Stann 
Creek districts. Those living in Cayo, Orange Walk, Corozal and Belize City districts which 
constitute the more urban areas of the country, enjoy better connections to electricity, thus 
reducing their need to rely on the burning of fuels to provide energy to carry out domestic duties 
or to support their livelihoods. In turn, they are less likely to be exposed to harmful pollutants 
released from the combustion of these fuels. A notable exception to this would be for the island 
of Caye Caulker with 1,700 persons residing on the island that would have utilised a diesel 
generator to supply the island with power. The island imports 730, 000 gallons of diesel annually 
to provide electricity for the entire island, however, in 2019, CDB approved a loan to fund the 
connection of the island to the national grid via a submarine cable.94 This result in the island 
utilising renewable energy for 50% of its energy and reduces electricity costs from USD 0.26 per 
kWh to USD 0.11 per kWh. The submarine cable adds the benefit of being more reliable and 
resilient to storm surges and hurricane winds.  
LED Lighting  
In 2019, the energy unit of the Ministry of Public Service, Energy and Public Utilities launched 
an initiative in collaboration with electrical students at the Institute for Technical and Vocational 
Education and Training (ITVET) to install LED lights in public buildings.95 The initiative saw the 
installation of 185 tubes across four locations in Stann Creek with the aim of promoting EE in 
public building and energy conservation measures to the public while also developing the

technical knowledge and skills of the ITVET students. The initiative noted that over a 5-year 
period, the cost of using fluorescent lighting would have amounted to USD 27,311.90 USD (BZD 
55,302.91) while the use of LED lighting for the same period would only cost USD 13,655.95 
(BZD 27,651.46). The switching of traditional (fluorescent) bulbs to LED bulbs can result in a 
reduction in electricity consumption which in turn, means households can save additional money 
on monthly basis as their electricity bill would be reduced. This reduction in monthly expenses 
can provide single income especially female-headed homes with some relief as their financial 
burdens are lessened which could possibly translate to better mental health as persons would 
have less issues to worry about. At the same time, these savings though small, could be utilised 
to improve other aspects of the household.  
BEL carried out a renewable energy project nationwide, aimed at replacing 31,000 streetlights 
with LED lights in 2016.96 This project benefited areas across the country that did not have well 
lit streets, noting that some lights were installed in strategic areas frequented by people on their 
way to work or home to help deter criminal activities. Residents of San Mateo (located on the 
island of Ambergris Caye) expressed satisfaction over the activity as recent crime in the area 
had residents fearful as many streets were not properly lighted. Lighting can be used as a 
passive measure to reduce crime as persons would feel less inclined to commit criminal acts 
when their identity is more exposed.97 Additionally, women would feel a bit more empowered to 
participate in society, particularly in sectors that may have them working late at night as the light 
provides a sense of safety.  
Solar Water Heating  
The Development Finance Corporation (DFC) has partnered with the Belize Biltmore Plaza to 
promote the adoption of renewable energy in businesses. In 2019, they provided the plaza with 
resources to setup a solar water heating demonstration project.98 The demonstration project was 
aimed to raise awareness of financial instruments that can be utilised to invest in SWH as well 
as other RE technologies and demonstrate the benefits of switching to renewable energy. This 
demonstration was targeted at hotels as they use a large amount of hot water for laundry and 
guest usage. Caribbean Regional Energy Development Programme of the CARICOM 
Secretariat hosted two one-day seminars in Belize aimed at introducing hoteliers to the benefits 
of using SWH in their businesses as well as promote CARICOM region SWH manufacturers and 
explore business opportunities.99  
While a lot of the promotion and awareness efforts for SWH has been targeted at the tourism 
sector, SWH can also be beneficial to the domestic household. The use of a SWH can lead to 
the reduction of electricity consumption as less electricity is used to heat water needed for 
showering and other domestic tasks. Through this reduction, persons could possibly save up to 
USD 597.58 (BZD 1204) per year depending on the usage.100  This could lead to increased 
financial ability through the savings made from using a SWH. Additionally, it could lessen the 
domestic work performed by women by speeding up the process of heating water when needed.  
Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration  
The country experiences a moist tropical climate that slightly differs from other Caribbean 
Countries. The wet season occurs from May to October and its dry season occurs during 
November to April. The country sees a mean annual temperature range from 23-27°C, noting

that mean annual temperatures have increased by 0.45°C at an average rate of 0.10°C per

persons, there is an increased need for cooling and thus efficient cooling for the cost saving 
aspects. Relief from rising temperatures is necessary as looking at forecast data from Belize’s 
meteorological office shows temperatures from 31.9-34.5°C across the country with maximum 
temperatures reaching 38.8°C.  
No data could be found on the differential usage of cooling between men, women, children and 
the elderly. It is important to understand that efficient cooling will be important in coming days to 
combat heat strokes as they may become common and rising temperatures may exacerbate 
discomfort women experience during menopause. Heat stress can be disruptive to productivity 
and quality of life. Caribbean Climate Outlook Forum warns that Belize among other Caribbean 
countries will be expected to deal with increased heat stress in the third quarter of 2023.102  
3.2.3 
Demand 
The demand for renewable energy and renewable energy technologies has been rising steadily 
over the years which has been supported by the government as it works towards achieving its 
renewable energy targets. Looking at the Belize’s energy sources in 2010 43% came from fossil 
fuels (see Figure 3-13), the government has been working to develop policies103 and strategies 
to guide the country towards more renewable energy sources and increase the adoption of EE 
to reduce energy consumption.104

aiming to hit 70% of energy coming from renewable sources by 2030 and by 2021, the country

3.2.4 
Barriers  
3.2.3.1 Financial Considerations 
Rising costs of living is not the only factor that inhibits the financial ability of Belizean people, 
particularly women to afford a range of energy sources and technologies. Employment greatly 
impacts the finances available to persons whether they are employed in seasonal sectors, 
working in underpaid jobs or working in the informal sector. Many persons operate in the informal 
sector, particularly in rural areas as they may lack qualifications (education) or fail to meet 
requirements (possessing a valid form of national identification) or simply unable to afford the 
costs of travel. Many places of employment require persons to possess a valid form of national 
identification, however, it must be noted this could be a barrier of entry to low-income persons 
as the national identification card costs USD 2.48 (BZD 5106) to issue for the first time and USD 
12.91 (BZD 26)  to replace107 while the driver’s license costs USD 44.68 (BZD 90) to issue and 
 
105 “Energy Report Card 2021 – Belize” retrieved from https://www.ccreee.org/wp-
content/uploads/2023/01/ERC_Belize_final_002.pdf  
106 The conversion rate used is 1 BZD equals 0.49 USD as of June 2023. 
107 “Card Information” retrieved from https://www.socialsecurity.org.bz/card-information/

=== PAGE 187 ===
USD 29.79 (BZD 60) to renew yearly.108 These costs may be untenable for some as they balance 
costs for themselves and unemployed dependents.  
The UNICEF 2015 Multi-Indicator Cluster Survey for Belize found only 37% of women and 40% 
of men have a secondary level education.109 It also notes that two in five women (2 in 5) have 
only obtained a primary school level of education and a similar rate can be seen in men. These 
factors alone or working in conjunction together leave a noted proportion of persons (35.3% or 
64, 268 persons out of the 182, 084 employed persons110) with only informal work as a means 
of generating income.  
Working in the informal sector may provide persons with opportunities and certain freedoms 
particularly rural women who may not have the ability to access more formal employment due 
to ineligibility or conflicting responsibilities (such as family care). Despite these benefits, informal 
sector work can lack the security and integrity afforded to formal employment. This can have far-
reaching consequences for persons as the lack of an employment letter or self-employed forms 
could pose as a barrier to accessing loans and other financial tools from financial institutions.  
Having access to additional finance will be important to purchase renewable energy 
technologies. The average  cost of a solar water heater in Belize ranges from USD 992.25 (BZD 
1998.71) to USD 1984.50111 (BZD 3,997.42) which is 7-14.1% of the average yearly income 
(USD 14,112112/BZD 28,426.13) in Belize. Noting this level of expense, access to financial aid 
will be a necessity for low-income female- headed households and other vulnerable groups to 
access these technologies, without which they would otherwise be left behind in the transition to 
green energy.  
Similar barriers are experienced by indigenous communities, especially those in rural parts of 
the country. These communities, particularly the Maya, are located in areas that are quite difficult 
to access, often without fully developed roads and telecommunication infrastructure and/or 
coverage. These conditions pose a significant hindrance to the economic empowerment of 
indigenous people. The barriers posed by limited transportation options and long travel times 
severely limit economic opportunities, be it seeking employment or selling goods as examples 
of how livelihoods could be impacted. This leaves formal and informal agriculture as a source of 
livelihood for many and depending on the way agricultural activity is managed, many may be 
ineligible to apply for loans from financial institutions. Obtaining loans could entail needing valid 
identification, financial records, business plans, marketing plans113 and some rural indigenous 
farmers, especially female farmers, may not have the capacity to develop these critical 
documents to meet the scope of the institutions.   
Persons with disabilities (PWDs) face their own unique barriers and plight when it comes to 
access to finance and the cost of living. Some PWDs live in squalor114 as they subsist on 
whatever aid they receive from relatives or organisations or government subvention. Many 
PWDs are unable to work due to (i) a lack of accommodations in the workplace to meet the 
needs of their disability and (ii) their limited access to education because of their disability thus 
the lack of necessary qualifications. Some PWDs live with relatives on whom they are entirely 
dependent financially, thereby creating an additional set of possible challenges to caregivers.

Given this financial dependence, PWDs have limited economic power. In addition to the lack of 
finances due to unemployment, the cost of caring and treating for disabilities can be quite 
expensive - this could range from the price of obtaining mobility aids to requiring frequent medical 
treatments and/or the price of medicine to treat chronic conditions. PWDs’ ability to implement 
renewable energy technologies is thus lowered due to these financial demands as they form 
additional financial hurdles that PWDs must deal with.    
3.2.3.2 Housing 
The state and quality of housing has been an issue identified in Belize even within urban areas 
of the country in relation to the implementation (installation) of RE/DER technologies identified 
for the IUS project particularly for solar water heaters, and rooftop solar. It must be noted that 
the project entails rooftop solar (although there is a possibility of this technology being 
implemented in other ways) and efficient cooling through air conditioning, there exists a 
requirement for homes to meet certain standards in order for these technologies to be effective. 
Figure 3-16 shows the characteristics of housing across the country derived from the MICS 
survey. From the MICS survey it can be seen that a low but still significant number of households 
would not be able to support some of these technologies efficiently and would require 
adjustments or support to bring their houses up to standard.  Only 95.7% of urban households 
and 88% of rural households have finished floors, roofing and walls. 7.9% of urban households 
and 12% of rural households have rudimentary roofing which is of concern due to these types 
of roofs being unable to support the solar water heaters or solar photovoltaic and may even 
suffer damages from them.  
From the survey, 67% of households are headed by men with the remaining 33% headed by 
women.115 The Mestizo/Spanish/Latino ethnic group accounts for the largest ethnic group for 
household heads at 49.1%, followed by Creole at 25.5% while the indigenous ethic groups of 
Maya and Garifuna account for 9.1% and 5.7% respectively.116  
The state of housing quality is especially of concern for PWDs in situations where they are living 
on their own and typically lack the finances to secure proper housing or to maintain and upkeep 
their homes.117 Another group of major concern, because of housing quality and housing 
infrastructure, is indigenous communities living in rural areas, especially those who live in 
traditional housing. Traditional indigenous housing structures are not typically suited to RE, EE 
and DERs and require an adapted approach (either installation or the technology itself). Rooftop 
solar, in particular, may compromise the structural integrity of the roofing over time without 
adjustments.

115 Information on the distribution of these households across the six districts is not available nor is data on breakdown of these 
households by ethnicity and sex.  
116 “Belize Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey, 2015-2016, Final Report” retrieved from 
https://www.unicef.org/belize/media/936/file/Multiple%20indicator%20cluster%20survey.pdf 
117 “Living With a Disability in Dire Conditions” retrieved from https://edition.channel5belize.com/archives/251267  
118 “Belize Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey, 2015-2016, Final Report” retrieved from 
https://www.unicef.org/belize/media/936/file/Multiple%20indicator%20cluster%20survey.pdf

=== PAGE 189 ===
An additional housing-related issue to be considered is the ownership of the home. 68.9% of all 
households are owned by a household member with 59.3% of urban households and 77.2% of 
rural households being owned by a household member.119 For persons in urban areas, this 
suggest more than 35% of persons are  renting or living in a house owned by another relative 
outside of the home. For persons living in homes they do not own, there are likely limitations on 
what modifications they are allowed to make to the house without the consent of the property 
owner based on the rental agreement/lease they signed. The Landlord and Tenant Act does not 
have provisions120 that would prevent renters acting on their own volition, this means the extent 
of modifications a renter is allowed would be dependent on the stipulations made in the legal 
contract between the landlord and tenant. Implementing rooftop solar photovoltaic, solar water 
heaters and air conditioning in rented homes may require having to navigate the power dynamics 
between renter and property owner and the legal requirements in place as they may require 
infrastructural work to be installed unlike LED lighting which is a simple installation.  
With regard to leadership and decision-making within homes, it is notable that 67% of 
households are male-headed while only 33% are female-headed.  Given that men and women 
have different energy needs and uses due to division of labour121, it is important to consider 
whoever is making the decision on the sources of energy to be used by the household as there 
can be implications for the household’s energy costs and consumption patterns. Women in male-
headed households may spend more time at home performing domestic work and will benefit 
more from the use of solar water heaters in assisting with their household tasks than the male 
head would, whom may have less use for hot water. Likewise, women in male-headed household 
would spend more time at home and would benefit more from the cooling provided by air 
conditioning, gaining relief from the rising temperatures than the male head who may spend a 
third or more of the day out of the home for work. Given these possibilities, the male head may 
deem these technologies as low priority as they do not directly see the immediate benefits. 
On the opposite spectrum, female-headed households may have the same needs of the solar 
water heater and other technologies as women in male-headed households, however they must 
contend with conflicting responsibilities. In a male-headed household, there is a possibility for 
multiple income sources in cases such as married couple, common law couple or other types of 
relations. For female-headed households122, these are typically single income homes with a 
woman caring for children or other dependents. In this situation with more limited resources, 
women must weigh their needs and chose according to their priorities wherein acquiring solar 
water heater or the other technologies may be designated as a low priority action in favour of 
other pressing needs.   
Livelihoods  
A critical element to consider when assessing the barriers and the capacities of persons to 
implement the target technologies is understanding the source of their income generation. 
Livelihood plays a major role in a person’s ability to build capacity and empower themselves and 
would have implications for the climate change adaptations, in this case the implementation of 
RE/EE/DER technologies they can afford and are capable of implementing.  While livelihoods 
could be impacted by a person’s ability to access education and opportunities, it influences the 
other barriers to accessing these technologies such as housing and finance. Income from 
livelihoods would then determine what technologies a person can afford, what loans they are

=== PAGE 190 ===
eligible and what other related measures they are able to adopt for the successful and effective 
implementation of the target technologies.   
One of the biggest factors that affects a person’s livelihood and their options for livelihood is their 
ability to access education and assets.123 Having access to necessary resources and education, 
empowers one to capitalise on opportunities and enhances their ability to participate in climate 
change adaptation measures in this instance. From the highest education level attained by 
heads of households, it could be seen that almost half (49.2%) heads of households have only 
primary school level of education as their highest form education, 26.7% with a secondary school 
level, 16.9% with a tertiary school level and 7.2% with no formal education.124 It is critical to 
acknowledge that affects the kinds of livelihoods persons engage in and it is reflected in the 
occupations held by persons as seen in the Belize Labour Force Survey. From the 2022 survey, 
services and sales workers had the largest share of employed persons at 58.1 and generally 
female-dominated (39.7% females to 18.4% males) while elementary occupations followed with 
35.5, still male-dominated (21.5% males to 14% female).125 A large percentage of services and 
sales jobs are associated with the tourism sector (with it accounting for 28% of employed 
persons) which, in turn, has significant implications for income generation for those tied to the 
sector.  The seasonal nature of the tourism sector is of concern due persons’ sources of income 
decreasing during the off-peak season which receives less visitors to the country (the high 
season for tourism in Belize is from December to April which coincides with its dry season126) as 
this will affect the level of loan they might qualify for, the financial decisions they need to make 
during and before the off-season and other issues that may result from the lowered economic 
ability during this time. 
Traditional gender norms are at play in the share of men and women in the tourism sector. 
Despite it being one of the more important sectors in the country, women still dominate this 
sector as jobs related to catering to and providing services to persons which are typically 
perceived as activities for women. This perception of some jobs being deemed for women, and 
some being deemed for men continues to perpetuate the labour market segmentation and 
impacts career/livelihood choices for men and women. This is seen above with the share of 
women employed in sales and services jobs accounting for double the number of men in this 
field. This implies that these traditional gender norms still hold root in society with the way 
persons choose careers. The domination of women in this field has one major consequence 
which is on a yearly basis; many women see reduced income between May to November when 
the country receives less visitors and business slows down. The gender division of labour can 
also be seen in the share of women and men in clerical support worker roles (13.5% for women 
and 5.6% for men). Meanwhile, in more technical/physical fields, men dominate more as seen 
in the following breakdown: plant and machine operators 9.1% men to 0.6% women, craft and 
related trades works 19.3% men to 3.8% women and skilled agricultural and forestry workers 
7.8% men to 0.8 women%127. Noted in 2020 Energy Report Card, women only accounted for 
30.3% of the energy sector workforce which is comprised of the energy unit, BEL and the public 
utilities commission (PUC) (see Figure 3-17), further exhibiting the traditional division of labour 
seen in the country.128

Men enjoy more stable sources of income which would allow them to amass finances and be

opportunities as men are represented more in the associated engineering and energy fields. The 
three indigenous women who were trained in solar technology do represent an opportunity for 
more women to get involved in the sector.  
3.2.5 
Institutional Gender Mainstreaming Capacity 
A limited gender mainstreaming capacity assessment was performed with stakeholders as part 
of the stakeholder engagement process conducted to support the gender assessment of Belize. 
These series of questions were designated to obtain some insight into the capacity of 
stakeholders to perform gender mainstreaming as well as delve into what institutional 
frameworks within their organisations exist for gender. Meeting with country’s main body 
charged with gender would have been pivotal to understand its capacity and ongoing work that 
may be able to inform or feed into this project.  
From the government institutions, it can be seen in Figure 3-18, there is no gender 
mainstreaming capacity131 and limited experience with gender mainstreaming capacities, 
highlighting a need for gender sensitisation and capacity- building. It is important to note that 
NGOs particularly Productive Organization for Women in Action (POWA) has capacity to support 
gender mainstreaming activities and capacity- building, given their history with supporting 
renewable energy initiatives from another Belizean NGO, Plenty Belize.   
Noting from the Ministry of Energy that while projects they have carried out in the past had 
substantial participation from women, the ministry has never analysed these projects from a 
gender perspective. A competency-based approach is used in hiring and procurement. 
Important to flag is the limited gender mainstreaming capacity of the Executing Entity (BEL) as 
they will be charged with executing and handling various aspects of the project. Their limited

Executing Entity Gender Capacity Analysis  
One of the key stakeholders for this project will be the utility, BEL (the Executing Entity). They 
will be charged with executing this project which would entail managing the funds to be allocated 
to support the distribution of the renewable energy technologies to customers and interacting 
with customers in general. Noting this will entail BEL interacting with vulnerable groups such as 
rural, low-income women, indigenous people and PWDs, it was important to understand how 
BEL operates, how they engage with customers, how complaints are handled and the capacity 
of the organisation and staff. Given BEL’s importance to the project, it was key to engage with 
them to gain insight into these areas.  
BEL shows flexibility in meeting and accommodating their customers through policies that allow 
customers to conduct all their business via WhatsApp communication. For those customers who 
do not utilise WhatsApp, BEL will go to their location in order to conduct business with said 
customers.  
BEL frequently participates in trade shows which they use to educate customers on policies and 
procedures in order to boost customers’ ability to engage the utility. It is noted that BEL has no 
specific engagement with PWDs or persons from LGBTQIA+ community but the utility does work 
with indigenous groups to increase access to electricity. BEL is aware of the Free, Prior, and 
Informed Consent (FPIC) and operations with indigenous people are guided by FPIC to a 
degree.  
BEL has a grievance redress mechanism in place for employees, however for customers, 
grievance is handled by the regulator (PUC).  
BEL notes that it is not in possession of a gender policy.   
3.3 
Jamaica  
3.3.1 
Policy environment 
Jamaica’s renewable energy capacity stood at 17.1% in 2020 with solar and wind contributing 
to most of the country's current renewable energy usage, which is means a lot of work needs to 
be done in order to meet the new goal set out.134 
Jamaica’s Integrated Resource Plan 2018 laid out the country’s goal to reach 30% renewable 
energy usage by 2030.135 However, the country has a new goal it is aiming to achieve following 
after Prime Minister Andrew Holness revealed intentions for a new standard of 50% renewable 
energy by 2030 instead.136 The transition towards more energy efficient methods has the 
capacity to greatly minimise the time and labour constraints associated with gender inequality 
while also enhancing community health, income generating opportunities, and capacity-building 
efforts.137 However, Jamaica faces issues of costing, an enabling policy environment and 
barriers to entry, particularly for women.  
Figure 3-19 provides a review of current policies. The policies listed in the Jamaica context 
include no substantive provisions for advancing the area of gender equality for the country and 
can be deemed as negligible in terms of support for any necessary gender mainstreaming 
actions. However, the National Policy on Gender Equality (NPGE), the NDC and the Vision 2030

Transformative 
 
Seven policies were identified in Jamaica: 3 neutral, 1 sensitive and 3 transformative.  The latest, 
the LTS, combines several elements and provides a critical long-term vision for where energy 
fits within a broader climate change response and sustainable development pathway. While the 
energy policies are relatively gender-neutral (or gender blind), other broader policies tended to 
be more gender transformative.  
The NPGE is quite detailed and one of the few across the region and among the three countries 
that is detailed across several sectors, including energy. The fact that two of the above-
mentioned policy frameworks are transformative also lays a critical foundation for just transition 
elements of the proposed GCF proposal depending on what is agreed for Jamaica compared to 
other countries. It provides a framework on the country’s commitments toward gender equality 
measures, providing an enabling environment to achieve fairness and socio-economic justice 
for both women and men, in line with the goals and objectives as defined by Jamaica’s Vision 
2030 plan. Within the policy, an overarching goal defines the need to transform existing gender 
ideologies and create transformation within the public sector. The policy’s objectives support 
reducing gender-based discrimination and improving mainstreaming of gender within all cultural, 
social, political, and economic structures and systems. It also encourages the promotion of 
capacity-building and improved organisational behaviour throughout the development, 
implementation, and monitoring of gender responsive activities. Despite the extent of cross-
cutting information across sectors: poverty, education, labour, health, trade, agriculture, and 
social protection systems, and culture, the author noted no specific references to renewable 
energy and women’s involvement or provision of services, noted within the policy.  
Jamaica’s update of their NDC was published in 2020. In preparation of the document, 
consultation was conducted with local communities and indigenous populations in a gender-
responsive way. The document notes the country’s progress towards its measures for reaching 
SDG 5, gender equality. It also notes its commitment to adhering to policies and programmes 
aimed at improving environments for inclusivity and fairness as outlined in the National Policy 
for Gender Equality, 2021.  
Jamaica’s National Development Plan139, Vision 2030, developed in 2010 in intended to provide 
a roadmap for making Jamaica a developed country by 2030. The plan consists of several goals 
and associated outcomes. Within the four main goals are measures for social protection, health, 
 
138 A draft of this document was received by the consultant who participated in a validation meeting for the document.  
139 “Vision 2030” retrieved from https://www.vision2030.gov.jm/

=== PAGE 197 ===
and education; a secure and cohesive society; a prosperous economy; and maintaining a 
thriving natural resource environment. The plan recognises inequalities experienced by women 
and PWDs and seeks to rectify these issues by creating an environment that ensures future 
policies captures these issues while also outlining plans to address some of the underlying 
societal problems. Within the energy sector, the plan promotes improved energy conservation 
within households, but does not make specific reference to gender or women. The same exists 
for promoting a technology-enabled society, while noting a desire to expand knowledge and 
development, creativity and development of products and services, does not specifically 
reference gender or improving the needs and livelihoods of women.  
Jamaica has recently, as of July 2022, developed and launched a gender and climate change 
strategy and action plan (GCCSAP), guided by the results of a national gender and climate 
change assessment.140 The assessment, in part, conducted an analysis of gender and climate 
change linkages, providing recommendations for action in the country going forward. Financed 
by the GCF, the GCCSAP includes provisions for institutional capacity for mainstreaming gender 
in climate finance programming.141 
3.3.2 
Usage  
In general, women tend to face a disproportionate burden of labour compared with men, through 
their responsibilities taking care of children and other household tasks, in addition to any labour 
performed outside the home for earned income142. In terms of women’s abilities to access 
financial assets and resources, infrastructure services can play a role in lessening the added 
burdens placed on women though their dual roles as caretakers and paid employees in the 
labour force.143  
Ensuring proper services through sustainable energy provisions can provide both cost and time-
savings to consumers, especially women who already may be operating in a deficit in each of 
these areas. These economic and time savings may allow women to accumulate capital which 
can have additional benefits in terms of resource allocation toward better health, added income 
and education for women in most contexts. 
LED Lighting 
In recent years, the Jamaican Public Service (JPS) has been working on energy conversion 
projects and initiatives in Jamaica, including those for light-emitting diode (LED) lighting and 
high-pressure sodium (HSP) lamps. Currently, LED lighting and public buildings targeted for 
energy efficiency retrofits consume only 7% of Jamaica’s total generated electricity.144 In 2018, 
the JPS, converted 63% of the island's streetlights over to LEDs. In real terms, this covered a 
total of 67,000 – out of the 105,000 streetlights on the nation’s roads. In 2022, the company was 
also reported to have converted all existing high-pressure sodium (HPS) lamps with LEDs within 
Jamaica. These are more energy efficient, have a longer service life and added intelligent-
technology capabilities. JPS is continuing work on its streetlight general maintenance and repair 
programs in the interim.  
Difficulties with the HPS lighting, including vandalism, have been an issue. These problems are 
confirmed with statistics showing that an average of 620 repairs per month were conducted by

LED Lighting for Support for Women and Vulnerable Groups  
Provision of improved lighting services can support various vulnerable groups, in the urban and 
rural context. Poor lighting services can keep women and girls out of public spaces, exacerbating 
their senses of vulnerability and insecurity, and placing restrictions of their ability to safely pursue 
independent living. Ample street lighting has been seen to reduce the prevalence of crime in 
areas with sufficient lighting.149 Increased Street lighting can offer important economic 
advantages in addition to safety benefits. Better lighting services can enable longer business 
hours in some cases where work hours transition from daylight hours, this is especially the case 
for women who operate in the informal economy in roles such as street vendors.  
By creating safe gathering spaces, lighting services have also been shown to support the 
reduction of social isolation, boost community cohesion, and decrease the number of people, 
especially women who stay indoors during the night.150 For other vulnerable populations, 
improved lighting services has also shown to assist with children’s education in terms of how 
well they perform and function by supporting their visual, cognitive, and behavioural abilities 
through focus, alertness, and mood management.151 It also makes it easier for those with visual 
disabilities to see better and allows a person with hearing impairments to make lipreading easier. 
Problems and challenges, however, do exist in transitioning energy systems and improving 
lighting capacity. In 2021, the theft situation rapidly worsened in Jamaica, hampering 
government efforts to illuminate crime-ridden areas. The power company disclosed in 2019 that 
the thefts were harming its investments in public lighting.152 Within the first 5 months of the year, 
525 streetlights have been stolen. The US Department of State reports that the municipalities of 
Montego Bay and Portmore have some of Jamaica's worst rates of violent crime. These locations 
are where streetlight theft was most prevalent. Theft also occurs in the Northern towns of 
Moneague and Claremont in St. Ann, Jamaica's largest parish.153  
In such environments, the lack of proper public lighting due to theft can further exacerbate 
feelings of insecurity and vulnerability, especially for women who may need to navigate these 
areas during nighttime hours. Moreover, women are often primary caregivers and responsible

As most of the country has already been converted to LED lighting, additional challenges can

to constantly invest in replacement, especially in high-crime areas as mentioned before. It may 
not be deemed feasible to fix streetlights, leaving the areas that need lighting the most, without. 
The safety of JPS employees is also a concern as installing and fixing streetlights in high crime 
areas is another potential constraint as employees become hesitant to enter areas for fear of 
persons stealing the lights from their possession and for their own personal safety.  
Solar Water Heating  
In 2009, it was estimated that more than 5,000 solar water heaters (SWH) were installed in 
Jamaica. As of 2020, Jamaica’s use of renewables accounted for approximately 11 per cent of 
its energy: 6.5% wind, 3.5% hydropower, and 1% solar power.154    
The SWH market in Jamaica has the potential to be expanded due to the high levels of insolation 
and urbanism. The country receives an average daily peak of 4.6 hours of sunlight for solar 
panel absorption.155 The combination of good climatic conditions and Jamaica’s readiness and 
urgency to diversify its energy sector will create favorable demand for SWH. However, there are 
specific barriers to SWH adoption which include limited access to capital, incomplete information 
and data, lack of policies governing the injection and transitions of renewable energy resources 
into the power grid and economic regulatory distortion.156 Each of these factors pose challenges 
to promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment efforts due to the limited access to 
capital available for women, a lack of available sex disaggregated data to inform decision-
making and the limitations on gender mainstreaming in policy across the energy sector more 
generally.   
SWH systems in the Caribbean typically have short payback periods and a high net present 
value (NPV) which means over time, not only will systems pay for themselves but also generate 
profits or in this case immense savings.157 They can also be extended depending on incentives, 
local electricity rates, and system sizing.158 Prices vary based on sizes offered, materials used 
and other factors, but they generally begin at USD1,000 not including installation.159 This cost 
can deter home and business owners from investing. These issues of costing can pose a 
significant barrier for women-headed households who, in general, de facto face income 
constraints. It is estimated that 47% of children live in single-parent homes with their biological 
mothers.160 To address some of the issues around this cost prohibitive barrier to access, financial 
institutions have begun offering segmented renewable energy financing. Although the direct 
purpose of environmentally conscious financial initiatives are typically intended to support 
energy efficiencies more generally, conservation and alternatives, they can be utilised for the 
installation of SWH. The Development Bank of Jamaica, Exim Bank, National Housing Trust and

159 These cost can range from USD 2 to 4 per Watt with most homes having up to 5kW systems.  
160 “Shifting the narrative of the single parent” retrieved from https://www.jamaicaobserver.com/all-woman/shifting-the-narrative-of-

=== PAGE 200 ===
the National People’s Cooperative Bank of Jamaica Limited all offer financial services 
specifically mentioning SWH.161 
Heated water may be considered a luxury for some, but it is a part of many persons’ everyday 
life. While the water is mainly used by everyone in the household for showering, women tend to 
use warm water more for themselves as well as for their children and household chores. As the 
current levels of water heating currently account for up to 30 per cent of a domestic light bill in 
Jamaica162, more sustainable methods of water heating should be considered.  
SWH can provide financial benefits along with environmental sustainability. It lowers the carbon 
footprint and is expected to reduce electricity bills since the fuel is free sunlight.163 Due to low 
operating and maintenance costs, solar water heaters over the long-term cover the huge initial 
fixed cost.164 When all costs for purchase, installation, maintenance and operation are taken into 
account after the payback period. the SWH systems are expected to provide hot water for free 
with bare maintenance costs as sunlight is free.165 Thereafter, electricity bills can be reduced by 
30%, making it a cost-effective option for low-income homes including single-mother homes, 
persons with disabilities and retired persons.166  
Rooftop Solar Photovoltaic 
The transition to a greener and more sustainable future can be facilitated by creating a sector 
for clean energy that is gender inclusive and gives women in rural communities more influence 
as both consumers and producers of clean energy. Targeted policy initiatives concentrating on 
improving loan access, skill development, education, and broader systemic gender issues like 
property and equal access laws, land rights, and political engagement are necessary to support 
women's success in the sector.167 
Women may play a crucial role in the transition to renewable energy by influencing patterns of 
energy use. They directly gain from using clean energy as consumers. With access to clean 
energy technologies, women's exposure to indoor air pollution can be eliminated.168 Additionally, 
the ability to use time-saving appliances (like lighting, cookstoves, fans, and mobile phone 
chargers) are made possible due to increased connection to electricity and this in turns leads to 
a reduction in women’s time poverty.169 This involves running clean energy businesses that use 
energy directly (such as water pumps for irrigation and grain mills), as well as marketing these 
products and services (for example solar panels, clean cookstoves, solar lamps, etc.).170 
Clean energy such as rooftop solar can reduce the load on the electricity grid, increasing the 
rate of access to many.  Jamaica’s urban community enjoys generally full access to electric 
services, whilst those within the rural community have experienced increased access to 
electricity with 96.01% coverage in 2016 up from 94% in 2015. Many positive ripple effects on

=== PAGE 201 ===
women's lives and socioeconomic standing emerge from having access to reliable energy. This 
includes lowering the health hazards associated with some of the present energy practices, 
cutting back on the time and effort required for domestic activities, improving local job prospects, 
raising the standard of living, and boosting productivity. There is a compelling argument for 
empowering women by integrating and employing them as business owners, workers, 
innovators, designers, and even decision-makers at the highest levels of the energy system's 
value chain.171 This would provide women with the opportunity to realise their entrepreneurial 
potential and act as change agents on the front lines.172 
Low-income consumers, generally comprised of female- headed households within Jamaica, 
can use solar energy technology as one of several strategies to lessen their energy costs, but 
up to now, they have not adopted solar at the same rate as other income groups. Since they pay 
a larger percentage of their income toward utilities, low-income consumers are 
disproportionately more vulnerable to the effects of shifting electricity prices.173 The 2017 
Jamaica Survey of Living Conditions provided valuable data indicating that poverty 
disproportionately affects female-headed households. The survey revealed that 14.8% of such 
households experienced poverty. Furthermore, a significant majority of the poorest female-
headed households, approximately 54.8%, were single-parent households.174 These findings 
highlight the vulnerability of these households and individuals to various socio-economic 
challenges, including labour market fluctuations, energy insecurity, and affordability issues.175 
Moreover, they face additional difficulties when confronted with energy rate increases or 
temporary hikes. These circumstances further accentuate the economic pressures faced by 
female-headed households and underscore the need for targeted support and measures to 
address their specific energy-related concerns. 
Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration  
Energy-related behaviours and cooling energy consumption are influenced by gender, noting 
variations in consumption among different genders.176  
A general gender comparison study conducted by researchers in China noted that, particularly 
for women, using the air conditioner frequently is a regular practice that affects cooling energy 
use. However, thermal preferences have more noticeable influence on cooling energy 
consumption for males than females.177 These differences exhibit themselves in common  habits 
such as daily average hours of air conditioning use, the use of electric fans in place of air 
conditioners, and the occupancy ratio in room and these habits have an impact on cooling energy 
consumption for both men and women. Women are more likely to provide unpaid care for young 
children, elderly individuals, and those with disabilities, who spend more time indoors due to 
physical and mental conditions and would therefore require more cooling in poorly ventilated 
homes.178 With this situation, they need to use more energy for cooling. In comparison, men are 
shown to be more likely to labour outdoors or in hot locations, making them more sensitive to

=== PAGE 202 ===
the effects of climate change from an outdoor labour perspective which can also have similar 
implication towards health and livelihood management.179 
A refrigeration and cooling case study conducted in 2020 in Kenya found that the use of cooling 
technology was able to boost productivity and give women more options to earn money and 
more freedom in general. While this study was not specifically directed to the Jamaican context, 
refrigerators can provide women more power by eliminating tedious tasks and providing added 
time savings. The capacity to preserve food can reduce the time needed to shop and prepare 
meals and can save food costs by reducing the need to get rid of food that has spoiled.  
As another method to provide cooling for labour and in the home, fans are a relatively low-cost 
appliance, which can help increase productivity and help those at most risk adapt to climate 
change.180 A study conducted to look at the insights of electricity poverty in urban Jamaica found 
that electric fans were the most common electrical appliance used (at a rate of 94%); followed 
by television (90.8 per cent); and refrigerators (89.2%) in the communities of Kingston and St. 
Andrew, St James, Clarendon, and St Catherine.181 Electrical appliances that consume a large 
amount of electricity such as air conditioners and electric stoves were in use for only 7.8% and 
2.5% of the households, respectively.  
At present, there are currently no approved policies in place to implement and spur the use of 
efficient cooling methods and refrigeration in Jamaica. However, as of 2021, as part of the 
regional Caribbean Cooling Initiative (C-COOL), Jamaica’s National Cooling Strategy (JNCS) is 
currently entering its final stage toward approval, and its subsequent implementation. The 
strategy’s recommendations were refined and were undertaken by various inter-agency working 
groups before submission to the Cabinet. Once approved, would be the second such strategy 
of its kind adopted in the region.182
