demand for energy was projected to decline by 6 million barrels of oil equivalent in 2020.184 This 
downward trend is the consequence of the implementation of an effective efficiency 
improvement and conversation program as seen in Figure 3-20 and Figure 3-21.185

3.3.4 
Barriers 
Some of the specific challenges women face in terms of access to affordable energy, and more 
specifically with their roles as typically the primary care providers in the home, include  having 
access to energy for maintaining domestic work, access to water and energy options that are 
both safe and affordable, and help ease the burden women already face by taking on more hours 
of labour, through their domestic duties and those outside the home.  Various barriers also affect 
uptake and use of technologies.  
Institutional  
A weak enabling policy environment for SWH also poses a barrier to implementation. While 
Jamaica has multiple policies on renewable energy, there seems to be only one with stipulations 
specifically for SWH. Jamaica’s National Energy Policy 2009-2030 provides incentives for the 
installation of SWH and solar lights in the public and private sectors and communities are 
included under Goal 1: Jamaicans use energy wisely and aggressively pursue opportunities for 
conservation and efficiency.188 The incentives, include concessional loans and import duties on 
EE and RE, and the exemption of lithium-ion batteries from the General Consumption Tax. 
Lack of public awareness also limits the usage of SWH systems. There are currently no specific 
outreach campaigns for SWH in Jamaica. There have been efforts to increase awareness of the 
merits of renewable energy more generally, however, in order for women to have access to this 
renewable source of energy, financial incentives for system installations, as well as low-interest 
rates loan programs, should be implemented. 189   
Another barrier that arises in the administrative process involved when applying for the 
installation of new lights or the replacement of lights. The process includes many steps and can 
take a long time to implement, posing an obstacle to persons applying.190 This process makes it 
more difficult to have consistent street lighting. The lack of street lighting increases the risk of 
domestic violence and other forms of gender-based violence to occur during times where 
 
186 Ibid.  
187 “Energy Profile – Jamaica” retrieved from https://www.irena.org/-
/media/Files/IRENA/Agency/Statistics/Statistical_Profiles/Central%20America%20and%20the%20Caribbean/Jamaica_Central%20

sunlight is absent. With a high rate of violence within the country, the lack of this infrastructure 
increases the risk and probability of these events occurring and further burden the already 
burdened police and security services  in the country. 
Social 
Without increasing the provision of support through the addition of reliable and sustainable 
services and infrastructure, it is more difficult for women in low-income households to improve 
their positions and livelihoods, their ability to earn decent livelihoods, and to improve their socio-
economic situation and outlook. Without target actions in these areas, the imbalance women 
face in their economic status from abilities to engage in income- generating activities and benefit 
from time time-savings, may consequently lead to further negative impacts across all areas 
mentioned including health, housing, and education for both women and the families they 
provide for.  
As primary consumers and users of domestic energy, for CDRs, e.g. washing, household 
cleaning and personal care, cooking etc., women need to be involved in energy efficiency and 
renewable energy projects from their direct and indirect impacts on levels and usage of 
household energy consumption. Women may also inform their children's future energy 
consumption and conservation practices through their own examples and sustainable 
behaviours in the home. They may also engage children in discussions around energy usage, 
incorporate energy-conscious practices into daily life, or utilise educational resources and 
involve children in energy-saving initiatives.191These means of engaging with their environment 
impacts the way future generations will learn to engage in efficient and sustainable energy use 
and practices in a household. 
There are other barriers faced in implementing rooftop solar, in particular. Due to the lower 
discretionary income in low- to medium-income communities, hefty upfront expenses for rooftop 
solar can be a significant deterrent. Even when upfront costs are decreased, low-income 
persons do not always benefit fairly from such offers. Single-family homeowners also frequently 
encounter housing-related obstacles to implementing rooftop PV, such as unsuitable rooftops, 
and shoddy or outdated electrical wiring. The best roofs for solar have a solid foundation, no 
leaks, and do not require much maintenance when first installed.192 Families with lower incomes 
are more likely to reside in older homes than those with higher incomes, necessitating more 
expensive home renovations to accommodate on-site solar arrays.193 
Renting also can present barriers to accessing solar. In 2008, almost 63% of Jamaicans lived in 
their own homes. Regarding other common household tenure types, the report says 19.1% of 
Jamaicans occupied rented or leased property.194 A survey found that investors consider 
affordability issues to be just as relevant as renters' unwillingness to pay higher rents for 
buildings with solar PV as investment roadblocks. The focus of apartment or townhouse owners 
is one of the structural barriers.195  Moreover, adding to this barrier is the unknown element of 
who bears responsibility for applicable, and any damages that installation or usage may cause 
to the property.

capacity that can be used to address the noticeable lack of gender mainstreaming capacity held

=== PAGE 208 ===
4 
Multi-Country Level Analysis  
 
4.1 
Comparative Policy Environment Analysis  
Looking across the three countries, based on the analysis executed, it can be determined that 
Barbados is presently lagging behind the other countries in terms of gender- responsive policies. 
From all the Barbadian policies reviewed, only the Barbadian NDC was found to be gender- 
responsive which may be due to how recently it was developed and the guidelines around NDC 
development and submission. Belize, and Jamaica (following closely behind) are making greater 
strides in incorporating gender mainstreaming into their gender policies to ensure the vulnerable 
are sufficiently covered within their future plans and activities. 
It is important to note that Barbados’s energy policy released in 2019 failed to mention gender, 
women or vulnerable groups at all putting the policy on par with Belize’s and Jamaica’s much 
older energy policies 2011 and 2009, respectively. This has implications for Barbados as the 
policy is meant to drive country’s energy direction until 2030.  
The gender policies reviewed for Belize and Jamaica were all transformative, indicating that 
there exists a good framework for gender-responsive action to occur within these countries.  Two 
out of the 4 NDCs were gender-responsive which would be due to the acknowledgement of the 
differentiated impacts of climate change felt by different groups but as well as the need to outline 
actions that would mitigate and address these issues to ensure the needs of the populations are 
met.  
A major focus of the desk-based research was delving into the policy environment for renewable 
energy and gender for each country to understand the enabling environment that exists within 
each . With this focus and the nature of the project in mind, the policy analysis sought to look at 
the countries’ NDCs (outlines the countries’ actions to combatting climate change which has 
implications for energy use and social impacts on persons), energy policies, gender policies 
(including policies related to the protection or support of vulnerable groups), utilities Acts and 
other development policies that would guide the countries directions.  
Twenty-six policies were reviewed overall and while 12 were gender-responsive or 
transformative, in general, the policy environment was found to be gender-aware at best (See 
Figure 4-1). Specifically looking at the energy policies of the countries which would have major 
influence on the execution of this project, only the Guyanese draft policy was found to be gender- 
responsive.  
Key chapter takeaway  
Though progress has been made across the region on gender equality, women’s empowerment 
and social inclusion generally and in energy access and affordability, progress and sustainable 
progress is uneven. While the policy environment shows encouraging signs, still more needs to be 
done on financial and social barriers as well as instruments designed to improve an even promotion 
of a range of technologies.

Neutral 
 
Consultations were started in 2022 for a draft gender equality strategy for the CARICOM region 
titled ‘Stepping it up: a strategy to achieve gender equality in the Caribbean Community. 198 The 
document comprises a series of strategic actions to achieve gender equality and women’s 
empowerment in the context of the SDGs. The strategy reflects the work of the CARICOM 
Gender Equality Policy which aims to strengthen CARICOM secretariat capacity to promote 
gender equality and women’s empowerment in partnership with member states and international 
and regional partners toward improving social and cultural prosperity while improving efforts to 
mainstream gender.  The CARICOM Gender Equality Strategy is driven by six guiding principles 
and commitments as shown in Figure 4-3.  
Figure 4-3: CARICOM Gender Equality Strategy Guiding Principles and Commmitments 
 
This work centred on a human rights approach, which recognises every person’s right to freedom 
from discrimination and violence while operating in a just and equal society. It also upholds 
respect for diversity and social inclusion, including those most vulnerable: PWDs, elderly, youth, 
and people living with illness. The strategy also prioritises governance which further recognises 
the rights of women and men in terms of equal access and participation in decision-making in 
order to create effective governance that recognises the unique needs, barriers, and 
opportunities for both women and men. The harmonisation principle references the strategy’s 
 
198 “Stepping it up: A Strategy to achieve gender equality in the Caribbean Community” retrieved from 
https://www.cepal.org/sites/default/files/events/files/stepping_it_up_draft_cges_synthesised.pdf.

=== PAGE 211 ===
position within guiding international frameworks including CEDAW and the SDGs, specifically 
SDG 5. These build on 2003 efforts to develop a ‘Plan of Action to 2005: Framework for 
Mainstreaming Gender in Key CARICOM Programmes. This Plan was developed to provide 
more support for mainstreaming gender in CARICOM’s work, including research and policy 
implementation and design. 
The strategy firmly recognises the systemic nature of inequality, as based within social, political 
and economic institutions while sustained through the norms, values and behaviours present in 
society. It also notes the goal toward reaching substantive equality through equal access and 
equal 
benefits 
which 
are 
reached 
through 
sector 
coordination 
in 
order 
to 
influence/inform/support? substantive change. The strategy also calls for greater partnership 
between men and women as a shared responsibility toward gender equality through 
transforming traditional gender roles, in addition to attitudes and behaviours. Finally, the strategy 
calls for a systemic and cross-cutting approach to achieve the desired outcomes, including 
allocation of resources, both human capital and financial resources to encourage capacity 
toward mainstreaming gender in the Caribbean community.   
Based on these guiding principles, the strategy outlines six key areas for action: equality and 
social inclusion, freedom from violence, economic empowerment, good governance, access to 
health services, and inclusive quality education – at least three of which are directly relevant to 
a just energy transition and the others enabling a clean and sustainable economy. While there 
is no specific reference within the policy to access to affordable and renewable energy with 
reference to women or other vulnerable groups, within the context of economic empowerment, 
the policy notes the need for equitable access to productive resources - where renewable energy 
and energy resources could potentially fit.  
The other overarching policy reviewed from a regional perspective is the CARICOM Energy 
Policy.199 The Energy Policy was approved in 2013 at the forty-first special meeting on energy, 
in Trinidad and Tobago. The purpose of the policy is to support the “fundamental transformation 
of the energy sectors of the Member States of the Community through the provision of secure 
and sustainable supplies of energy in a manner which minimises energy waste in all sectors”.  
The Policy also supports ensuring that all CARICOM citizens can access ‘modern’, clean and 
reliable energy resources at prices that are both stable and affordable while ensuring the growth 
and facilitation of regional industries, competitive in the global market with the intention toward 
creating sustainable development for the Caribbean region.  Within the policy’s 15 objectives for 
expanding the energy sector, it recognises the need for having improved access to affordable 
energy for the region’s poor and vulnerable populations. It also notes, as important for this 
project, the need to create strategies for maintaining sufficient energy supplies in the event of 
disasters and improving R and D in terms of clean and renewable energy sources. The policy, 
however, in terms of access to affordable and reliable energy, makes no specific reference to 
gender or women.   
Two policies from CDB were also assessed as they pertained to gender, energy, youth and 
indigenous peoples within the region i.e. Energy Sector Policy and Strategy 2022200 and Gender 
Equality Policy and Operational Strategy.201 These policies were analysed as they outline CDB’s 
operating principles and future plans for working and supporting these areas as well as guiding 
how projects funded/investments supported by the CDB should operate.  
From the analysis done on these policies, it can be determined that CDB’s own enabling 
environment is geared towards fully supporting the implementation of this project (see Figure

within the gender equality policy should be adopted and applied to the project’s operations and 
development as it would allow for gender equality to be effectively incorporated within the project.

4.2 
Cross-cutting Stakeholder Findings 
Despite being from different countries and operating in different contexts, stakeholders shared 
similar insights and concerns with regard to the issues discussed during direct engagement via 
key informant interviews and group interviews as well as focus group discussions. Below are 
some key findings that emerged and are applicable across all countries.  
4.2.1 
Finance 
 
A major hurdle to accessing loans particularly for women is time. Banking transactions 
typically require a person to allocate a fair of amount of time and effort to engage these 
services and women especially those from female-headed households are usually 
constrained for time due to their domestic duties and responsibilities. 
 
Location plays a role in hindering access to financial tools, particularly for many who reside 
in rural locations (especially those living in indigenous communities). The nearest financial 
institution is usually very far away from where they reside, and the distance acts a deterrent. 
This particular barrier can also involve time - the time it takes to get to and from the 
bank/financial institution and the cost to get there and back.  
 
Banking processes can be tedious and the requirements to engage in their services such as 
proof of address, form of national identification, lack of collateral often eliminate or 
discourage persons from seeking these options.  
 
Persons working in the informal sector and low-income persons utilise informal saving 
mechanisms such as syndicate, partner202, boxhand, etc. to pool financial resources that 
allow persons to conduct large transactions when they are unable to meet requirements 
imposed by financial institutions203. In some cases, these pooled resources may be used to 
meet downpayments requested by financial institutions.   
 
202 “Jamaica’s ROSCA: The Partner System” retrieved from https://www.gdrc.org/icm/partner-sys.html   
203 “Building Economic Solidarity: Caribbean ROSCAS in Jamaica, Guyana, and Haiti” retrieved from 
https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1057/978-1-137-60047-9_5

=== PAGE 213 ===
4.2.2 
Gender Mainstreaming Capacity  
 
Efforts are being made by energy ministries to enhance their gender mainstreaming 
capacities. The Ministry of Energy and Business of Barbados noted that work on a gender 
equality strategy and action plan for the electricity sector is being advanced that could have 
bearing on this project (however, access to this document has not been realised).  
 
Many NGOs, particularly the disability-focused NGOs noted that they are rarely consulted 
for any project development or implementation, and it could be seen in the results of the 
project as their vulnerable groups do not benefit fully from these works.  
 
Gender mainstreaming capacity is low among government stakeholders, noting many do not 
possess gender policies and rarely engage with the gender bureaux of their countries. 
However, it must be stated that with increased engagement with international funds which 
require gender to be involved in any work they fund, capacity is slowly being developed and 
more awareness on gender is being raised. 
4.2.3 
Employment in the Energy Sector  
 
The energy sector is traditionally not one in which women participated significantly in the 
past but in more recent years, this has been changing with women involved across several 
areas. Numbers remain relatively low as men still dominate this field. Feedback received via 
stakeholder interviews (both KIIs and FGDs), suggest that a few technical roles in the Energy 
Ministry are now occupied by women.  
4.2.4 
Engagement with Vulnerable Groups  
 
NGOs’, in their responses, indicate that they are rarely consulted by project actors on energy 
projects. 
 
Many government stakeholders noted that there are no explicit policies that guide 
stakeholder engagement to ensure its gender-responsiveness or inclusiveness.   
4.2.5 
Indigenous Persons (Belize)   
 
Many of the indigenous people are found in far/remote or rural areas of the countries and 
exhibit the highest rates of poverty in the country and share similar vulnerabilities and 
challenges with access. 
 
Respect must be paid to governing systems employed within the different communities and 
knowledge of these systems is critical to successfully navigating engagements with local 
indigenous populations.  
 
Recognising and acknowledging that a “one size fits all” approach will not work when 
engaging indigenous communities as they hold varying traditions and practices.   
Exploring the technologies with the stakeholders, many shared similar perspectives and 
information on uses, benefits and demand for the target technologies (see Figure 4-5). The 
inputs gained from stakeholders were fairly aligned with what was discovered through desk-
based research but also provided further country context based on lived experiences.

From the stakeholder feedback (reflected also in the Table), there is  uniformity in the uses and 
benefits of the technologies across the three countries. While there is demand for each 
technology, Barbados sees the most demand for SWH due to the extensive framework 
supporting the technology in the country  with this framework being supported by the provision 
of incentives such as income tax rebates. Noted across the board by stakeholders is that the 
cost of the technologies being the main prohibitive factor to persons purchasing these items.  
4.3 
Barriers Across the Region  
From the analysis of the three  countries, a pattern of barriers has emerged. Despite differences 
in location, culture, economies, etc. the people particularly vulnerable groups all face similar 
barriers when it comes to accessing renewable energy technologies. While in Barbados, we see 
the most support for the adoption of renewable energy technologies by the population, 
vulnerable groups still encounter comparative issues faced by persons in countries with less of 
a supporting framework.  
There is relatively poor access to and availability of sex and age-disaggregated data across the 
three  countries and the Caribbean region in general, particularly on energy use, demand, needs 
and uptake. This results in challenges in differentiating energy usage among men, women, 
gender-diverse persons, PWDs, indigenous persons, children and the elderly. Current available 
energy data usually is household rather than individual specific and does not engender 
intersectional analysis. Also available to some extent is data/information on implications for the 
design and implementation of energy services, for education campaigns, and about financial 
measures. This means that existing and future actions utilising current data are less effective as 
the approaches would tend to be more generic. A generic approach is not desirable for many 
reasons including the likelihood that investments will not address the practical or the strategic 
gender needs that do exist amongst various groups and that it would make it difficult to also 
differentiate impacts across groups of beneficiaries.

=== PAGE 215 ===
Economic barriers are heavily present in all three countries as strict requirements for loans 
imposed by banks is the norm across the region and women are challenged to enhance their 
access and control of such resources because of reduced economic ability due to traditional 
divisions of labour or burdens imposed by unpaid care work. In Belize, indigenous people exhibit 
the highest rates of poverty while also being located in the most rural areas of their respective 
countries and with the greatest collective need for modern and accessible energy solutions. Not 
only do they have to navigate the loan requirements, but they must also contend with the 
challenges that time and distance imposes on them.  
Socially, we can see in the three countries that work in the energy sector is skewed more towards 
men than women. Even the small percentage of women working in the sector mostly take on 
roles traditionally designated to women such as clerical and administrative work with few women 
taking on technical roles in the field. This gendered division of employment is heavily influenced 
by patriarchal norms204 and patriarchal norms’ influence is not only restricted to formal work. The 
gendered division of labour can be seen in the allocation of domestic work which is skewed 
disproportionately to women and is a common occurrence in the region. This imbalance of 
domestic labour impairs women’s economic ability through the limited finances they are able to 
attain due to the resulting time-poverty that comes from them performing the majority of 
domestic, primarily unpaid care work. 
 
204 “Gender at Work in the Caribbean” retrieved from https://www.ilo.org/wcmsp5/groups/public/---americas/---ro-lima/---sro-
port_of_spain/documents/publication/wcms_651944.pdf

=== PAGE 216 ===
5 
Key Conclusions 
 
Based on the engagement with stakeholders and the research, there is both appetite and 
increasing need for renewable energy technologies, particularly affordable, easily maintained 
and those that generate multiple co-benefits. From available data and limited consumer analysis, 
demand is primarily rooted in potential cost and income savings and the additional potential for 
income- generation that may arise. As a result, the climate change adaptations benefits derived 
from these technologies are often viewed as additional benefits and does not serve the main 
motivation for driving the interest/desire of households and businesses for technologies. For 
government actors, a combination of these factors appears relevant, including the combined 
mitigation-adaptation co-benefit.  
Despite this demand, many persons across the three focus countries are restricted from 
investing in these technologies primarily due to financial barriers. Uptake of these technologies 
for low-income persons is often restrained by limited financial resources or inability to access 
additional finance from financial institutions due to various factors such as limited collateral, time 
conflicts, and competing priorities. This project’s ability to market and distribute these 
technologies in a way that minimises strain on low-income persons will be key. Doing so in ways 
that respond to social, cultural and livelihoods needs is crucial to meet multiple SDGs.  
Consideration must be given to involving vulnerable groups in the further development and 
implementation of this project. The goal of this project supporting a Just Transition cannot be 
accomplished without involving those typically left behind and who are generally 
underrepresented in the development and expansion of the sector and in reaping the benefits 
from significant public sector and public finance investment. Moreover, the project should be 
actively gender-responsive paying close attention to intersectional gender needs as well as 
intersecting energy demand.   
For Belize with large rural populations and significant indigenous sub-populations, customer 
outreach is crucial. Coupled with the financial issues mentioned, these communities also deal 
with the issues related to their location meaning their isolation and distance from the rest of the 
country and from technology hubs. Likewise, the needs of PWDs require greater attention and 
a specialised response.  
Noting the lack of gender mainstreaming capacity amongst the government stakeholders who 
will be involved in the project and critically the lack of engagement with most of the executing 
entities, there are clear gaps that will need to be bridged. Efforts to boost gender awareness and 
sensitivity in ways that shape implementation, sustainability and scaling-up will be critical in the 
pre-implementation period of the project, as well as the first year of implementation.   
Key chapter takeaway  
While the demand and need for an IUS project is clearly present in the target countries and there 
exist enabling environments of varying degrees to support the project. At present, given the 
capacities of key stakeholders and state of policies, the ability of this project to successfully support 
and promote a Just Transition is highly questionable and will need several high level as well as 
practical interventions to ensure it is able to fulfill this goal. Key opportunities to enable the 
participation of low-income and single-headed households could be tapped into as well, leaving 
fewer persons behind in the energy transition in the short and medium-term. Gender and social 
considerations are relevant in all countries.

=== PAGE 217 ===
6 
Recommendations 
 
Emerging from the gender assessment, some of the recommendations to be explored in this 
section will be based on a 7-point gender needs framework. The recommendations touch on 6 of 
the 7: leadership and decision-making, gender-based rights and participation, governance, 
gender-based education and assets, access to and control over resources, and livelihoods. 
Recommendations are divided into two categories; 1) general recommendations that speak to 
actions that will boost programme actors’ capacities and 2) stakeholder engagement 
recommendations that seek to guide programme actors’ interactions with vulnerable groups and 
overall communication with beneficiaries and enhance the level of ownership and participation at 
the local, community, group, household, and individual level.   
6.1.1 
General recommendations  
These recommendations seek to empower programme actors and address gender 
mainstreaming gaps identified in the assessment. The general recommendations are as follows: 
1. Partnership guidelines aimed to serve as a framework to be used by programme actors 
(government stakeholders) to guide how they interact and form working relationships with 
NGOs.  
2. Partnership building exercises aimed at building strong working relationships between 
government agencies and NGOs as well as between government agencies to eliminate 
potential siloing of activities.  
3. Fostering collaboration between the three countries to enhance knowledge and 
experience sharing.  
4. Execution of an in-depth gender capacity assessment of key government stakeholders 
and utilities involved in the programme. The development of gender sensitisation and 
gender capacity-building training will be based on the results of the gender capacity 
assessment. 
5. More in-depth analysis of the financial tools being offered by the banks, credit unions, etc. 
to ensure the financial tools being offered by the utilities as part of this programme are 
competitive.  
Key chapter takeaway  
Ensuring the project can fulfil its goal of supporting a just transition will require the execution of 
some key general and stakeholder-related capacity building activities, aimed at enhancing and 
addressing existing capabilities and gaps that were found in this gender assessment. Critical gender 
actions have been identified in 6 of 7 gender needs and some actions will be catalytic while others 
are more long-term in also setting a key good practice for other multi-country energy programmes. 
Utilities, less familiar with the requirements of international public finance, will experience a 
somewhat sharp learning curve but one that will boost the scope for multiple and expanding public-
private sector partnership.

=== PAGE 218 ===
6. A review of the programme’s logic framework and the design of gender targets of the prog 
including sex disaggregated indicators.  
7. Development of training programmes that could help persons to seek employment in the 
energy sector while supporting the distribution and installation of technologies.  
8. The IUS programme should provide support designed to address gender inequalities such 
as - gender sensitive repayment terms (no ID required; longer periods; lower collateral 
requirements; etc); special and appropriate technology offers for women in MSME 
business; adjustable rates for female headed households with children, targeted capacity 
building outreach to women-led businesses and other vulnerable groups to support their 
ability to develop sub-projects.  
9. Consideration will need to be given to removing or mitigating inequity imbalances in terms 
of access to technologies including exploring more supportive pay back terms of loans, 
and or special process on AC/SWH for women customers. 
10. Assess lessons learned from existing and past loan and grant programmes that target or 
engage vulnerable groups so that these can inform the GCF programme design and 
implementation and also shape financing options and how these can be accessed 
differently by such groups. 
6.1.2 
Stakeholder engagement recommendations 
Given the outward-facing nature of this programme and the many potential actors that could be 
involved throughout the its activities, there is a need to create a framework for gender-responsive 
engagement with the beneficiaries. The stakeholder engagement recommendations are as 
follows:  
1. Development of a code of conduct for all programme actors to follow when engaging the 
public and particularly direct beneficiaries.  
2. Development of GBV checklist to reduce the potential of GBV occurring within the project 
due to the interactions between programme actors and the public and between different 
publics as well as at the household level.  
3. Development a communication strategy that aims to provide the public with timely updates 
on the programme and ensures that communications including updates and technology 
awareness is crafted to ensure the information is easily understood and accessible to 
those who may be blind, deaf or speak different local languages such as the indigenous 
populations.  
4. Development of a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) for the programme that is readily 
accessible and easy to utilise by the public including multiple methods to access and use.  
5. Development of a gender-responsive stakeholder engagement plan to guide all 
programme engagement including inclusive and gender-sensitive communications. 
6. The hosting of townhall meetings or alternative approaches to engage specific vulnerable 
groups to ensure their issues and concerns are considered within the programme.  
Figure 6-1 aligns the recommended actions to gender needs and identified barriers mapping out 
in summary key elements to be further expanded on and programmed through the Gender Action 
Plan (GAP). It responds to three main barriers: institutional, social and economic/financial.

=== PAGE 220 ===
7 
Gender Action Plan  
 
In the preparation of the proposal for the “Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services 
(IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean (Phase I)” Programme, it is 
essential to ensure the programme meets both the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF’s) and Caribbean 
Development Bank’s (CDB’s) requirements for gender equality. As a result, a gender assessment 
was done and from this assessment, emerged areas of concern and weakness that need to be 
addressed to ensure gender is adequately mainstreamed in this programme so it can support a 
Just Transition.  
Gender mainstreaming is the process by which gender equality can be achieved through the 
integration of a gender perspective into key processes, policies and frameworks that usually adopt 
a gender-neutral stance, and by creating the necessary provisions and relevant supporting 
documents to facilitate the removal of gender inequalities. Within the context of the IUS 
programme, this entails ensuring the services and financing models employed by the programme 
are gender-responsive and provide benefits to marginalised groups as it pertains to the 
requirements of the GCF and CDB.   
For the GCF, its updated 2020 Gender Policy (GP) seeks to ensure the institution and its network 
of partners adopt a comprehensive gender approach to support climate change interventions and 
innovations, advance gender equality and minimise social, gender and climate related risks 
through the promotion of climate investments and support the closing of the gender gap through 
mainstreaming gender equality in climate investments.  
Applying the GCF GP to the IUS Programme would involve:  
 
Addressing and reducing gender inequality resulting in sustainable livelihoods 
opportunities, improved health and wellbeing, and increased resilience to climate-induced 
shocks and risks for men and women. 
 
Creation of an enabling environment among programme stakeholders founded on existing 
policy commitments wherein social and environmental standards and grievance redress 
mechanisms are supported and sustained in order to achieve gender equality. 
Key chapter takeaway  
Ensuring the programme can full its goal of supporting a just transition will require the execution of 
some key general and stakeholder-related capacity building activities, aimed at enhancing and 
addressing existing capabilities and gaps that were found in the gender assessment. Critical gender 
actions have been identified in 6 of 7 gender needs and some actions will be catalytic while others 
are more long-term in also setting a key good practice for other multi-country energy programmes. 
Utilities, less familiar with the requirements of international public finance, will experience a 
somewhat sharp learning curve but one that will boost the scope for multiple and expanding public-
private sector partnership.

=== PAGE 221 ===
 
Support the comprehensive and systematic collection of sex-disaggregated data and 
execute qualitative and quantitative gender analysis to monitor, report and evaluate 
project impact.  
GCF’s 2018 Indigenous Peoples’ Policy seeks to guide the institution’s engagement with IP in 
climate change policies and action while acknowledging IP as a distinct stakeholder group that 
needs to be involved. The policy is guided by the following principles: 
 
Develop and implement free, prior and informed consent. 
 
Respect and enhance the rights of IP to their lands, territories and resources. 
 
Recognize key international human rights and principles. 
 
Respect the right of IP under voluntary isolation. 
 
Respect and recognize traditional knowledge and livelihood systems. 
 
Enhance the capacity for IP issues within GCF.  
 
Facilitate access to GCF resources for IP.  
 
Respecting the system of self-governance.  
 
In the context of the IUS Programme and its GAP, the IP policy would apply to the following 
areas:  
 
Programme actors properly seek free, prior and informed consent when engaging with 
IP throughout the programme’s lifespan, and 
 
Ensure that adverse impacts are avoided or minimized and compensate for residual 
impacts.  
 
The CDB’s Gender Equality Policy and Operational Strategy (GEPOS: 2019) outlines CDB’s 
commitment to advancing gender equality through recognising the equal rights, responsibilities 
and opportunities of women, men, girls and boys which include the equal power to shape their 
own lives and contribute to society. The policy seeks to achieve gender equality in BMCs based 
on the following action pillars: 
 
Equitable access to infrastructure services for all,  
 
Economic empowerment of all, 
 
Education and training for all, 
 
Eliminating gender-based violence for all, and  
 
Resilience for all.  
 
The guiding principles of the GEPOS that are applicable in the IUS programme include: 
 
Advocacy, dialogue and partnership, 
 
Intersectionality, participatory and inclusive perspectives and approaches, 
 
Institutional strengthening in BMCs, 
 
Enhancing leadership for and increasing compliance with gender equality, and 
 
Results monitoring. 
 
7.1 
IUS Programme Approach to Gender Action Planning   
Noting that the programme will be implemented in three countries with differing contexts to be 
considered, the action plan will outline actions that are generally applicable to all three countries 
and where relevant, actions that are tailored to a specific country will be indicated.

=== PAGE 222 ===
7.1.1 
Implementing Partners 
The activities outlined in the GAP will be headed and supported by various partners. For clarity, 
the stakeholders who will be involved in the implementation of the GAP are outlined below. In 
the GAP, the specific stakeholders will be allocated to each activity, also specifying whether 
they are the lead or support on the activity.  
 
Stakeholders with responsibilities in the GAP:  
 
Regional Executing Entity (CDB Technical Assistance/TA Execution Team) 
 
National Executing Entities (the electric utilities in the BMCs), 
 
Implementing Partners (government agencies such as ministries of energy, utilities 
regulators, gender bureaus), 
 
The Accredited Entity (CDB Accredited Entity Team), 
 
Consultants (hired for specific activities and roles), and 
 
Other partners (includes NGOs that represent marginalised groups). 
 
7.1.2 
Policy Environments  
The gender assessment (drafted and submitted) which informs and accompanies this GAP 
highlights that the policy environment for energy is relatively gender-neutral for most countries. 
Accordingly, a universal approach or broad-brush strategy based on model policy and/ or 
legislation or even approaches to policy reform are not suitable and unlikely to be successful. Any 
such effort needs to be grounded in capacity-building and behaviour change to be viable, 
sustainable and scalable into other projects in the future. Each country has its own unique societal 
circumstances and existing systems that position them differently in how, where and when 
reforms are suitable, viable and likely to be impactful during the life of the programme and this is 
an important pre-requisite in defining starting points for policy reform.  
 
There is also fertile ground upon which to identify and achieve win-win opportunities via the policy 
placeholders set in place in existing policies and frameworks including NDCs which outline 
operational strategies and guidance for gender mainstreaming in order to achieve tangible gender 
impact and a gender-just transition through mitigation investments in RE/EE. 
 
7.2 
Gender Action Plan  
Given this context, the activities in this GAP are divided into (i) general activities that are 
foundational, required for all countries and critical to creating the right conditions for gender- 
responsive action and (ii) activities critical for delivering specific gender- responsive actions per 
outcome/output based on the programme’s Theory of Change. The second set of activities are 
catalysers that build on the foundational elements to dual effect – resulting in a general paradigm 
shift in utility-led energy transition and investments that is more people-centred and deliver 
adaptation co-benefits and expands the scope and innovativeness of DERs in terms of the design, 
scope and application that are tailored also to the common as well as differentiated needs of 
households, public sector service delivery actors as well as businesses.  
 
Table 1 outlines the GAP in detail including indicators and targets, timelines, responsibilities and 
costs in keeping with GCF requirements for the GAP. It aligns with several key findings and 
recommendations outlined in the Gender Assessment.

=== PAGE 223 ===

Impact Statement: increased access to clean, affordable energy and access to energy and cost saving technologies for women, 
low-income persons, persons with disabilities, indigenous people and the LGBTQIA community that meets their needs and aids in 
building their climate resilience while improving the quality of their lives and livelihoods.  
General Recommendations

(CoC) for engagement with

=== PAGE 232 ===
Noting the GCF and CDB’s gender requirements stated earlier, it is critical that much of the 
gender mainstreaming work needed for this project (totalling USD 1,5488,000), occurs as early 
as possible during the project lifespan (Table 1). This is needed to ensure that the necessary 
capacity- building, measures, and systems are in place to support this project’s ability to meet 
these gender requirements while also meeting its own objectives of facilitating access to RE/EE 
technologies and supporting a Just Transition in the target countries. Understanding this, the 
Inception Phase and Year 1 of the project will be when most activities occur. The Inception phase 
activities accrue a cost of USD 16,000 with activities centred on building capacity and developing 
systems to support gender-responsive stakeholder engagement which will be essential in 
developing later actions.

=== PAGE 233 ===
Appendix A Stakeholder Engagement 
Plan  
Background/Introduction to Stakeholder Engagement Process  
Stakeholder engagement is aimed to address gaps emerging from desk-based research. Noting 
the limited work done in the region on gender and energy (particularly gender and renewable 
energy) and the limited online availability of what has already been done, the main goal is to 
address gaps such as qualitative data on gender mainstreaming capacity and experience 
particularly for renewable energy projects, and barriers and constraints to accessing stable, 
reliable and affordable energy.  
This approach builds on the Baseline Assessment and addresses issues considered in the 
outlines for the Gender Assessment and the Gender Action Plan previously submitted to the 
Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) for consideration and comment. In addition to this, we will 
be seeking insight on barriers to and best practices for women’s participation and economic 
empowerment in a just energy transition as well as the requirements for a socially inclusive 
approach, noting the different marginalised communities and the intersection of different 
vulnerabilities. This is in keeping with the policies of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) policy and 
guidelines as well as the CDB’s environment and social review procedures as well as the Terms 
of Reference (TOR) for this assignment.  
Gendered and Socially Inclusive Stakeholder Needs being considered  
While the project will focus on technology types and supply, a gender and social inclusion 
analysis also need to consider demands, needs and interests even in a limited fashion and what 
will or may drive these including uptake of proposed interventions. Accordingly, areas to be 
explored during these more direct consultations include: 
 
Barriers and Constraints to accessing stable, reliable, affordable energy and lessons learnt 
from the Covid19 pandemic.  
 
Gender Mainstreaming Knowledge, Exposure, Experience and Capacity particularly related 
to renewable energy projects (particularly for implementing actors)  
 
Gaps, Barriers and Constraints and Best practices for women’s participation and economic 
empowerment in a just, energy transition 
 
Requirements for an inclusive approach that considers the specific needs of persons with 
disabilities, the elderly, youth, LGBTQIA+ persons, indigenous people and other 
marginalised groups as well as intersecting vulnerabilities224.  
Given the focus of the project, the baseline findings on gender equality and initial gaps as well 
as the economic context of the three countries – Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica, the following 
marginalized groups need to be considered in our engagements in the context of the four 
technologies – rooftop solar, solar water-heating, LED lighting and cooling: 
 
Women, 
 
Young people, 
 
School-aged children for whom alternative and consistent access to energy was a significant 
issue during the COVID-19 pandemic, 
 
The elderly for whom cooling can be a critical asset given the NCDs prevalent in the region, 
 
Persons with Disability,  
 
224 This refers to persons who meet all or some of these groups at the same time.

=== PAGE 234 ===
 
LGBTQIA+ persons or representative groups, and 
 
Indigenous People. 
These inputs, from them directly where possible and from others in terms of how they currently 
engage with these needs, are critical to the quality of analysis as well as the recommendations 
that may emerge from the assignment to inform the design of interventions, the gender 
mainstreaming focus of the project as well as the Gender Action Plan (GAP). 
Stakeholder Engagement Type 
Stakeholder engagement will take three forms, key informant interviews (KII), group interviews 
(GI) and focus group discussions (FGD). All forms will be guided by structured questions or 
presentations and will primarily occur online except in cases where it would be better suited to 
conduct engagement in the country.  
KIIs are planned primarily for government agencies and utilities. These interviews are intended 
to provide the representatives from these organisations with a safe space to speak about the 
topics from their organisation’s perspective without any outside distractions.  
Group interviews are planned for some governmental organisations and non-governmental 
organisations (NGOs) that work in a closely related field and would benefit from being present 
with each other during the interview or may feel comfortable in a limited group setting (e.g. 
LBGTQIA+ organisations). 
Focused Group Discussions are planned for NGO stakeholders with the idea that discussions 
with these organisations working in a similar/the same field would create an engaging, safe 
space that facilitates discussions and possibly elicit different perspectives when presented with 
a talking point that they may relate to but have a different experience with.  
A summary of the number of planned engagements per country and the type of engagement is 
provided in Figure A-1.

Proposed Consultation Schedule  
Stakeholder engagements are planned from April 3rd to April 21st. The plan takes note of the 
public holidays of Good Friday and Easter Monday occurring on April 7th and 10th respectively 
for all countries. Each country will be allotted specific days to hold consultations, this will allow 
for following up on information with relevant organisations within the same period as necessary.  
To facilitate scheduling Calendly to facilitate appointment setting and to limit back and forth 
regarding availability. This also allows more control over the stakeholder in identify and selecting 
a time based on availability in the schedule. This will be designated by the country days identified 
below.  
Country Days Designation  
Barbados – 3rd -6th  
Belize – 11th – 13th  
Jamaica – 19th to 21st  
A draft of the email to be sent out to stakeholders follows.  
Draft Email to Stakeholders  
 
Dear Sir/Madam:  
The Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) in collaboration with the governments and utilities of 
three Borrowing Member Countries (BMCs) is preparing a funding proposal to the Green Climate 
Fund (GCF) for the “Scaling Up The Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (IUS) To Support 
Energy Sector Transformation In The Caribbean” programme.  This programme arises out of the 
needs and requests for support from BMCs in transitioning their energy sectors to more 
sustainable, renewable energy sources. This first iteration of the programme focuses on 
Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. The programme will support these countries to (1) reduce 
greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) and (2) enhance resilience through the increased uptake of 
distributed renewable energy (RE), energy efficiency (EE) and distributed energy resources 
(DER) among customers of the countries’ electric utilities. The programme also aims to enable 
widespread investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs while promoting equitable access 
to such technologies and enhancing electric utilities to be able to continue applying the IUS 
model after the end of the programme.  
CDB has contracted CPCS (in collaboration with FOKABS Inc and SAEDI Consulting Barbados 
Inc) to support the development of the full funding proposal that meets the requirements of the 
GCF. SAEDI Consulting’s role is to bring gender and social inclusion expertise to the process. 
This involves conducting a gender assessment and developing a gender mainstreaming action 
plan in compliance with GCF guidelines.   
To accurately reflect the target countries’ gender equality and socio-economic context and 
provide recommendations that deliver meaningful, inclusive results for the benefit of the 
countries, particularly for those most vulnerable, SAEDI Consulting will hold stakeholder 
consultations between April 10th to April 21st. Stakeholders targeted include key governmental 
and community-level actors in the energy transformation and social development space.  
The following days are allocated to the three countries for stakeholder engagement (excludes 
weekends): 
Barbados – 3rd -6th

=== PAGE 236 ===
Belize – 11th – 13th  
Jamaica – 19th to 21st  
The stakeholder consultations will explore the following: 
 
Barriers and Constraints to accessing stable, reliable, affordable energy and lessons learnt 
from the Covid19 pandemic.  
 
Gender Mainstreaming Knowledge, Exposure, Experience and Capacity particularly related 
to renewable energy projects (particularly for implementing actors)  
 
Gaps, Barriers and Constraints and Best practices for women’s participation and economic 
empowerment in a just, energy transition 
 
Requirements for an inclusive approach that considers the specific needs of persons with 
disabilities, the elderly, youth, LGBTQIA+ persons, indigenous people and other 
marginalised groups as well as intersecting vulnerabilities225.  
Your voice counts in this process. We look forward to your earliest response and any information 
the organisation or the identified representatives may be able to provide that is relevant to these 
issues.  
We encourage confirmations by April 2nd as we begin planning for these engagements To 
facilitate this process, we encourage you to use our Calendly link to book a suitable time within 
your country’s allocated days. If you require any assistance or have any queries, please contact 
Kwasi Pierre at juniorassociate1@saediconsulting.com.  Thank you in advance for your kind 
consideration.  
Yours Sincerely, 
SAEDI Consulting Barbados Inc

Appendix B Stakeholders Consulted 
Figure B-1 to Figure B-3 present the list of stakeholders engaged for each country with Table 29 
additionally presenting stakeholders of that group that were not engaged. These stakeholders 
that were not engaged were due to time constraints and conflicting priorities that made meeting 
difficult. Stakeholders from the stakeholder country that were engaged on the same day were 
done in group interviews. 28 organisations were met during the period of April to June.

Appendix C Unconsulted Stakeholders  
Figure C-1 presents a list of stakeholders that the consultant attempted to engage as part of the 
stakeholder engagement process to support the gender assessment. Failure to obtain dates and 
times to meet or a lack of response from stakeholders were the reasons for the inability to meet 
with these stakeholders. This list primarily consists of NGOs and a few government 
organisations from Belize (and excludes the utilities, see Figure C-1).
